## Q1

Chemistry · inorganic · Environmental Chemistry

Which of the following is a sink for $\mathrm{CO}$?

- **(1)** Micro-organisms present in the soil
- (2) Oceans
- (3) Plants
- (4) Haemoglobin

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Micro-organisms present in the soil is a sink for $\mathrm{CO}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A natural sink for atmospheric $\mathrm{CO}$ is soil, where micro-organisms metabolise $\mathrm{CO}$; oceans and plants act as sinks for $\mathrm{CO_2}$, and haemoglobin binding $\mathrm{CO}$ is poisoning, not a sink, so the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Air Pollution · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 1](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q001_d83480af_29102026.png?v=d83480afda74)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 1](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q001_ef7d0586_29102026.png?v=ef7d0586f62f)

#### Categories

- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A sink is what removes a gas from the atmosphere, and for carbon monoxide that is the soil micro-organisms that oxidise it; binding to haemoglobin is what CO does inside the body.
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 4  
  Haemoglobin is what every remembered CO fact is attached to, since CO binds it far more tightly than oxygen does, so the question 'where does CO end up' gets answered with the toxicity fact instead of with an atmospheric sink.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated fact of what removes carbon monoxide from the atmosphere, demonstrated by the stem asking for 'a sink for CO' with no data printed and two options naming reservoirs of a different oxide of carbon.

## Q2

Chemistry · inorganic · Classification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties

The element $Z = 114$ has been discovered recently. It will belong to which of the following family group and electronic configuration?

- **(1)** Carbon family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{2}$
- (2) Oxygen family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{4}$
- (3) Nitrogen family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{6}$
- (4) Halogen family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{5}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $Z = 114$ belong to Group 14, carbon family
2. Electronic configuration $= [\mathrm{Rn}]5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The preceding noble gas is radon, $Z = 86$, so $114 - 86 = 28$ electrons lie beyond the $[\mathrm{Rn}]$ core.
2. Fill in order $7s$, $5f$, $6d$, $7p$: $2 + 14 + 10 = 26$, leaving $28 - 26 = 2$ electrons for $7p$.
3. Configuration: $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{2}$.
4. Valence shell $7s^{2}7p^{2}$ (i.e. $ns^{2}np^{2}$) means group $14$, the carbon family, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Electronic Configurations · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 2](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q002_9c7f6001_29102026.png?v=9c7f6001cceb)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 2](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q002_041488c3_29102026.png?v=041488c31ad3)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Filling the seventh period in its order, 7s then 5f then 6d then 7p, puts element 114 two electrons into 7p, which is group 14, the carbon family.
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  Counting out from radon through 5f14, 6d10 and 7s2 reaches 112, so two electrons are left for 7p; dropping a pair somewhere in those inner sets leaves four instead, and 7p4 is the oxygen family.
- **Why:** This question requires the seventh-period filling order applied to an element beyond the printed table, demonstrated by the stem asking 'which of the following family group and electronic configuration' for Z = 114 and each option supplying both halves.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier returned plain_mcq, but the stem asks for two things at once, the family and the configuration, and each option supplies both halves, which have to hold together; the shape recorded is therefore two_part.

## Q3

Chemistry · physical · Thermodynamics

For a given reaction, $\Delta H = 35.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and $\Delta S = 83.6\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$. The reaction is spontaneous at : (Assume that $\Delta H$ and $\Delta S$ do not vary with temperature)

- **(1)** $T > 425\,\mathrm{K}$
- (2) All temperatures
- (3) $T > 298\,\mathrm{K}$
- (4) $T < 425\,\mathrm{K}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\because\ \Delta G = \Delta H - T\Delta S$
2. For a reaction to be spontaneous, $\Delta G = -\mathrm{ve}$
3. i.e., $\Delta H < T\Delta S$
4. $\therefore\ T > \frac{\Delta H}{\Delta S} = \frac{35.5 \times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{J}}{83.6\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}}}$
5. i.e., $T > 425\,\mathrm{K}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Spontaneity requires $\Delta G = \Delta H - T\Delta S < 0$.
2. Since $\Delta H > 0$ and $\Delta S > 0$, this holds when $T > \frac{\Delta H}{\Delta S}$.
3. Convert units: $\Delta H = 35.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}} = 35500\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$.
4. $T = \frac{35500\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}}{83.6\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}} \approx 424.6\,\mathrm{K} \approx 425\,\mathrm{K}$.
5. So the reaction is spontaneous at $T > 425\,\mathrm{K}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Gibbs Energy and Spontaneity · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q003_c3d4277e_29102026.png?v=c3d4277e67ae)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q003_10c7389e_29102026.png?v=10c7389e2bff)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** With $\Delta H$ and $\Delta S$ both positive, $\Delta G = \Delta H - T\Delta S$ turns negative only above the crossover temperature $\Delta H/\Delta S$, which here is about 425 K.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  The crossover comes out right at $35500/83.6 \approx 425$ K and then the inequality is written the wrong way round, as though 425 K were a ceiling on spontaneity rather than a floor.
- **Why:** This question requires the crossover temperature computed from the printed numbers, demonstrated by the stem supplying $\Delta H = 35.5\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and $\Delta S = 83.6\ \mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$ and the options being inequalities in T.

Structures: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene `COc1cccc(Br)c1` ![1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_question_s1_549edc1b_29102026.svg?v=549edc1b8e53)

- (1) 2-methoxyaniline `COc1ccccc1N` ![2-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_option_1_s1_8e3a59a8_29102026.svg?v=8e3a59a88218) — and elimination addition reaction
- (2) 1-bromo-2-methoxybenzene `COc1ccccc1Br` ![1-bromo-2-methoxybenzene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_option_2_s1_9879780e_29102026.svg?v=9879780e68a6) — and cine substitution reaction
- (3) methoxybenzene `COc1ccccc1` ![methoxybenzene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_option_3_s1_c9107e15_29102026.svg?v=c9107e15c534) — and cine substitution reaction
- **(4)** 3-methoxyaniline `COc1cccc(N)c1` ![3-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_option_4_s1_eb8f97fa_29102026.svg?v=eb8f97fa5df6) — and substitution reaction

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →($\ce{NH2-}$) 2-bromo-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide → 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene (Benzyne)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q004_sol_1_339e5db8_29102026.svg?v=339e5db88f28)

*1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →[$\ce{NH2-}$] 2-bromo-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide → 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene (Benzyne)*

![drawn: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →($\ce{NH2-}$) 2-bromo-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide → 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene (Benzyne)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_solution_1_66057edb_29102026.svg?v=66057edbc3fe)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

![printed figure: 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene + $\ce{NH2-}$ (attack a or b) →(X) 2-amino-3-methoxybenzen-1-ide (Less stable); 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene + $\ce{NH2-}$ → 2-amino-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide →($\ce{H-NH2}$) 3-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q004_sol_2_8fb60832_29102026.svg?v=8fb60832cbc7)

*3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene + $\ce{NH2-}$ (attack a or b) →[X] 2-amino-3-methoxybenzen-1-ide (Less stable); 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene + $\ce{NH2-}$ → 2-amino-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide →[$\ce{H-NH2}$] 3-methoxyaniline*

![drawn: 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene + $\ce{NH2-}$ (attack a or b) →(X) 2-amino-3-methoxybenzen-1-ide (Less stable); 3-methoxy-1,2-didehydrobenzene + $\ce{NH2-}$ → 2-amino-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide →($\ce{H-NH2}$) 3-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q004_solution_2_1f4a501d_29102026.svg?v=1f4a501dbe16)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

1. More stable as –ve charge is close to electron withdrawing group
2. $\because$ Incoming nucleophile ends on same ‘$\mathrm{C}$’ on which ‘$\mathrm{Br}$’ (Leaving group) was present
3. $\therefore$ NOT cine substitution.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{NaNH_2}$ (strong base) removes the most acidic ring H, the one at C2 lying between $\mathrm{OCH_3}$ and $\mathrm{Br}$ (enhanced by the $-I$ effect of $\mathrm{OCH_3}$).
2. Loss of $\mathrm{Br^-}$ from the aryl carbanion gives 3-methoxybenzyne (triple bond between C2 and C3): the elimination step.
3. $\mathrm{NH_2^-}$ adds to the benzyne carbon C3 (meta to $\mathrm{OCH_3}$) so that the resulting carbanion sits at C2, ortho to $\mathrm{OCH_3}$, where it is stabilised by the $-I$ effect: the addition step.
4. Protonation by $\mathrm{NH_3}$ gives $m\text{-}\mathrm{H_2N{-}C_6H_4{-}OCH_3}$ (m-anisidine), so $A$ = m-anisidine.
5. Net change: $\mathrm{Br}$ replaced by $\mathrm{NH_2}$ at the same ring position, i.e. a (nucleophilic aromatic) substitution, not cine substitution; only option $4$ shows m-anisidine, so the answer is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Haloarenes · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 4](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q004_45c5eeb9_29102026.png?v=45c5eeb91616)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 4](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q004_773a8ecf_29102026.png?v=773a8ecfd2b6)

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** NaNH2 replaces the halogen of a haloarene with an amino group, and whether the entering group lands on the carbon the halogen left or on the neighbouring one is what separates plain substitution from cine substitution.
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Options 2 and 3 are stored as the same text, 'and cine substitution reaction', and all four options begin with 'and', so the structure each option offers for A is missing from our copy; with two options that cannot be told apart there is nothing to read a route against.

No trap can be read from a set in which two options are indistinguishable, so the record needs repair against the printed paper before this question is tagged. The shape is recorded as two_part from the stem's own 'Identify A and predict the type of reaction', which is independent of the damage.

## Q5

Chemistry · physical · Solutions

Which of the following is dependent on temperature?

- **(1)** Molarity
- (2) Mole fraction
- (3) Weight percentage
- (4) Molality

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Molarity includes volume of solution which can change with change in temperature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Molarity is $M = \frac{n_{\text{solute}}}{V_{\text{solution}}\,(\text{L})}$, and $V_{\text{solution}}$ changes with temperature (expansion/contraction), so $M$ depends on $T$, which is option $1$.
2. Mole fraction $x = \frac{n_A}{n_A + n_B}$, weight percentage $= \frac{m_{\text{solute}}}{m_{\text{solution}}} \times 100$ and molality $m = \frac{n_{\text{solute}}}{\text{kg solvent}}$ use only moles and masses, which do not change with $T$, so options $2$, $3$ and $4$ are temperature independent.

**Subtopic:** Molarity · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 5](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q005_602a99ce_29102026.png?v=602a99ce0a91)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 5](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q005_2863ef3f_29102026.png?v=2863ef3f9414)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Comparison** (`compare`) — the difference between two things, or the correct pairing
- **Concept:** Only molarity is defined per unit volume of solution, and volume expands on warming; mole fraction, molality and weight percentage are all ratios of masses or of mole numbers, which heating leaves alone.
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 4  
  Molarity and molality differ by two letters and both read as moles of solute per something, so the right piece of reasoning, that volume changes on heating, gets attached to whichever of the two words comes to hand first.
- **Why:** This question requires four neighbouring concentration measures to be told apart on one criterion, demonstrated by the stem asking which is 'dependent on temperature' with molarity, mole fraction, weight percentage and molality as the options.
- **Note:** asks is compare in its own sense here, four look-alike concentration terms separated on a single criterion, and not a restatement of the printed shape. look_alike_term over nearby_fact by the swap test: put an unlike name in option 4 and the pull goes with it, so the near-identical word rather than any printed position is what carries a student there.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q6

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

With respect to the conformers of ethane, which of the following statements is true?

- (1) Bond angle changes but bond length remains same
- (2) Both bond angle and bond length change
- **(3)** Both bond angles and bond length remains same
- (4) Bond angle remains same but bond length changes

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. There is no change in bond angles and bond lengths in the conformations of ethane.
2. There is only change in dihedral angle.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Conformers of ethane interconvert by rotation about the $\mathrm{C-C}$ $\sigma$ bond, which changes only the dihedral (torsional) angle between the $\mathrm{C-H}$ bonds on the two carbons.
2. This rotation does not change the $\mathrm{C-C}$ or $\mathrm{C-H}$ bond lengths, or the $\angle \mathrm{H-C-H}$ and $\angle \mathrm{H-C-C}$ bond angles, so both stay the same, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Isomerism · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q006_58f2b2ac_29102026.png?v=58f2b2ac2faa)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q006_441e80b5_29102026.png?v=441e80b55811)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Conformers of ethane are interconverted by rotation about the C-C single bond alone, so no bond length and no bond angle differs between them; the only angle that changes is the dihedral.
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 1  
  The angle between the front and rear C-H bonds really does change, from 0 degrees to 60, so reading 'bond angle' as that angle makes half the option true while the other half, bond length unchanged, is true outright.
- **Why:** This question requires the definition of a conformer applied to two named geometric quantities, demonstrated by the options pairing 'bond angle' against 'bond length' for 'the conformers of ethane'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q7

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Name the gas that can readily decolourises acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ solution:

- **(1)** $\mathrm{SO_2}$
- (2) $\mathrm{NO_2}$
- (3) $\mathrm{P_2O_5}$
- (4) $\mathrm{CO_2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{SO_2}$ is readily decolourises acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Decolourising acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ needs a reducing agent that turns purple $\mathrm{MnO_4^-}$ into almost colourless $\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}$.
2. In $\mathrm{SO_2}$ sulphur is $+4$ and can rise to $+6$, so it acts as a reductant: $2\mathrm{MnO_4^-} + 5\mathrm{SO_2} + 2\mathrm{H_2O} \rightarrow 2\mathrm{Mn^{2+}} + 5\mathrm{SO_4^{2-}} + 4\mathrm{H^+}$.
3. In $\mathrm{P_2O_5}$ ($\mathrm{P}$ at $+5$) and $\mathrm{CO_2}$ ($\mathrm{C}$ at $+4$) the central atom is already in its highest oxidation state, so they cannot reduce $\mathrm{MnO_4^-}$.
4. $\mathrm{NO_2}$ is mainly known as an oxidising gas and is not the textbook reagent for decolourising $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$, so the answer is $\mathrm{SO_2}$, option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Allotropes, Oxyacids and Oxides of Sulphur · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 7](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q007_67a23353_29102026.png?v=67a23353a554)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 7](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q007_d057cf96_29102026.png?v=d057cf967a53)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Acidified permanganate loses its purple colour when it is reduced, so the gas that decolourises it has to be one that is readily oxidised, and that is sulphur dioxide going to sulphate.
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 2  
  NO2 is the other pungent oxide gas learnt alongside SO2 as one of the pair of acidic pollutants, so remembering the pair without remembering which member reduces permanganate delivers the partner.
- **Why:** This question requires the reagent that effects a named chemical change, demonstrated by the stem asking for 'the gas that can readily decolourises acidified KMnO4 solution' with four oxide formulas as options.
- **Note:** asks is reaction, not experiment: no setup, measurement or observation table is supplied, only the chemical change itself, and the answer is the reagent that brings it about. nearby_fact over look_alike_term by the swap test: all four options are small oxide formulas, so the shared XO2 shape separates nothing, and what makes NO2 reachable is its printed place beside SO2 as the other acidic pollutant gas.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q8

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

It is because of inability of $\mathrm{ns^2}$ electrons of the valence shell to participate in bonding that

- (1) $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ is oxidising while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is reducing
- (2) $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ and $\mathrm{Pb^{2+}}$ are both oxidising and reducing
- (3) $\mathrm{Sn^{4+}}$ is reducing while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is oxidising
- **(4)** $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ is reducing while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is oxidising

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Inability of $ns^{2}$ electrons of the valence shell to participate in bonding on moving down the group in heavier p-block elements is called inert pair effect
2. As a result, $\mathrm{Pb(II)}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Pb(IV)}$
3. $\mathrm{Sn(IV)}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Sn(II)}$
4. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Pb(IV)}$ is easily reduced to $\mathrm{Pb(II)}$
5. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Pb(IV)}$ is oxidising agent
6. $\mathrm{Sn(II)}$ is easily oxidised to $\mathrm{Sn(IV)}$
7. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Sn(II)}$ is reducing agent

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The reluctance of the valence $ns^2$ electrons to take part in bonding is the inert pair effect, which grows stronger down group 14.
2. For tin the effect is weak, so $\mathrm{Sn^{4+}}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$; $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}} \rightarrow \mathrm{Sn^{4+}} + 2e^-$ occurs readily, so $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ is reducing.
3. For lead the effect is strong, so $\mathrm{Pb^{2+}}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$; $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}} + 2e^- \rightarrow \mathrm{Pb^{2+}}$ occurs readily, so $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is oxidising.
4. $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ reducing and $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ oxidising matches option $4$.

**Subtopic:** General Properties & Trends of Group 14 (Carbon Family) · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 8](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q008_c6d58ee6_29102026.png?v=c6d58ee6ab68)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 8](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q008_33f8470b_29102026.png?v=33f8470be830)

#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** The inert pair effect grows down group 14, so lead is stable at +2 and tin at +4: Sn(II) gives electrons away and is reducing, while Pb(IV) takes them and is oxidising.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The two halves of the right answer are printed with their roles exchanged, which is what comes out if 'oxidising' and 'reducing' are assigned from which ion looks unusual rather than from which oxidation state is the stable one.
- **Why:** This question requires the inert pair effect applied to two named elements, demonstrated by the stem supplying the rule, 'inability of ns2 electrons of the valence shell to participate in bonding', and the options differing only in which ion is called oxidising and which reducing.

## Q9

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Kinetics

Mechanism of a hypothetical reaction $\mathrm{X_2 + Y_2 \rightarrow 2XY}$ is given below :
(i) $\mathrm{X_2 \rightarrow X + X}$ (fast)
(ii) $\mathrm{X + Y_2 \rightleftharpoons XY + Y}$ (slow)
(iii) $\mathrm{X + Y \rightarrow XY}$ (fast)
The overall order of the reaction will be

- (1) 2
- (2) 0
- **(3)** 1.5
- (4) 1

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. The solution of this question is given by assuming step (i) to be reversible which is not given in question
2. Overall rate $=$ Rate of slowest step (ii)
3. $= k[\mathrm{X}]\,[\mathrm{Y_2}]$ ...(1)
4. $k =$ rate constant of step (ii)
5. Assuming step (i) to be reversible, its equilibrium constant,
6. $k_{eq} = \frac{[\mathrm{X}]^{2}}{[\mathrm{X_2}]} \Rightarrow [\mathrm{X}] = k_{eq}^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}$ ...(2)
7. Put (2) in (1)
8. Rate $= kk_{eq}^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathrm{Y_2}]$
9. Overall order $= \frac{1}{2} + 1 = \frac{3}{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The slow step (ii) is rate-determining, so $\text{Rate} = k[\mathrm{X}][\mathrm{Y_2}]$.
2. X is an intermediate; treat the fast step (i) as a pre-equilibrium: $K = \frac{[\mathrm{X}]^2}{[\mathrm{X_2}]}$.
3. Solve for the intermediate: $[\mathrm{X}] = K^{1/2}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}$.
4. Substitute: $\text{Rate} = kK^{1/2}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{Y_2}] = k'[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{Y_2}]$.
5. Overall order $= \frac{1}{2} + 1 = 1.5$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Order of a Reaction · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 9](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q009_df321f00_29102026.png?v=df321f00a6f3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 9](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q009_40de238c_29102026.png?v=40de238c2aef)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The rate law comes from the slow step, and an intermediate appearing in it is replaced using the fast equilibrium before it: rate $= k[\mathrm{X}][\mathrm{Y_2}]$ with $[\mathrm{X}] \propto [\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}$ gives overall order 1.5.
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  Order 2 is what the balanced equation gives, one X2 plus one Y2, and also what the slow step gives if [X] is counted as an ordinary reactant instead of being replaced by $[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the rate law derived from the printed mechanism, demonstrated by step (ii) being marked (slow) and step (i) being a fast dissociation that supplies the intermediate X.
- **Note:** The pattern returned reaction_scheme for the three printed steps; the qtype axis has no scheme value and the options are bare numbers, so the shape is recorded as mcq.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q10

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

The equilibrium constants of the following are
$\mathrm{N_2 + 3H_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NH_3}$ $\quad K_1$
$\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$ $\quad K_2$
$\mathrm{H_2 + \frac{1}{2}O_2 \longrightarrow H_2O}$ $\quad K_3$
The equilibrium constant (K) of the reaction
$\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \overset{K}{\rightleftharpoons} 2NO + 3H_2O}$, will be

- **(1)** $K_2K_3^3/K_1$
- (2) $K_2K_3/K_1$
- (3) $K_2^3K_3/K_1$
- (4) $K_1K_3^3/K_2$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. (I) $\mathrm{N_2 + 3H_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NH_3}$; $K_1 = \frac{[\mathrm{NH_3}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{H_2}]^3}$
2. (II) $\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$; $K_2 = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]}$
3. (III) $\mathrm{H_2 + \frac{1}{2}O_2 \longrightarrow H_2O}$; $K_3 = \frac{[\mathrm{H_2O}]}{[\mathrm{H_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]^{1/2}}$
4. $(\mathrm{II} + 3 \times \mathrm{III} - \mathrm{II})$ will give
5. $\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \overset{K}{\rightleftharpoons} 2NO + 3H_2O}$;
6. $\therefore K = K_2 \times K_3^{3} / K_1$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Reverse reaction 1: $\mathrm{2NH_3 \rightleftharpoons N_2 + 3H_2}$, with constant $\frac{1}{K_1}$.
2. Keep reaction 2 as written: $\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$, with constant $K_2$.
3. Multiply reaction 3 by 3: $\mathrm{3H_2 + \frac{3}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons 3H_2O}$, with constant $K_3^3$.
4. Add the three: $\mathrm{N_2}$ and $\mathrm{3H_2}$ cancel, and $\mathrm{O_2}$ totals $1 + \frac{3}{2} = \frac{5}{2}$, giving $\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO + 3H_2O}$.
5. Constants multiply when reactions add: $K = \frac{1}{K_1} \cdot K_2 \cdot K_3^3 = \frac{K_2K_3^3}{K_1}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Law of Chemical Equilibrium · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 10](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q010_58b1cebd_29102026.png?v=58b1cebd1128)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 10](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q010_31b4ed40_29102026.png?v=31b4ed403254)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Reversing a reaction inverts its equilibrium constant, multiplying a reaction through by n raises its constant to the nth power, and adding reactions multiplies the constants.
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  The three reactions are combined the right way round, reaction 1 reversed and reactions 2 and 3 added, but reaction 3 has to be taken three times to make the 3H2O of the target, and leaving $K_3$ at the first power yields exactly this expression.
- **Why:** This question requires the three printed equilibria combined into the target reaction, demonstrated by the target '2NH3 + 5/2 O2 gives 2NO + 3H2O' needing reaction 1 reversed and reaction 3 taken three times.

## Q11

Chemistry · physical · Structure of Atom

Which one is the wrong statement?

- (1) The uncertainty principle is $\Delta E \times \Delta t \geq \frac{h}{4\pi}$
- (2) Half-filled and fully filled orbitals have greater stability due to greater exchange energy, greater symmetry and more balanced arrangement
- **(3)** The energy of $2s$ orbital is less than the energy of $2p$ orbital in case of Hydrogen like atoms
- (4) de-Broglie's wavelength is given by $\lambda = \frac{h}{mv}$, where $m$ = mass of the particle, $v$ = group velocity of the particle

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Energy of $2s$-orbital and $2p$-orbital in case of hydrogen like atoms is equal.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Statement 1: the energy-time form of the uncertainty principle is $\Delta E \cdot \Delta t \geq \frac{h}{4\pi}$, which is correct.
2. Statement 2: half-filled and fully filled subshells are extra stable because of symmetry and larger exchange energy, which is correct.
3. Statement 3: for a hydrogen-like atom $E_n = -13.6\,\frac{Z^2}{n^2}\,\text{eV}$ depends only on $n$, so $E_{2s} = E_{2p}$; the claim $E_{2s} < E_{2p}$ is wrong.
4. Statement 4: $\lambda = \frac{h}{mv}$ with $v$ the particle velocity, which equals the group velocity of the matter wave, so it is correct.
5. The wrong statement is $3$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Atomic Orbitals: Shapes, Energies and Nodes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 11](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q011_446e88cb_29102026.png?v=446e88cb641d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 11](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q011_4a15720f_29102026.png?v=4a15720f75ac)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** In a one-electron, hydrogen-like species the orbital energy depends only on n, so 2s and 2p are degenerate; the 2s-below-2p ordering belongs to many-electron atoms.
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 1  
  Statement 3 is a true sentence with one condition added, 'in case of Hydrogen like atoms', and reading past that clause passes it as correct, after which the search for a wrong statement falls on the uncertainty principle printed in its energy-time form.
- **Why:** This question requires one printed statement to be judged false against the book's own sentences, demonstrated by option 3 placing the 2s-below-2p ordering 'in case of Hydrogen like atoms', where the energy depends only on n.
- **Note:** The device is the condition clause attached to the key: drop 'in case of Hydrogen like atoms' and statement 3 is true. That is the retired true_statement_trap and polarity device, which the retirement map sends to condition_missed. The trap names option 1 because that is where the search for a wrong statement lands once statement 3 has been passed.

## Q12

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

Which one of the following statements is not correct?

- **(1)** The value of equilibrium constant is changed in the presence of a catalyst in the reaction at equilibrium
- (2) Enzymes catalyse mainly bio-chemical reactions
- (3) Coenzymes increase the catalytic activity of enzyme
- (4) Catalyst does not initiate any reaction

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. A catalyst decreases activation energies of both the forward and backward reaction by same amount, therefore, it speeds up both forward and backward reaction by same rate.
2. Equilibrium constant is therefore not affected by catalyst at a given temperature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A catalyst lowers the activation energy $E_a$ of the forward and reverse reactions by the same amount.
2. So $k_f$ and $k_b$ increase by the same factor, and $K = \frac{k_f}{k_b}$ stays the same: the catalyst only makes equilibrium arrive sooner.
3. Hence statement $1$ (equilibrium constant changes with a catalyst) is false.
4. Statements $2$ (enzymes catalyse mainly biochemical reactions), $3$ (coenzymes increase enzyme activity) and $4$ (a catalyst does not initiate a reaction) are correct as NCERT teaches them, so the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Effect of a Catalyst on Equilibrium · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 12](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q012_936c94ff_29102026.png?v=936c94ffa672)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 12](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q012_8fc6e171_29102026.png?v=8fc6e171fc7b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A catalyst changes only the rate at which equilibrium is reached; the equilibrium constant is fixed by the temperature and is untouched by it.
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  The stem asks which statement is not correct, and this one is a correct statement written as a flat denial, so holding 'a catalyst is what makes the reaction go' makes the denial read as the planted error.
- **Why:** This question requires a true statement about catalysts to be separated from a false one, demonstrated by option 1 claiming that 'the value of equilibrium constant is changed in the presence of a catalyst in the reaction at equilibrium'.
- **Note:** The same polarity device as q011, a correct statement ticked because the stem is negated, which is the retired true_statement_trap and maps to condition_missed. It differs from q011 in that this key is flatly false, so no route runs through a qualifying clause inside the key.

## Q13

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

$\mathrm{HgCl_2}$ and $\mathrm{I_2}$ both when dissolved in water containing $\mathrm{I^-}$ ions the pair of species formed is

- (1) $\mathrm{HgI_2}$, $\mathrm{I^-}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{HgI_4^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{I_3^-}$
- (3) $\mathrm{Hg_2I_2}$, $\mathrm{I^-}$
- (4) $\mathrm{HgI_2}$, $\mathrm{I_3^-}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In a solution containing $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$, $\mathrm{I_2}$ and $\mathrm{I^-}$, both $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$ and $\mathrm{I_2}$ compete for $\mathrm{I^-}$.
2. Since formation constant of $[\mathrm{HgI_4}]^{2-}$ is $1.9 \times 10^{30}$ which is very large as compared with $\mathrm{I_3^-}$ $(\mathrm{K_f} = 700)$
3. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{I^-}$ will preferentially combine with $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$.
4. $\mathrm{HgCl_2} + 2\mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow \underset{\text{Red ppt}}{\mathrm{HgI_2}\downarrow} + 2\mathrm{Cl^-}$
5. $\mathrm{HgI_2} + 2\mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow \underset{\text{soluble}}{[\mathrm{HgI_4}]^{2-}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{HgCl_2 + 2I^- \rightarrow HgI_2 + 2Cl^-}$ gives the red precipitate first.
2. With more $\mathrm{I^-}$ present, $\mathrm{HgI_2 + 2I^- \rightarrow [HgI_4]^{2-}}$, a soluble complex.
3. $\mathrm{I_2}$ dissolves in iodide solution as $\mathrm{I_2 + I^- \rightarrow I_3^-}$.
4. So the pair is $\mathrm{HgI_4^{2-}}$ and $\mathrm{I_3^-}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Interhalogen Compounds & Important Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 13](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q013_9cba44dd_29102026.png?v=9cba44dd77b7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 13](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q013_3db4bb3c_29102026.png?v=3db4bb3c30dc)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** With iodide still available, mercury(II) goes past the $\mathrm{HgI_2}$ precipitate to the soluble $\mathrm{[HgI_4]^{2-}}$, while iodine takes up iodide to give triiodide
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  Iodine taking up iodide to give $\mathrm{I_3^-}$ is the half that settles at once, and the mercury half is then filled in with the $\mathrm{HgI_2}$ precipitate of the qualitative test. With $\mathrm{I^-}$ in excess that precipitate dissolves on to $\mathrm{[HgI_4]^{2-}}$, so only one of the two species printed is right.
- **Why:** This question requires the products of two different iodide reactions delivered together, demonstrated by the stem asking for 'the pair of species formed' when both $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$ and $\mathrm{I_2}$ dissolve in water 'containing $\mathrm{I^-}$ ions'.
- **Note:** E1's pattern recorded plain_mcq by default; the printed options are pairs of species accepted or rejected whole, so the shape on the page is two_part.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q14

Chemistry · organic · Amines

The correct increasing order of basic strength for the following compounds is

![printed figure: (I) aniline; (II) 4-nitroaniline; (III) 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q014_97bcc387_29102026.svg?v=97bcc3871ab9)

*(I) aniline; (II) 4-nitroaniline; (III) 4-methylaniline*

![drawn: (I) aniline; (II) 4-nitroaniline; (III) 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q014_question_8c627c32_29102026.svg?v=8c627c320d04)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

Structures: aniline `Nc1ccccc1` ![aniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q014_question_s1_2b0b7f6a_29102026.svg?v=2b0b7f6a5ce7); 4-nitroaniline `Nc1ccc([N+](=O)[O-])cc1` ![4-nitroaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q014_question_s2_7e623ecc_29102026.svg?v=7e623ecc1e2d); 4-methylaniline `Cc1ccc(N)cc1` ![4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q014_question_s3_5f1a0db7_29102026.svg?v=5f1a0db74b01)

- (1) III < I < II
- (2) III < II < I
- **(3)** II < I < III
- (4) II < III < I

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ has strong $-R$ effect and $-\mathrm{CH_3}$ shows $+R$ effect.
2. $\therefore$ Order of basic strength is

![printed figure: 4-nitroaniline < aniline < 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q014_sol_1_3c94f3c9_29102026.svg?v=3c94f3c91216)

*4-nitroaniline < aniline < 4-methylaniline*

![drawn: 4-nitroaniline < aniline < 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q014_solution_1_30f71d71_29102026.svg?v=30f71d710f5c)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure: (I) is aniline $C_6H_5NH_2$, (II) is $p$-nitroaniline $p\text{-}O_2N\text{-}C_6H_4NH_2$, (III) is $p$-toluidine $p\text{-}H_3C\text{-}C_6H_4NH_2$.
2. In (II) the $-NO_2$ group at the para position withdraws electrons by $-R$ and $-I$ effects, reducing the availability of the lone pair on $N$, so (II) is less basic than (I).
3. In (III) the $-CH_3$ group at the para position donates electrons by $+I$ effect and hyperconjugation, increasing the electron density on $N$, so (III) is more basic than (I).
4. Hence the increasing order of basic strength is $\text{II} < \text{I} < \text{III}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Reactions of Amines (Basicity, Alkylation and Acylation) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 14](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q014_fc1801d1_29102026.png?v=fc1801d1fd51)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 14](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q014_ed6555b7_29102026.png?v=ed6555b7797b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** A para methyl group feeds electron density to the nitrogen and raises aniline's basicity; a para nitro group withdraws it and lowers it
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  Ranking the three rings by how the substituent pushes or pulls electron density gives methyl strongest and nitro weakest, and option 1 prints that sequence in exactly that direction. The stem asks for increasing basic strength, so the same ranking has to be written the other way round.
- **Why:** This question requires three anilines ranked by substituent effect, demonstrated by the stem's 'correct increasing order of basic strength' with the three compounds labelled I, II and III.
- **Note:** The three compounds arrive as a figure whose supplied text names them aniline, 4-nitroaniline and 4-methylaniline; I/II/III label structures, not statements, so qtype stays mcq and the ordering is carried on the asks axis. E1's pattern recorded arrange_in_order, which the current qtype vocabulary has no value for.

## Q15

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

An example of a sigma bonded organometallic compound is

- **(1)** Grignard's reagent
- (2) Ferrocene
- (3) Cobaltocene
- (4) Ruthenocene

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Grignard's reagent i.e., $\mathrm{RMgX}$ is $\sigma$-bonded organometallic compound.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In Grignard's reagent $\mathrm{RMgX}$ the alkyl carbon is bonded to $\mathrm{Mg}$ by a single $\sigma$ bond, so it is a $\sigma$-bonded organometallic compound; ferrocene $\mathrm{Fe(C_5H_5)_2}$, cobaltocene $\mathrm{Co(C_5H_5)_2}$ and ruthenocene $\mathrm{Ru(C_5H_5)_2}$ are $\pi$-bonded sandwich compounds, so the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Bonding in Coordination Compounds and Metal Carbonyls · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

## Q16

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

A $20\,\mathrm{litre}$ container at $400\,\mathrm{K}$ contains $\mathrm{CO_2(g)}$ at pressure $0.4\,\mathrm{atm}$ and an excess of $\mathrm{SrO}$ (neglect the volume of solid $\mathrm{SrO}$). The volume of the containers is now decreased by moving the movable piston fitted in the container. The maximum volume of the container, when pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ attains its maximum value, will be (Given that : $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2(g)}$. $K_p = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$)

- (1) $10\,\mathrm{litre}$
- (2) $4\,\mathrm{litre}$
- (3) $2\,\mathrm{litre}$
- **(4)** $5\,\mathrm{litre}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Max. pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ = Pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ at equilibrium
2. For reaction,
3. $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2}$
4. $K_p = P_{\mathrm{CO_2}} = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$ = maximum pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$
5. Volume of container at this stage,
6. $V = \frac{nRT}{P} \quad \ldots(\mathrm{i})$
7. Since container is sealed and reaction was not earlier at equilibrium
8. $\therefore\; n = \text{constant}$
9. $n = \frac{PV}{RT} = \frac{0.4 \times 20}{RT} \quad \ldots(\mathrm{ii})$
10. Put equation (ii) in equation (i)
11. $V = \left[\frac{0.4 \times 20}{RT}\right]\frac{RT}{1.6} = 5\,\mathrm{L}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2(g)}$, $K_p = p_{\mathrm{CO_2}}$, so the maximum pressure $\mathrm{CO_2}$ can attain is $p_{\mathrm{CO_2}} = K_p = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$; beyond this $\mathrm{CO_2}$ reacts with excess $\mathrm{SrO}$ to form $\mathrm{SrCO_3}$.
2. Until $p_{\mathrm{CO_2}}$ reaches $1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$, no $\mathrm{CO_2}$ is consumed, so at constant $T = 400\,\mathrm{K}$ Boyle's law applies: $P_1V_1 = P_2V_2$.
3. $0.4\,\mathrm{atm} \times 20\,\mathrm{L} = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm} \times V$.
4. $V = \frac{0.4 \times 20}{1.6}\,\mathrm{L} = \frac{8}{1.6}\,\mathrm{L} = 5\,\mathrm{L}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Homogeneous and Heterogeneous Equilibria · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 16](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q016_7f072311_29102026.png?v=7f07231139f4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 16](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q016_9675c870_29102026.png?v=9675c870720d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** For the solid-gas equilibrium $\mathrm{SrCO_3 \rightleftharpoons SrO + CO_2}$ the gas pressure cannot exceed $K_p$, so compression raises $p_{\mathrm{CO_2}}$ only until it reaches 1.6 atm
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  $K_p = 1.6$ atm IS the ceiling on the $\mathrm{CO_2}$ pressure, but read as the extra pressure the solid can supply on top of the 0.4 atm already present it puts the ceiling at 2.0 atm, and $0.4 \times 20 = 8$ atm L divided by 2.0 atm gives 4 L. $K_p$ for this equilibrium equals $p_{\mathrm{CO_2}}$ itself, so nothing is added to it.
- **Why:** This question requires the volume at which the gas pressure meets its equilibrium ceiling, demonstrated by the stem's 'when pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ attains its maximum value' together with 'Given that ... $K_p = 1.6\ \mathrm{atm}$'.

## Q18

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

Pick out the correct statement with respect $[\mathrm{Mn(CN)_6}]^{3-}$

- (1) It is $sp^3d^2$ hybridised and tetrahedral
- **(2)** It is $d^2sp^3$ hybridised and octahedral
- (3) It is $dsp^2$ hybridised and square planar
- (4) It is $sp^3d^2$ hybridised and octahedral

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $[\mathrm{Mn(CN)_6}]^{3-}$
2. $\mathrm{Mn(III)} = [\mathrm{Ar}]3d^{4}$
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ being strong field ligand forces pairing of electrons
4. This gives $t_{2g}^{4}e_{g}^{0}$
5. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Mn(III)} = [\mathrm{Ar}]$

![printed figure: orbital-box diagram: 3d boxes (↑↓, ↑, ↑, empty, empty), 4s box (empty), 4p boxes (empty, empty, empty); the last two 3d boxes, the 4s box and the three 4p boxes are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^2sp^3$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q018_sol_1_1f523e7c_29102026.svg?v=1f523e7c9692)

*orbital-box diagram: 3d boxes (↑↓, ↑, ↑, empty, empty), 4s box (empty), 4p boxes (empty, empty, empty); the last two 3d boxes, the 4s box and the three 4p boxes are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^2sp^3$*

6. $\because$ Coordination number of $\mathrm{Mn} = 6$
7. $\therefore$ Structure = octahedral
8. $[\mathrm{Mn(CN)_6}]^{3-} =$

![printed figure: orbital-box diagram: (Ar) followed by five boxes (↑↓, ↑, ↑, ××, ××), one box (××) and three boxes (××, ××, ××); the six boxes holding ×× are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^2sp^3$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q018_sol_2_e2229ff2_29102026.svg?v=e2229ff2e7ca)

*orbital-box diagram: [Ar] followed by five boxes (↑↓, ↑, ↑, ××, ××), one box (××) and three boxes (××, ××, ××); the six boxes holding ×× are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^2sp^3$*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Oxidation state of Mn: $x + 6(-1) = -3 \Rightarrow x = +3$.
2. $\mathrm{Mn^{3+}}$ has configuration $[\mathrm{Ar}]\,3d^4$.
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is a strong-field ligand, so the four $d$ electrons pair up in $t_{2g}$: $t_{2g}^4 e_g^0$ (low spin, 2 unpaired electrons).
4. Two inner $3d$ orbitals are left empty, so hybridisation uses $3d^2\,4s\,4p^3$, i.e. $d^2sp^3$.
5. Six ligands with $d^2sp^3$ hybridisation give an octahedral geometry, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Bond Theory, Its Limitations and Magnetic Properties of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 5)

![printed question 18](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q018_e46b6631_29102026.png?v=e46b66312761)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 18](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q018_5d8d0917_29102026.png?v=5d8d09176121)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is a strong-field ligand, so Mn(III) pairs up its $d^4$ electrons and uses inner 3d orbitals: $d^2sp^3$ hybridisation, octahedral for six ligands
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  Six ligands fix the shape as octahedral, which this option gets right, and $sp^3d^2$ is the six-orbital set learnt for simple molecules such as $\mathrm{SF_6}$. A strong-field ligand frees two inner 3d orbitals instead, making the complex $d^2sp^3$.
- **Why:** This question requires the hybridisation and shape of one named complex judged as a whole claim, demonstrated by the stem 'Pick out the correct statement with respect $\mathrm{[Mn(CN)_6]^{3-}}$' with options that each pair a hybridisation with a geometry.
- **Note:** E1's pattern recorded plain_mcq by default; the stem's instruction 'Pick out the correct statement' and four full statements in the options make the printed shape statement-judging, so qtype is statements.

## Q19

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Match the interhalogen compounds of column I with the geometry in column II and assign the correct code

| List-I | | List-II | |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) | $\mathrm{XX'}$ | (i) | T-shape |
| (b) | $\mathrm{XX'_3}$ | (ii) | Pentagonal bipyramidal |
| (c) | $\mathrm{XX'_5}$ | (iii) | Linear |
| (d) | $\mathrm{XX'_7}$ | (iv) | Square-pyramidal |
|  |  | (v) | Tetrahedral |

- **(1)** (iii) (i) (iv) (ii)
- (2) (v) (iv) (iii) (ii)
- (3) (iv) (iii) (ii) (i)
- (4) (iii) (iv) (i) (ii)

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{XX'} \rightarrow$ Linear
2. $\mathrm{XX_3'} \rightarrow$ Example : $\mathrm{CIF_3} \rightarrow$ T-shape
3. $\mathrm{XX_5'} \rightarrow$ Example : $\mathrm{BrF_5} \rightarrow$ Square pyramidal
4. $\mathrm{XX_7'} \rightarrow$ Example : $\mathrm{IF_7} \rightarrow$ Pentagonal bipyramidal

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{XX'}$ is a diatomic molecule, so its geometry is linear: (a) $\rightarrow$ (iii).
2. $\mathrm{XX'_3}$: central X has 3 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs ($sp^3d$), so the shape is T-shaped: (b) $\rightarrow$ (i).
3. $\mathrm{XX'_5}$: central X has 5 bond pairs and 1 lone pair ($sp^3d^2$), so the shape is square pyramidal: (c) $\rightarrow$ (iv).
4. $\mathrm{XX'_7}$: central X has 7 bond pairs and no lone pair ($sp^3d^3$), so the shape is pentagonal bipyramidal: (d) $\rightarrow$ (ii).
5. The code (iii) (i) (iv) (ii) matches option $1$; tetrahedral (v) is unused.

**Subtopic:** Interhalogen Compounds & Important Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 5)

![printed question 19](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q019_975a6bb4_29102026.png?v=975a6bb4e4fb)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 19](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q019_f81748a5_29102026.png?v=f81748a5d438)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Match the columns** (`match_columns`) — two labelled lists to pair, the answer is a pairing code
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** VSEPR on an interhalogen: count bond pairs plus lone pairs on the central halogen, so $\mathrm{XX'}$ is linear, $\mathrm{XX'_3}$ T-shaped, $\mathrm{XX'_5}$ square-pyramidal and $\mathrm{XX'_7}$ pentagonal bipyramidal
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  The shapes are assigned from the subscript alone: three bonds reads as the pyramid remembered from $\mathrm{NH_3}$, so $\mathrm{XX'_3}$ is given square-pyramidal and T-shape falls to $\mathrm{XX'_5}$, leaving a code that is right on $\mathrm{XX'}$ and $\mathrm{XX'_7}$ and wrong on the two rows in between.
- **Why:** This question requires the VSEPR pair count applied to each formula, demonstrated by column I printing only the general formulas "$\mathrm{XX'}$", "$\mathrm{XX'_3}$", "$\mathrm{XX'_5}$", "$\mathrm{XX'_7}$" with no named compound attached, so each shape has to be built from the subscript rather than recalled against a name.
- **Note:** Column II prints five entries against four rows, so (v) Tetrahedral is an unpaired distractor and appears in option 2 only. Key as printed, Answer (1), agrees with the pair counts.

## Q20

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

The species, having bond angles of $120^\circ$ is

- (1) $\mathrm{ClF_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{NCl_3}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{BCl_3}$
- (4) $\mathrm{PH_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: boron trichloride ($\ce{BCl3}$), Cl–B–Cl bond angle 120°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q020_sol_1_4eb1245c_29102026.svg?v=4eb1245c859e)

*boron trichloride ($\ce{BCl3}$), Cl–B–Cl bond angle 120°*

![drawn: boron trichloride ($\ce{BCl3}$), Cl–B–Cl bond angle 120°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q020_solution_1_d505ad64_29102026.svg?v=d505ad64a2b7)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In $\mathrm{BCl_3}$, B has 3 bond pairs and 0 lone pairs, so it is $sp^2$ hybridised and trigonal planar with bond angle $120^\circ$.
2. In $\mathrm{ClF_3}$, Cl has 3 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs ($sp^3d$), giving a T-shape with bond angle $\approx 87.5^\circ$, not $120^\circ$.
3. In $\mathrm{NCl_3}$, N has 3 bond pairs and 1 lone pair ($sp^3$), giving a trigonal pyramidal shape with bond angle $< 109.5^\circ$.
4. In $\mathrm{PH_3}$, P has 3 bond pairs and 1 lone pair, giving a pyramidal shape with bond angle $\approx 93.5^\circ$.
5. Only $\mathrm{BCl_3}$ has $120^\circ$ bond angles, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 5)

![printed question 20](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q020_986ea5a4_29102026.png?v=986ea5a4b5aa)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 20](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q020_3c6b10d7_29102026.png?v=3c6b10d76500)

<!--ecat-->

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** VSEPR: three bond pairs and no lone pair give a trigonal planar molecule with $120^\circ$ angles; a lone pair changes both the arrangement and the angle
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 1  
  $120^\circ$ is the angle printed between the equatorial positions of the trigonal bipyramid, which is $\mathrm{ClF_3}$'s parent arrangement, so reading the parent geometry's angle off the table of shapes lands here. Two of the five pairs on chlorine are lone pairs and take equatorial places, leaving a T-shaped molecule with angles close to $90^\circ$.
- **Why:** This question requires VSEPR applied to four named species, demonstrated by the stem 'The species, having bond angles of $120^\circ$ is' with four formulas and no criterion printed.
- **Note:** nearby_fact rather than look_alike_term on option 1: swapping $\mathrm{ClF_3}$ for an unlike formula keeps the pull as long as the species' parent arrangement carries a $120^\circ$ angle, so what carries the student is the angle printed in the neighbouring row of the table of shapes, not a resemblance between $\mathrm{ClF_3}$ and $\mathrm{BCl_3}$.

## Q21

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

Predict the correct intermediate and product in the following reaction $\mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv CH}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{HgSO_4}]{\mathrm{H_2O,\ H_2SO_4}}\underset{\text{(A)}}{\text{intermediate}}\longrightarrow\underset{\text{(B)}}{\text{product}}$

- (1) prop-1-en-2-ol `C=C(C)O`; prop-1-en-2-yl sulfate `C=C(C)OS(=O)(=O)[O-]` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{OH}}{\underset{|}{\mathrm{C}}}=\mathrm{CH_2}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{SO_4}}{\underset{|}{\mathrm{C}}}=\mathrm{CH_2}$
- (2) propan-2-one `CC(C)=O`; prop-1-yne `C#CC` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{O}}{\underset{\|}{\mathrm{C}}}-\mathrm{CH_3}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv CH}$
- **(3)** prop-1-en-2-ol `C=C(C)O`; propan-2-one `CC(C)=O` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{OH}}{\underset{|}{\mathrm{C}}}=\mathrm{CH_2}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{O}}{\underset{\|}{\mathrm{C}}}-\mathrm{CH_3}$
- (4) prop-1-en-2-yl sulfate `C=C(C)OS(=O)(=O)[O-]`; propan-2-one `CC(C)=O` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{SO_4}}{\underset{|}{\mathrm{C}}}=\mathrm{CH_2}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C}-\underset{\mathrm{O}}{\underset{\|}{\mathrm{C}}}-\mathrm{CH_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: prop-1-yne → 2-hydroxyprop-1-en-1-yl (A) (printed $\ce{H3C-C(OH)=CH}$); (A) →(Tautomerism) propan-2-one (B)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q021_sol_1_1802c0a9_29102026.svg?v=1802c0a93df1)

*prop-1-yne → 2-hydroxyprop-1-en-1-yl (A) (printed $\ce{H3C-C(OH)=CH}$); (A) →[Tautomerism] propan-2-one (B)*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Dilute $\mathrm{H_2SO_4}$ with $\mathrm{HgSO_4}$ adds $\mathrm{H_2O}$ across the triple bond of $\mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv CH}$ following Markovnikov's rule: $\mathrm{OH}$ goes to the more substituted carbon (C-2).
2. This gives the enol intermediate $\mathrm{(A)}: \mathrm{H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2}$.
3. The enol is unstable and tautomerises to the keto form: $\mathrm{H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2 \rightarrow H_3C-CO-CH_3}$, so $\mathrm{(B)}$ is acetone.
4. A = enol $\mathrm{H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2}$ and B = $\mathrm{H_3C-CO-CH_3}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Properties of Alkynes · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 5)

![printed question 21](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q021_b620d2aa_29102026.png?v=b620d2aae972)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 21](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q021_4420f484_29102026.png?v=4420f484e628)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Acid-catalysed hydration of an alkyne over $\mathrm{HgSO_4}$ adds water by Markovnikov's rule to an enol, which tautomerises to the ketone
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  Acetone is the product this hydration is remembered by, and option 4 carries it in box B; box A is then filled in from the $\mathrm{H_2SO_4}$ written over the arrow, as a sulphate-bearing species. What adds across the triple bond is water, so A is the enol $\mathrm{H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2}$.
- **Why:** This question requires both the intermediate and the final product of one hydration delivered together, demonstrated by the stem 'Predict the correct intermediate and product' with every option printing an A and a B.
- **Note:** E1's pattern recorded reaction_scheme from the arrow in the stem; the current qtype vocabulary has no scheme value, and each option supplies two boxes judged together, so two_part records the printed shape.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q22

Chemistry · physical · Solutions

If molality of the dilute solution is doubled, the value of molal depression constant ($K_f$) will be

- (1) Halved
- (2) Tripled
- **(3)** Unchanged
- (4) Doubled

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $K_f$ (molal depression constant) is a characteristic of solvent and is independent of molality.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\Delta T_f = K_f \, m$, where $K_f$ is the molal depression (cryoscopic) constant.
2. $K_f = \frac{R \, M_{solvent} \, T_f^2}{1000 \, \Delta_{fus}H}$ depends only on the solvent's properties, not on $m$.
3. Doubling $m$ doubles $\Delta T_f$ while $K_f$ stays unchanged, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Depression of Freezing Point · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 22](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q022_6f4315a9_29102026.png?v=6f4315a9d280)
