## Q23

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

The heating of phenyl-methyl ethers with $\mathrm{HI}$ produces.

- (1) Iodobenzene
- **(2)** Phenol
- (3) Benzene
- (4) Ethyl chlorides

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: methoxybenzene →(HI) phenol + $\ce{CH3I}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q023_sol_1_7a9e6ce1_29102026.svg?v=7a9e6ce1ff7a)

*methoxybenzene →[HI] phenol + $\ce{CH3I}$*

![drawn: methoxybenzene →(HI) phenol + $\ce{CH3I}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q023_solution_1_71e584bd_29102026.svg?v=71e584bd0c09)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The ether is anisole, $\mathrm{C_6H_5{-}O{-}CH_3}$.
2. HI protonates the ether oxygen, giving $\mathrm{C_6H_5{-}\overset{+}{O}H{-}CH_3}$.
3. The $\mathrm{O{-}C_{aryl}}$ bond has partial double-bond character from resonance, and $\mathrm{I^-}$ cannot attack an sp$^2$ ring carbon, so that bond stays intact.
4. $\mathrm{I^-}$ attacks the methyl carbon by $S_N2$: $\mathrm{C_6H_5OCH_3 + HI \rightarrow C_6H_5OH + CH_3I}$.
5. The aromatic product is phenol, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Ethers · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 23](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q023_ebac6758_29102026.png?v=ebac67582701)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 23](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q023_d38e19c3_29102026.png?v=d38e19c37275)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** HI cleaves an aryl alkyl ether at the alkyl carbon, so anisole gives phenol and methyl iodide, never an aryl iodide
- **Trap:** **Wrong site attacked** (`wrong_site_attacked`) — the reagent is right but it attacks the other site, atom or bond than the one the question turns on — option 1  
  Cleavage by HI hands the iodide to one carbon and the OH to the other, and letting the iodide take the ring carbon gives iodobenzene with methanol. Iodide attacks the methyl carbon, because the aryl C-O bond has partial double-bond character from the ring and the aryl carbon takes no backside attack.
- **Why:** This question requires the products of HI cleavage of an aryl alkyl ether, demonstrated by the stem 'The heating of phenyl-methyl ethers with HI produces' against options that differ in which fragment keeps which atom.
- **Note:** Option 4 is printed in the plural, 'Ethyl chlorides'; tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q24

Chemistry · physical · Electrochemistry

In the electrochemical cell $\mathrm{Zn}|\mathrm{ZnSO_4}(0.01\,\mathrm{M})||\mathrm{CuSO_4}(1.0\,\mathrm{M})|\mathrm{Cu}$, the emf of this Daniel cell is $E_1$. When the concentration of $\mathrm{ZnSO_4}$ is changed to $1.0\,\mathrm{M}$ and that of $\mathrm{CuSO_4}$ changed to $0.01\,\mathrm{M}$, the emf changes to $E_2$. From the following, which one is the relationship between $E_1$ and $E_2$? (Given, $\frac{RT}{F} = 0.059$)

- (1) $E_1 < E_2$
- **(2)** $E_1 > E_2$
- (3) $E_2 = 0 \neq E_1$
- (4) $E_1 = E_2$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Zn}|\mathrm{ZnSO_4}(0.01\,\mathrm{M})||\mathrm{CuSO_4}(1.0\,\mathrm{M})|\mathrm{Cu}$
2. $\therefore\ E_1 = E^{\circ}_{\mathrm{cell}} - \frac{2.303RT}{2 \times F} \times \log\frac{(0.01)}{1}$
3. When concentrations are changed
4. $\therefore\ E_2 = E^{\circ}_{\mathrm{cell}} - \frac{2.303RT}{2F} \times \log\frac{1}{0.01}$
5. i.e., $E_1 > E_2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Cell reaction: $\mathrm{Zn} + \mathrm{Cu^{2+}} \rightarrow \mathrm{Zn^{2+}} + \mathrm{Cu}$, so $n = 2$.
2. Nernst equation: $E = E^\circ - \frac{0.059}{2}\log\frac{[\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}]}{[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]}$.
3. Case 1: $E_1 = E^\circ - \frac{0.059}{2}\log\frac{0.01}{1.0} = E^\circ - 0.0295 \times (-2) = E^\circ + 0.059\,\mathrm{V}$.
4. Case 2: $E_2 = E^\circ - \frac{0.059}{2}\log\frac{1.0}{0.01} = E^\circ - 0.0295 \times 2 = E^\circ - 0.059\,\mathrm{V}$.
5. $E_1 - E_2 = 0.118\,\mathrm{V} > 0$, so $E_1 > E_2$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Nernst Equation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 24](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q024_a8520b79_29102026.png?v=a8520b798482)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 24](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q024_a4581b4e_29102026.png?v=a4581b4e4056)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Nernst equation: the Daniell cell emf falls as $[\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}]/[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]$ rises, so swapping the two concentrations moves the emf from $E^\circ + 0.059$ to $E^\circ - 0.059$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  Building the log term with the copper ion on top instead of the zinc ion flips both substitutions: the first cell comes out as $E^\circ - 0.059$ and the second as $E^\circ + 0.059$, which is exactly this inequality. For $\mathrm{Zn + Cu^{2+} \rightarrow Zn^{2+} + Cu}$ the quotient is product over reactant, $[\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}]/[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]$.
- **Why:** This question requires two Nernst substitutions compared, demonstrated by the stem asking 'which one is the relationship between $E_1$ and $E_2$' and supplying 'Given, $\frac{RT}{F} = 0.059$'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q25

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Kinetics

A first order reaction has a specific reaction rate of $10^{-2}\,\mathrm{second^{-1}}$. How much time will it take for $20\,\mathrm{g}$ of the reactant to reduce to $5\,\mathrm{g}$?

- **(1)** $138.6\,\mathrm{second}$
- (2) $346.5\,\mathrm{second}$
- (3) $693.0\,\mathrm{second}$
- (4) $238.6\,\mathrm{second}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $t_{1/2} = \frac{0.693}{10^{-2}}\,\mathrm{second}$
2. For the reduction of $20\,\mathrm{g}$ of reactant to $5\,\mathrm{g}$, two $t_{1/2}$ is required.
3. $\therefore\ t = 2 \times \frac{0.693}{10^{-2}}\,\mathrm{second}$
4. $= 138.6\,\mathrm{second}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a first order reaction, $t = \frac{2.303}{k}\log\frac{[A]_0}{[A]}$.
2. $\frac{[A]_0}{[A]} = \frac{20\,\mathrm{g}}{5\,\mathrm{g}} = 4$.
3. $t = \frac{2.303}{10^{-2}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}}\log 4 = 230.3 \times 0.602\,\mathrm{s}$.
4. $t \approx 138.6\,\mathrm{s}$ (check: $2 t_{1/2} = 2 \times \frac{0.693}{10^{-2}} = 138.6\,\mathrm{s}$), which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** First Order Reactions · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 25](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q025_c0693109_29102026.png?v=c06931095dcd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 25](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q025_7caca1e5_29102026.png?v=7caca1e5c7b1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** First order kinetics: $t = (2.303/k)\log(N_0/N)$, and a fall from 20 g to 5 g is a quarter left, that is two half-lives
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  The half-life $0.693/k$ comes out right at 69.3 s, and the count it is then multiplied by is taken from the 5 printed in '5 g', giving $5 \times 69.3 = 346.5$ s exactly. The count has to be the number of halvings: 20 g to 10 g to 5 g is two, so $t = 138.6$ s.
- **Why:** This question requires the first-order time for a reactant to fall to a quarter of its mass, demonstrated by the stem's 'specific reaction rate of $10^{-2}\ \mathrm{second^{-1}}$' and '$20\ \mathrm{g}$ of the reactant to reduce to $5\ \mathrm{g}$'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q26

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

Which one of the following pairs of species have the same bond order?

- (1) $\mathrm{O_2}$, $\mathrm{NO^+}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{CN^-}$, $\mathrm{CO}$
- (3) $\mathrm{N_2}$, $\mathrm{O_2^-}$
- (4) $\mathrm{CO}$, $\mathrm{NO}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{CN^{(-)}}$ and $\mathrm{CO}$ have bond order $3$ each.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Bond order $= \frac{1}{2}(N_b - N_a)$ from MO theory; species with the same total electron count (isoelectronic) have the same bond order.
2. $\mathrm{O_2}$ has $16$ electrons, so $BO = \frac{10-6}{2} = 2$; $\mathrm{NO^+}$ has $7+8-1 = 14$ electrons, so $BO = \frac{10-4}{2} = 3$. Not equal.
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ has $6+7+1 = 14$ electrons, so $BO = 3$; $\mathrm{CO}$ has $6+8 = 14$ electrons, so $BO = 3$. Equal.
4. $\mathrm{N_2}$ has $14$ electrons, $BO = 3$; $\mathrm{O_2^-}$ has $17$ electrons, $BO = \frac{10-7}{2} = 1.5$. Not equal.
5. $\mathrm{CO}$ has $BO = 3$; $\mathrm{NO}$ has $15$ electrons, $BO = \frac{10-5}{2} = 2.5$. Not equal.
6. Only $\mathrm{CN^-}$ and $\mathrm{CO}$ share bond order $3$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Molecular Orbital Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 26](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q026_a77ff09f_29102026.png?v=a77ff09fc50e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 26](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q026_267b8583_29102026.png?v=267b858310ed)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Bond order from molecular-orbital filling: $\mathrm{CN^-}$ and $\mathrm{CO}$ each hold 10 valence electrons and both come out at bond order 3
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 4  
  The series carried in memory as isoelectronic with $\mathrm{N_2}$ is $\mathrm{CO}$, $\mathrm{CN^-}$ and $\mathrm{NO^+}$, and reading this option's $\mathrm{NO}$ as that $\mathrm{NO^+}$ puts both members at bond order 3. Neutral $\mathrm{NO}$ has one electron more, and it sits in an antibonding $\pi^*$ orbital, so its bond order is 2.5.
- **Why:** This question requires molecular-orbital bond orders worked out for eight species and matched within pairs, demonstrated by the stem 'Which one of the following pairs of species have the same bond order?'.
- **Note:** E1's pattern recorded plain_mcq by default; each printed option is a pair accepted or rejected whole, so the shape is two_part. look_alike_term rather than nearby_fact on option 4: swap $\mathrm{NO}$ for an unlike species and the pull goes, because what carries the student is $\mathrm{NO}$ reading as the $\mathrm{NO^+}$ of the isoelectronic list.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q27

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

In which pair of ions both the species contain $\mathrm{S - S}$ bond?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_8^{2-}}$
- (3) $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: tetrathionate ion, $\ce{(S4O6)^{2-}}$ ,](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q027_sol_1_ed8f0b31_29102026.svg?v=ed8f0b31f0e3)

*tetrathionate ion, $\ce{[S4O6]^{2-}}$ ,*

![drawn: tetrathionate ion, $\ce{(S4O6)^{2-}}$ ,](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q027_solution_1_67196810_29102026.svg?v=671968102c09)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

![printed figure: thiosulfate ion, $\ce{(S2O3)^{2-}}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q027_sol_2_e6455117_29102026.svg?v=e64551174c36)

*thiosulfate ion, $\ce{[S2O3]^{2-}}$*

![drawn: thiosulfate ion, $\ce{(S2O3)^{2-}}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q027_solution_2_9dee0081_29102026.svg?v=9dee0081340e)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$ (tetrathionate) has the chain $\mathrm{^-O_3S{-}S{-}S{-}SO_3^-}$, so it contains $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bonds.
2. $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$ (thiosulphate) has a central S bonded to three O and one terminal S, so it contains an $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond.
3. $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$ (pyrosulphate) is $\mathrm{O_3S{-}O{-}SO_3}$ with an $\mathrm{S{-}O{-}S}$ bridge, so it has no $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond.
4. $\mathrm{S_2O_8^{2-}}$ (peroxodisulphate) is $\mathrm{O_3S{-}O{-}O{-}SO_3}$ with an $\mathrm{O{-}O}$ bond, so it has no $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond.
5. Only the pair $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$ has $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ in both species, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Allotropes, Oxyacids and Oxides of Sulphur · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 27](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q027_f932f346_29102026.png?v=f932f3466edc)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 27](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q027_92a5ad34_29102026.png?v=92a5ad341a9f)

#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Among sulphur's oxoanions the direct S-S bond belongs to thiosulphate and the thionates; pyrosulphate is bridged through oxygen and peroxodisulphate through a peroxide link
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  Thiosulphate's S-S bond is the one the textbook draws, so with that member secure the partner is accepted because two sulphurs in one formula read as two sulphurs joined. In pyrosulphate the two sulphurs are held apart by a bridging oxygen, S-O-S.
- **Why:** This question requires which sulphur oxoanions hold a direct S-S bond, demonstrated by the stem 'In which pair of ions both the species contain $\mathrm{S-S}$ bond?'.

## Q28

Chemistry · physical · Ionic Equilibrium

Concentration of the $\mathrm{Ag^{+}}$ ions in a saturated solution of $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$ is $2.2 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. Solubility product of $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$ is

- (1) $2.66 \times 10^{-12}$
- (2) $4.5 \times 10^{-11}$
- **(3)** $5.3 \times 10^{-12}$
- (4) $2.42 \times 10^{-8}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}(s) \rightleftharpoons \underset{2s}{2\,\mathrm{Ag^{+}}(aq)} + \underset{s}{\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}(aq)}$
2. $K_{SP} = [\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]^{2}\,[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$
3. $[\mathrm{Ag^{+}}] = 2.2 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{M}$
4. $\therefore\ [\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}] = \frac{2.2 \times 10^{-4}}{2}\,\mathrm{M} = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{M}$
5. $\therefore\ K_{SP} = (2.2 \times 10^{-4})^{2}\,(1.1 \times 10^{-4})$
6. $= 5.324 \times 10^{-12}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Dissociation: $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4 \rightleftharpoons 2Ag^{+} + C_2O_4^{2-}}$, so $[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}] = \frac{[\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]}{2}$.
2. $[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}] = \frac{2.2 \times 10^{-4}}{2} = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$.
3. $K_{sp} = [\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]^2[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$.
4. $[\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]^2 = (2.2 \times 10^{-4})^2 = 4.84 \times 10^{-8}$.
5. $K_{sp} = 4.84 \times 10^{-8} \times 1.1 \times 10^{-4} = 5.324 \times 10^{-12} \approx 5.3 \times 10^{-12}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Solubility Product Constant · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 28](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q028_d244e084_29102026.png?v=d244e084fd65)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 28](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q028_679d02b7_29102026.png?v=679d02b7ac2b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** For $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$, $K_{sp} = [\mathrm{Ag^+}]^2[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$, and the oxalate concentration is half the silver one
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  Putting the square on the oxalate instead of the silver ion gives $2.2\times10^{-4} \times (1.1\times10^{-4})^2 = 2.66\times10^{-12}$, which lands within a factor of two of the key and at the same order of magnitude. The subscript 2 in $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$ sits on the silver, so the silver term is the one that is squared.
- **Why:** This question requires the solubility product assembled with the right exponents, demonstrated by the stem giving the 'Concentration of the $\mathrm{Ag^+}$ ions ... $2.2 \times 10^{-4}\ \mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$' for the salt $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$.
- **Note:** The route to option 2 computes $4.3\times10^{-11}$ against the $4.5\times10^{-11}$ printed; recorded because the arithmetic is otherwise exact and no other option sits within a factor of two of that value.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q29

Chemistry · inorganic · The s-Block Elements

Ionic mobility of which of the following alkali metal ions is lowest when aqueous solution of their salts are put under an electric field?

- (1) $\mathrm{K}$
- (2) $\mathrm{Rb}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{Li}$
- (4) $\mathrm{Na}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Li^{+}}$ being smallest, has maximum charge density
2. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Li^{+}}$ is most heavily hydrated among all alkali metal ions.
3. Effective size of $\mathrm{Li^{+}}$ in aq solution is therefore, largest.
4. $\therefore$ Moves slowest under electric field.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In aqueous solution, the smaller the bare ion, the higher its charge density and the more water molecules it binds: hydration order $\mathrm{Li^+} > \mathrm{Na^+} > \mathrm{K^+} > \mathrm{Rb^+}$.
2. So the hydrated radius follows $r_{\text{hyd}}(\mathrm{Li^+}) > r_{\text{hyd}}(\mathrm{Na^+}) > r_{\text{hyd}}(\mathrm{K^+}) > r_{\text{hyd}}(\mathrm{Rb^+})$.
3. A larger hydrated ion moves more slowly in an electric field, so mobility order is $\mathrm{Li^+} < \mathrm{Na^+} < \mathrm{K^+} < \mathrm{Rb^+}$; lowest is $\mathrm{Li^+}$, option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Alkali Metals: Properties and Trends · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 29](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q029_d14a9876_29102026.png?v=d14a987630d0)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 29](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q029_3fae38d4_29102026.png?v=3fae38d44016)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** In water the smallest bare alkali metal ion is the most hydrated, so hydrated $\mathrm{Li^+}$ is the largest and drifts slowest in a field
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  Larger ion, slower drift is the right rule for bare ions, and among K, Rb, Li and Na the largest is $\mathrm{Rb^+}$. The stem places the ions in 'aqueous solution', where each moves wrapped in a hydration shell and the size order is turned round.
- **Why:** This question requires the hydration correction applied to the group size trend, demonstrated by the stem's condition 'when aqueous solution of their salts are put under an electric field'.
- **Note:** The options are printed as the bare element symbols K, Rb, Li, Na while the stem asks about the ions; tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q30

Chemistry · physical · Thermodynamics

A gas is allowed to expand in a well insulated container against a constant external pressure of $2.5\,\mathrm{atm}$ from an initial volume of $2.50\,\mathrm{L}$ to a final volume of $4.50\,\mathrm{L}$. The change in internal energy $\Delta U$ of the gas in joules will be

- (1) $-500\,\mathrm{J}$
- **(2)** $-505\,\mathrm{J}$
- (3) $+505\,\mathrm{J}$
- (4) $1136.25\,\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\Delta U = q + w$
2. For adiabatic process, $q = 0$
3. $\therefore\ \Delta U = w$
4. $= -P\cdot\Delta V$
5. $= -2.5\,\mathrm{atm} \times (4.5 - 2.5)\,\mathrm{L}$
6. $= -2.5 \times 2\,\mathrm{L\text{-}atm}$
7. $= -5 \times 101.3\,\mathrm{J}$
8. $= -506.5\,\mathrm{J}$
9. $\approx -505\,\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The container is well insulated, so no heat is exchanged: $q = 0$.
2. Work against constant external pressure: $w = -p_{ext}\,\Delta V$.
3. $\Delta V = 4.50\,\mathrm{L} - 2.50\,\mathrm{L} = 2.00\,\mathrm{L}$.
4. $w = -2.5\,\mathrm{atm} \times 2.00\,\mathrm{L} = -5.0\,\mathrm{L\,atm}$.
5. First law: $\Delta U = q + w = 0 + w = -5.0\,\mathrm{L\,atm}$.
6. Convert: $-5.0\,\mathrm{L\,atm} \times 101\,\mathrm{J\,L^{-1}\,atm^{-1}} \approx -505\,\mathrm{J}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Internal Energy and First Law of Thermodynamics · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 30](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q030_05dd6125_29102026.png?v=05dd61255634)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 30](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q030_416c0147_29102026.png?v=416c0147b7fd)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In an insulated container $q = 0$, so $\Delta U$ equals the work alone, $-p_{ext}\Delta V$, and an expansion makes it negative
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  The magnitude comes from the right route -- $2.5\ \mathrm{atm} \times 2.00\ \mathrm{L} = 5.0\ \mathrm{L\,atm} \approx 505\ \mathrm{J}$ -- and only the sign is left to settle, so taking the gas's expansion work as a gain prints it positive. The gas pushes the surroundings back with no heat entering, so its internal energy falls.
- **Why:** This question requires the first law applied with no heat exchange, demonstrated by the stem's 'well insulated container' expanding 'against a constant external pressure of $2.5\ \mathrm{atm}$' from 2.50 L to 4.50 L.
- **Note:** The printed key $-505\ \mathrm{J}$ follows from taking $1\ \mathrm{L\,atm}$ as about 101 J; the exact conversion gives $-506.5\ \mathrm{J}$. Tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q31

Chemistry · inorganic · The D- and F-Block Elements – Practice

The reason for greater range of oxidation states in actinoids is attributed to

- (1) Actinoid contraction
- **(2)** $5f$, $6d$ and $7s$ levels having comparable energies
- (3) $4f$ and $5d$ levels being close in energies
- (4) The radioactive nature of actinoids

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. It is a fact.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In actinoids the $5f$, $6d$ and $7s$ subshells lie close in energy, so a variable number of electrons from all three can be lost or shared, which gives a greater range of oxidation states; this is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Lanthanoids & Actinoids · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

- **Concept:** The actinoids show many oxidation states because their 5f, 6d and 7s levels lie close enough in energy for all three to give up electrons

## Q32

Chemistry · organic · Basic Principles and Techniques of Organic Compounds

The most suitable method of separation of $1:1$ mixture of ortho and para-nitrophenols is

- (1) Chromatography
- (2) Crystallisation
- **(3)** Steam distillation
- (4) Sublimation

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Steam distillation is the most suitable method of separation of $1:1$ mixture of ortho and para nitrophenols as there is intramolecular H-bonds in ortho nitrophenol.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In $o$-nitrophenol the $-OH$ and $-NO_2$ groups are adjacent, so it forms an intramolecular hydrogen bond and is not associated with neighbouring molecules, which makes it volatile in steam.
2. In $p$-nitrophenol the groups are far apart, so it forms intermolecular hydrogen bonds, becomes associated and is not steam volatile.
3. One component is steam volatile and the other is not, so steam distillation separates the $1:1$ mixture, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Methods of Purification of Organic Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 32](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q032_91dd5ded_29102026.png?v=91dd5ded5a44)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 32](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q032_a119e705_29102026.png?v=a119e70569ba)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Experiment** (`experiment`) — a procedure, its setup, or what its data show
- **Concept:** Steam distillation lifts out the component that is volatile with steam: ortho-nitrophenol's intramolecular hydrogen bond frees it to distil, para-nitrophenol's intermolecular bonding holds it back
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 2  
  Both nitrophenols are solids, and crystallisation is the technique NCERT introduces for purifying solids by a difference in solubility, so a student working down the same list of purification methods stops at it; these two isomers are close in solubility and far apart in steam volatility.
- **Why:** This question requires naming the bench technique that suits a specified mixture, demonstrated by "The most suitable method of separation of 1:1 mixture of ortho and para-nitrophenols". On the trap axis the swap test was applied: crystallisation does not resemble steam distillation in name, and the pull survives only because both are printed in the same list of purification methods, so it is nearby_fact rather than look_alike_term.
- **Note:** Options 1 and 2 are both genuine separation techniques, so the key rests on the stem's "most suitable" rather than on one option being impossible; tagged as printed.

## Q33

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The correct order of the stoichiometries of $\mathrm{AgCl}$ formed when $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$ in excess is treated with the complexes : $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 6NH_3}$, $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 5NH_3}$, $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 4NH_3}$ respectively is

- (1) 3 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 1 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 2 $\mathrm{AgCl}$
- **(2)** 3 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 2 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 1 $\mathrm{AgCl}$
- (3) 2 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 3 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 1 $\mathrm{AgCl}$
- (4) 1 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 3 $\mathrm{AgCl}$, 2 $\mathrm{AgCl}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Complexes are respectively $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]Cl_3}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_5Cl]Cl_2}$ and $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2]Cl}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Co(III) has coordination number $6$; only chloride ions outside the coordination sphere are precipitated by excess $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$ as $\mathrm{AgCl}$.
2. $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 6NH_3} = [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_6}]\mathrm{Cl_3}$, which has $3$ ionisable $\mathrm{Cl^-}$, giving $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$.
3. $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 5NH_3} = [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_5Cl}]\mathrm{Cl_2}$, which has $2$ ionisable $\mathrm{Cl^-}$, giving $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$.
4. $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 4NH_3} = [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2}]\mathrm{Cl}$, which has $1$ ionisable $\mathrm{Cl^-}$, giving $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$.
5. The order $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}, 2\,\mathrm{AgCl}, 1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$ matches option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Werner’s Theory and Applications of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 33](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q033_2a79c1b5_29102026.png?v=2a79c1b5f910)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 33](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q033_ae5e10d4_29102026.png?v=ae5e10d47a9a)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** Only the chloride outside the coordination sphere is ionisable and precipitates with silver nitrate; ammonia fills the six coordination sites first, so 6NH3, 5NH3 and 4NH3 leave 3, 2 and 1 free chlorides
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 1  
  Counting the chloride written inside the bracket instead of outside it gives 1 for $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_5Cl]Cl_2}$ and 2 for $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2]Cl}$, which is option 1's tail, while the hexammine keeps the securely remembered 3.
- **Why:** This question requires a sequence of three computed counts, demonstrated by the stem's "correct order of the stoichiometries of AgCl formed … respectively" and options that are triples of AgCl amounts.
- **Note:** The order lives on the asks axis; the printed shape is still four plain answers, so qtype is mcq even though the pattern classifier called it arrange_in_order.

## Q34

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

Correct increasing order for the wavelengths of absorption in the visible region for the complexes of $\mathrm{Co^{3+}}$ is

- (1) $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$
- (3) $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. The order of the ligand in the spectrochemical series
2. $\mathrm{H_2O} < \mathrm{NH_3} < \mathrm{en}$
3. Hence, the wavelength of the light observed will be in the order
4. $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}} < \mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}} < \mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$
5. Thus, wavelength absorbed will be in the opposite order
6. i.e., $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Spectrochemical series for these ligands: $\mathrm{en} > \mathrm{NH_3} > \mathrm{H_2O}$ in field strength.
2. So the crystal field splitting follows $\Delta_o[\mathrm{Co(en)_3}]^{3+} > \Delta_o[\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_6}]^{3+} > \Delta_o[\mathrm{Co(H_2O)_6}]^{3+}$.
3. Absorbed wavelength is $\lambda = \frac{hc}{\Delta_o}$, so a larger $\Delta_o$ gives a shorter $\lambda$.
4. Increasing $\lambda$: $[\mathrm{Co(en)_3}]^{3+} < [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_6}]^{3+} < [\mathrm{Co(H_2O)_6}]^{3+}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Crystal Field Theory and Its Limitations, Colour in Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 34](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q034_567c2e56_29102026.png?v=567c2e5661f6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 34](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q034_827bcc5f_29102026.png?v=827bcc5ffb1a)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** Field strength and absorbed wavelength run opposite ways: the stronger the ligand field the larger the splitting and the shorter the wavelength absorbed, so the order of increasing wavelength is the spectrochemical series backwards
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  Writing the three ligands in spectrochemical order, $\mathrm{H_2O < NH_3 < en}$, and reading that straight off as increasing wavelength inverts the relation, because a larger splitting absorbs the shorter wavelength.
- **Why:** This question requires a sequence of three complexes ranked on one quantity, demonstrated by "Correct increasing order for the wavelengths of absorption" with ordered triples as options.

## Q35

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

Which one is the most acidic compound?

- (1) phenol `Oc1ccccc1` ![phenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q035_option_1_s1_4778923b_29102026.svg?v=4778923bbceb)
- (2) 4-nitrophenol `O=[N+]([O-])c1ccc(O)cc1` ![4-nitrophenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q035_option_2_s1_315832f8_29102026.svg?v=315832f878be)
- **(3)** 2,4,6-trinitrophenol `O=[N+]([O-])c1cc([N+](=O)[O-])c(O)c([N+](=O)[O-])c1` ![2,4,6-trinitrophenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q035_option_3_s1_7a1d355a_29102026.svg?v=7a1d355ad080)
- (4) 4-methylphenol `Cc1ccc(O)cc1` ![4-methylphenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q035_option_4_s1_36957d6d_29102026.svg?v=36957d6d42ec)

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ group has very strong $-I$ & $-R$ effects.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Phenol acidity depends on how well the phenoxide ion $\mathrm{C_6H_5O^-}$ is stabilised; electron-withdrawing groups raise acidity and electron-donating groups lower it.
2. Option $4$ has $-\mathrm{CH_3}$ at the para position, an electron-donating group ($+I$, hyperconjugation), so it is less acidic than phenol (option $1$).
3. Option $2$ has one $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ at the para position ($-I$, $-R$), which delocalises the negative charge of the phenoxide, so it is more acidic than phenol.
4. Option $3$ has three $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ groups at the $2,4,6$ (ortho and para) positions, giving the greatest stabilisation of the phenoxide ion.
5. Order: $3 > 2 > 1 > 4$, so the most acidic compound is 2,4,6-trinitrophenol, option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Acidity of Phenols · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 35](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q035_8f6d7f2a_29102026.png?v=8f6d7f2a20b1)

## Q36

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

Which of the following pairs of compounds is isoelectronic and isostructural?

- (1) $\mathrm{Tel_2}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
- (3) $\mathrm{IF_3}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
- (4) $\mathrm{BeCl_2}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
2. Total number of valence electrons are equal in both the species and both the species are linear also.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{XeF_2}$: valence electrons $= 8 + 2(7) = 22$; Xe has 2 bond pairs and 3 lone pairs, $sp^3d$, so the shape is linear.
2. $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$: valence electrons $= 7 + 2(7) + 1 = 22$; I has 2 bond pairs and 3 lone pairs, $sp^3d$, so the shape is linear.
3. $\mathrm{TeI_2}$: valence electrons $= 6 + 2(7) = 20$; Te has 2 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs, so it is bent, not like $\mathrm{XeF_2}$.
4. $\mathrm{IF_3}$: valence electrons $= 7 + 3(7) = 28$; I has 3 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs, so it is T-shaped, not like $\mathrm{XeF_2}$.
5. $\mathrm{BeCl_2}$: valence electrons $= 2 + 2(7) = 16$; linear but not isoelectronic with $\mathrm{XeF_2}$.
6. Only $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$ and $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ are both isoelectronic (22 valence electrons) and isostructural (linear), which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 36](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q036_72ffbd3e_29102026.png?v=72ffbd3edaca)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 36](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q036_c5889170_29102026.png?v=c5889170669d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Isoelectronic means the same count of valence electrons; isostructural means the same VSEPR shape — $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ is a 22-electron linear AB2 with three lone pairs, and only $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$ matches on both counts
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  $\mathrm{BeCl_2}$ really is linear, so a student who checks the shape and stops has an option that satisfies one of the stem's two demands; its 16 valence electrons against $\mathrm{XeF_2}$'s 22 fail the other.
- **Why:** This question requires one rule applied to four new species, demonstrated by the stem's demand that a pair be "isoelectronic and isostructural", which is settled by counting valence electrons and assigning a VSEPR shape rather than by recall of any printed line.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier returned plain_mcq, but each option is a pair of species accepted or rejected whole and both printed criteria must hold for it, which is the retired pair_options shape; the nearest current qtype is two_part, recorded here. Option 1 is printed as "Tel_2" in our record and read as $\mathrm{TeI_2}$.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q37

Chemistry · organic · Amines

Which of the following reactions is appropriate for converting acetamide to methanamine?

- **(1)** Hoffmann hypobromamide reaction
- (2) Stephens reaction
- (3) Gabriels phthalimide synthesis
- (4) Carbylamine reaction

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{CH_3-\overset{\overset{O}{\|}}{C}-NH_2 + Br_2 + 4NaOH \xrightarrow{\Delta} CH_3-NH_2 + 2NaBr + Na_2CO_3 + 3H_2O}$
2. This is Hoffmann Bromamide reaction.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Acetamide is $\mathrm{CH_3CONH_2}$ (2 C) and methanamine is $\mathrm{CH_3NH_2}$ (1 C), so the conversion removes one carbon from a primary amide.
2. Hoffmann bromamide degradation turns a primary amide into a primary amine with one carbon less: $\mathrm{CH_3CONH_2 + Br_2 + 4NaOH \rightarrow CH_3NH_2 + Na_2CO_3 + 2NaBr + 2H_2O}$.
3. Stephens reaction turns nitriles into aldehydes, Gabriel synthesis makes amines from alkyl halides via phthalimide, and the carbylamine reaction turns amines into isocyanides, so none of these fits; the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Methods of Preparation of Amines · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 37](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q037_7c5b4dd9_29102026.png?v=7c5b4dd91e6e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 37](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q037_b7017c9e_29102026.png?v=b7017c9e04d3)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Hoffmann bromamide degradation is the one named route that takes an amide to a primary amine with one carbon fewer, so acetamide gives methanamine
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 3  
  Gabriel phthalimide synthesis genuinely does make primary amines, methanamine included, so a student who matches on the product has a true statement in hand; its substrate is an alkyl halide, and the stem starts from acetamide.
- **Why:** This question requires the named reaction that effects a printed conversion, demonstrated by "appropriate for converting acetamide to methanamine" with four named reactions as options.
- **Note:** Option 1 is printed as "Hoffmann hypobromamide reaction" in our record; tagged as printed and read as the Hoffmann bromamide (hypobromite) degradation named by the key.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q38

Chemistry · organic · Aldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids

Of the following, which is the product formed when cyclohexanone undergoes aldol condensation followed by heating?

- **(1)** 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one `O=C1CCCCC1=C1CCCCC1` ![2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q038_option_1_s1_92674f6a_29102026.svg?v=92674f6aaea1)
- (2) 2-cyclohexylcyclohexan-1-ol `OC1CCCCC1C1CCCCC1` ![2-cyclohexylcyclohexan-1-ol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q038_option_2_s1_947f9300_29102026.svg?v=947f93008fab)
- (3) 2-(2-oxocyclohexylidene)cyclohexan-1-one `O=C1CCCCC1=C1CCCCC1=O` ![2-(2-oxocyclohexylidene)cyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q038_option_3_s1_65b387a1_29102026.svg?v=65b387a12dd8)
- (4) 2-(2-hydroxycyclohexyl)cyclohexan-1-one `O=C1CCCCC1C1CCCCC1O` ![2-(2-hydroxycyclohexyl)cyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q038_option_4_s1_7a577642_29102026.svg?v=7a577642b73a)

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: cyclohexanone + cyclohexanone →((i) $\ce{OH-}$ (ii) Δ) 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q038_sol_1_7886760b_29102026.svg?v=7886760be847)

*cyclohexanone + cyclohexanone →[(i) $\ce{OH-}$ (ii) Δ] 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one*

![drawn: cyclohexanone + cyclohexanone →((i) $\ce{OH-}$ (ii) Δ) 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q038_solution_1_0394d2e9_29102026.svg?v=0394d2e93a90)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The base removes an $\alpha$-H of cyclohexanone to give the enolate (carbanion at $C_2$).
2. The enolate attacks the carbonyl carbon of a second cyclohexanone, giving the aldol $2\text{-}(1\text{-hydroxycyclohexyl})\text{cyclohexan-1-one}$, where the $-OH$ sits on the ring carbon joined to $C_2$.
3. On heating, the $\beta$-hydroxy ketone loses $H_2O$ to form a $C=C$ conjugated with $C=O$: $2\text{-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one}$.
4. Only one $C=O$ remains, with an exocyclic $C=C$ joining the two rings, which matches option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Reactions: Reactions Due to Alpha Hydrogen · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 38](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q038_f0083f86_29102026.png?v=f0083f861bb3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 38](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q038_3f6be997_29102026.png?v=3f6be997c112)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Self-aldol of cyclohexanone followed by heating loses water to give the conjugated cross-ring enone, so the product is the dehydrated aldol and not the beta-hydroxy ketone
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  All four options are empty strings in this record; the products were printed as structures and did not survive extraction, so there is no option against which a route could be checked.
- **Why:** This question requires the product of a named transformation under a stated condition, demonstrated by "cyclohexanone undergoes aldol condensation followed by heating" — but the four product structures are missing from the record, so the comparison between them cannot be read.
- **Note:** Source damage, not a property of the question: options 1-4 are all empty and has_figure is false, so the drawn product structures were lost. A reviewer holding the printed paper should check whether the non-dehydrated aldol is offered beside the enone, which is the device the stem's "followed by heating" sets up. qtype and asks are read from the stem alone.

## Q39

Chemistry · organic · Biomolecules

Which of the following statements is not correct?

- (1) Ovalbumin is a simple food reserve in egg-white
- (2) Blood proteins thrombin and fibrinogen are involved in blood clotting
- **(3)** Denaturation makes the proteins more active
- (4) Insulin maintains sugar level in the blood of a human body

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Due to denaturation of proteins, globules unfold and helix get uncoiled and protein loses its biological activity.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Denaturation disrupts the secondary and tertiary structure of a protein while the primary structure stays intact, so the protein loses its biological activity; statement $3$ (denaturation makes proteins more active) is false, while ovalbumin as egg-white reserve, thrombin and fibrinogen in clotting, and insulin regulating blood sugar are all correct, so the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Denaturation of Proteins and Enzymes · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

  Hunting for the incorrect statement, a student who holds the clotting proteins and insulin as firm textbook lines but cannot place "a simple food reserve in egg-white" ticks the line they cannot vouch for; the line is true and is the answer to "which is correct", not to the stem's "not correct".
- **Why:** This question requires one printed fact about proteins to be graded against the others, demonstrated by the option "Denaturation makes the proteins more active" set against the stem's "not correct".

## Q40

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

Which one is the correct order of acidity?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{CH \equiv CH > CH_3 - C \equiv CH > CH_2 = CH_2 > CH_3 - CH_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{CH \equiv CH > CH_2 = CH_2 > CH_3 - C \equiv CH > CH_3 - CH_3}$
- (3) $\mathrm{CH_3 - CH_3 > CH_2 = CH_2 > CH_3 - C \equiv CH > CH \equiv CH}$
- (4) $\mathrm{CH_2 = CH_2 > CH_3 - CH = CH_2 > CH_3 - C \equiv CH > CH \equiv CH}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Correct order is $\underset{\text{(Two acidic hydrogens)}}{\mathrm{H - C \equiv C - H}} > \underset{\text{(One acidic hydrogen)}}{\mathrm{H_3C - C \equiv C - H}} > \mathrm{H_2C = CH_2} > \mathrm{CH_3 - CH_3}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The acidity of a $\mathrm{C-H}$ bond increases with the s-character of the carbon: $sp\,(50\%) > sp^2\,(33\%) > sp^3\,(25\%)$.
2. So the alkynes are more acidic than $\mathrm{CH_2=CH_2}$, which is more acidic than $\mathrm{CH_3-CH_3}$.
3. In $\mathrm{CH_3-C\equiv CH}$ the $+I$ effect of $\mathrm{CH_3}$ destabilises the conjugate base, so it is less acidic than $\mathrm{CH\equiv CH}$.
4. Order: $\mathrm{CH\equiv CH > CH_3-C\equiv CH > CH_2=CH_2 > CH_3-CH_3}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Properties of Alkynes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 40](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q040_b9dc75ac_29102026.png?v=b9dc75ac657b)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 40](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q040_da156d1e_29102026.png?v=da156d1eac87)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** Acidity of a C-H bond tracks the s-character of the carbon holding the hydrogen: sp acetylene beats sp propyne, which beats sp2 ethene, which beats sp3 ethane
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  Option 3 is the series exactly as the chapter builds it up — ethane, then ethene, then propyne, then acetylene — which is the order of INCREASING acidity, and the stem prints no direction word, only "the correct order of acidity".
- **Why:** This question requires four compounds ranked on one property, demonstrated by "Which one is the correct order of acidity?" with each option an ordered chain of inequalities.
- **Note:** The stem carries no direction word, which is what makes the exactly reversed option reachable; task polarity as printed is a bare "order".

## Q41

Chemistry · inorganic · General Principles and Processes of Isolation of Elements

Extraction of gold and silver involves leaching with $\mathrm{CN^{-}}$ ion. Silver is later recovered by

- (1) Distillation
- (2) Zone refining
- **(3)** Displacement with $\mathrm{Zn}$
- (4) Liquation

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Zn}$ being more reactive than $\mathrm{Ag}$ and $\mathrm{Au}$, displaces them.
2. From Native ore,
3. $\mathrm{4Ag + 8NaCN + 2H_2O + O_2} \xrightarrow{\text{Leaching}} \underset{\substack{\text{Soluble}\\ \text{Sodium dicyanoargentate(I)}}}{\mathrm{4Na[Ag(CN)_2]}} + \mathrm{4NaOH}$
4. $\mathrm{2Na[Ag(CN)_2] + Zn} \xrightarrow{\text{Displacement}} \mathrm{Na_2[Zn(CN)_4] + 2Ag}\downarrow$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Leaching with $\mathrm{CN^-}$ in the presence of air dissolves silver as the soluble complex $\mathrm{[Ag(CN)_2]^-}$.
2. $\mathrm{Zn}$ is more electropositive than $\mathrm{Ag}$, so it reduces the complex: $\mathrm{2[Ag(CN)_2]^- + Zn \rightarrow [Zn(CN)_4]^{2-} + 2Ag}$.
3. Silver is therefore recovered by displacement with $\mathrm{Zn}$, which is option $3$; distillation, zone refining and liquation are refining methods, not recovery from the cyanide solution.

**Subtopic:** Concentration of Ores · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 41](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q041_d22d0165_29102026.png?v=d22d016522f1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 41](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q041_ab8ff4d8_29102026.png?v=ab8ff4d8c2e5)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** After cyanide leaching, silver sits in solution as the dicyanoargentate complex and is thrown out by a more electropositive metal, zinc, in a displacement step
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 1  
  The leach liquor is a solution, and distillation is the method a student reaches for to get a solid back out of a liquid; in metallurgy distillation is a refining step for a metal that boils low, not a way to break up a dissolved complex.
- **Why:** This question requires the step the book states for recovering silver after cyanide leaching, demonstrated by the stem "Silver is later recovered by" with named metallurgical methods as options. On the trap axis the swap test was applied: "distillation" does not resemble "displacement with Zn" in name, and the pull survives only because both are methods printed in the same metallurgy chapter, so it is nearby_fact rather than look_alike_term.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q42

Chemistry · organic · General Organic Chemistry

The correct statement regarding electrophile is

- (1) Electrophile is a negatively charged species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from another electrophile
- (2) Electrophiles are generally neutral species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from a nucleophile
- **(3)** Electrophile can be either neutral or positively charged species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from a nucleophile
- (4) Electrophile is a negatively charged species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from a nucleophile

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Fact.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. An electrophile is an electron-seeking (electron-deficient) species that may be neutral (e.g. $\mathrm{BF_3}$, $\mathrm{AlCl_3}$, $>\mathrm{C{=}O}$ carbon) or positively charged (e.g. $\mathrm{H^+}$, $\mathrm{NO_2^+}$), and it forms a bond by accepting an electron pair from a nucleophile, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Electrophile and Nucleophile · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 42](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q042_f102e0be_29102026.png?v=f102e0beb33c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 42](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q042_dc88c013_29102026.png?v=dc88c0139888)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** An electrophile is an electron-pair acceptor, so it may be positively charged or neutral but never negative, and the pair it takes comes from a nucleophile
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  The second clause of option 2 is exactly right, and its first clause matches the electrophiles a student meets most often — $\mathrm{BF_3}$, $\mathrm{AlCl_3}$, a carbonyl carbon — so "generally neutral" reads as a fair description; it still leaves out the cationic electrophiles the definition has to cover.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated definition of a term, demonstrated by the stem "The correct statement regarding electrophile is" with four rival definitions as options.

## Q43

Chemistry · organic · Aldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids

Consider the reactions :
Identify A, X, Y and Z

![printed figure: X ($\ce{C2H6O}$) →(Cu / 573 K) A; A →($\ce{(Ag(NH3)2)+}$, ⁻OH, Δ) Silver mirror observed; A →(⁻OH, Δ) Y; A + hydrazinecarboxamide (semicarbazide) → Z](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q043_b6b8bb61_29102026.svg?v=b6b8bb613a8b)

*X ($\ce{C2H6O}$) →[Cu / 573 K] A; A →[$\ce{[Ag(NH3)2]+}$, ⁻OH, Δ] Silver mirror observed; A →[⁻OH, Δ] Y; A + hydrazinecarboxamide (semicarbazide) → Z*

Structures: hydrazinecarboxamide `NNC(N)=O`

- (1) A-Methoxymethane, X-Ethanol, Y-Ethanoic acid, Z-Semicarbazide
- **(2)** A-Ethanal, X-Ethanol, Y-But-2-enal, Z-Semicarbazone
- (3) A-Ethanol, X-Acetaldehyde, Y-Butanone, Z-Hydrazone
- (4) A-Methoxymethane, X-Ethanoic acid, Y-Acetate ion, Z-hydrazine

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Since 'A' gives positive silver mirror test therefore, it must be an aldehyde or $\alpha$-Hydroxyketone.
2. Reaction with semicarbazide indicates that A can be an aldehyde or ketone.
3. Reaction with $\mathrm{OH^{-}}$ i.e., aldol condensation (by assuming alkali to be dilute) indicates that A is aldehyde as aldol reaction of ketones is reversible and carried out in special apparatus.
4. These indicates option (2).

![printed figure: ethanol (X) →(Cu, 573 K) ethanal (A) →($\ce{(Ag(NH3)2)+}$, $\ce{OH-}$, Δ) ethanoic acid; ethanal (A) + hydrazinecarboxamide → 2-ethylidenehydrazine-1-carboxamide (Z); ethanal (A) →($\ce{OH-}$) 3-hydroxybutanal →(Δ) but-2-enal (Y)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q043_sol_1_1cabbacf_29102026.svg?v=1cabbacf4de3)

*ethanol (X) →[Cu, 573 K] ethanal (A) →[$\ce{[Ag(NH3)2]+}$, $\ce{OH-}$, Δ] ethanoic acid; ethanal (A) + hydrazinecarboxamide → 2-ethylidenehydrazine-1-carboxamide (Z); ethanal (A) →[$\ce{OH-}$] 3-hydroxybutanal →[Δ] but-2-enal (Y)*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $X$ has formula $\mathrm{C_2H_6O}$ and undergoes Cu/573 K dehydrogenation, so $X$ is ethanol, $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH}$.
2. $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH \xrightarrow{Cu,\,573\,K} CH_3CHO + H_2}$, so $A$ is ethanal.
3. $A$ gives a silver mirror with $[\mathrm{Ag(NH_3)_2}]^+$, confirming $A$ is an aldehyde (ethanal).
4. With $\mathrm{OH^-}$ and heat, ethanal undergoes aldol condensation: $\mathrm{2CH_3CHO \xrightarrow{OH^-,\,\Delta} CH_3CH{=}CHCHO + H_2O}$, so $Y$ is but-2-enal.
5. With semicarbazide: $\mathrm{CH_3CHO + H_2NNHCONH_2 \rightarrow CH_3CH{=}NNHCONH_2 + H_2O}$, so $Z$ is a semicarbazone.
6. $A$ = ethanal, $X$ = ethanol, $Y$ = but-2-enal, $Z$ = semicarbazone, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Reactions: Reactions Due to Alpha Hydrogen, Preparation of Aldehydes and Ketones · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 43](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q043_b1706cd3_29102026.png?v=b1706cd33e09)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 43](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q043_41bfd933_29102026.png?v=41bfd9339822)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Copper at 573 K dehydrogenates ethanol to ethanal; the silver mirror confirms the aldehyde, warm alkali aldolises and dehydrates it to but-2-enal, and semicarbazide condenses with it to the semicarbazone
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  Option 3 holds the right two species for the first step and attaches them to the wrong labels — ethanol as the product A and acetaldehyde as the starting material X — which is the scheme's arrow read backwards, helped by the options listing A before X while the scheme runs X to A.
- **Why:** This question requires four unknowns read off what each printed reagent does, demonstrated by the scheme's "X ($\mathrm{C_2H_6O}$) -> Cu / 573 K -> A" and the Tollens' step marked "Silver mirror observed".
- **Note:** The pattern classifier returned statement_options, which the printed shape contradicts: a reaction scheme stands in the stem and each option bundles four name assignments accepted or rejected whole, so qtype is recorded as two_part (the retired multi_part shape). The scheme itself came through in the figure record and is legible; only the RDKit redraw of the semicarbazide structures carries status failed_check, and no option turns on that drawing, so the question is ratable.

## Q44

Chemistry · organic · IUPAC Nomenclature of Organic Chemistry

The IUPAC name of the compound [figure] is ________.

![printed figure: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal (drawn as H–C(=O)–CH(CH3)–C(=O)–CH=CH–CH3)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q044_f3d3733e_29102026.svg?v=f3d3733e5958)

*(4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal (drawn as H–C(=O)–CH(CH3)–C(=O)–CH=CH–CH3)*

![drawn: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal (drawn as H–C(=O)–CH(CH3)–C(=O)–CH=CH–CH3)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q044_question_1142f130_29102026.svg?v=1142f1304db9)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

Structures: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal `C/C=C/C(=O)C(C)C=O` ![(4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q044_question_s1_1881e0a0_29102026.svg?v=1881e0a02fad)

- (1) 5-formylhex-2-en-3-one
- (2) 5-methyl-4-oxohex-2-en-5-al
- (3) 3-keto-2-methylhex-5-enal
- **(4)** 3-keto-2-methylhex-4-enal

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal with carbons numbered as printed: 1 (beside the methyl branch), 2, 3 (ketone C), 4, 5, 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q044_sol_1_241f5bd0_29102026.svg?v=241f5bd021a5)

*(4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal with carbons numbered as printed: 1 (beside the methyl branch), 2, 3 (ketone C), 4, 5, 6*

![drawn: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal with carbons numbered as printed: 1 (beside the methyl branch), 2, 3 (ketone C), 4, 5, 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q044_solution_1_bd48e4d4_29102026.svg?v=bd48e4d4bbbc)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

1. Aldehydes get higher priority over ketone and alkene in numbering of principal C-chain.
2. $\therefore$ 3-keto-2-methylhex-4-enal

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure the compound is $\mathrm{OHC{-}CH(CH_3){-}CO{-}CH{=}CH{-}CH_3}$.
2. The aldehyde is the principal group, so the longest chain containing it has $6$ carbons and $\mathrm{CHO}$ is $C_1$.
3. Then $C_2$ carries the methyl, $C_3$ is the ketone ($3$-oxo / $3$-keto) and the double bond is $C_4{=}C_5$.
4. Name: $2$-methyl-$3$-oxohex-$4$-enal, i.e. $3$-keto-$2$-methylhex-$4$-enal, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Nomenclature of Organic Compounds Having Functional Group(s) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 44](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q044_ef8a5c88_29102026.png?v=ef8a5c888af5)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** IUPAC naming when a chain carries both an aldehyde and a ketone: the aldehyde is the principal group and takes C1, and a double bond is cited by the lower of the two carbons it spans
- **Trap:** **Wrong site attacked** (`wrong_site_attacked`) — the reagent is right but it attacks the other site, atom or bond than the one the question turns on — option 3  
  Citing the double bond by the far carbon it reaches, 5 instead of 4, leaves every other part of the name untouched.
- **Why:** This question requires the IUPAC rules applied to a chain carrying an aldehyde, a ketone, a methyl branch and a double bond, demonstrated by the drawn skeleton H–C(=O)–CH(CH3)–C(=O)–CH=CH–CH3 under the stem's 'The IUPAC name of the compound ... is ________.'
- **Note:** The pattern recorded fill_in_blank for the printed blank; the current qtype vocabulary has no value for that overlay, so the shape is recorded as mcq and the naming demand is carried by asks = apply_rule. The printed key keeps the older 'keto' prefix and omits the E descriptor that the drawing supports, but the tag follows the key as printed. The label is wrong_site_attacked rather than look_alike_term: options 3 and 4 differ by one digit, yet what carries the student is which carbon the double bond is cited from, and that miscount survives whatever the two locants happen to be, so position rather than resemblance is doing the work.

## Q45

Chemistry · physical · The Solid State

Which is the incorrect statement?

- (1) Density decreases in case of crystals with Schottky's defect
- (2) $\mathrm{NaCl(s)}$ is insulator, silicon is semiconductor, silver is conductor, quartz is piezo electric crystal
- **(3)** Frenkel defect is favoured in those ionic compounds in which sizes of cation and anions are almost equal
- **(4)** $\mathrm{FeO_{0.98}}$ has non stoichiometric metal deficiency defect

**Answer** — booklet: (3, 4) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Frenkel defect occurs in those ionic compounds in which size of cation and anion is largely different.
2. Non-stoichiometric ferrous oxide is $\mathrm{Fe_{0.93-0.96}O_{1.00}}$ and it is due to metal deficiency defect.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Schottky defect removes equal numbers of cations and anions from the lattice, so mass per unit volume falls: statement $1$ is correct.
2. $\mathrm{NaCl(s)}$ is an insulator, $\mathrm{Si}$ a semiconductor, $\mathrm{Ag}$ a conductor and quartz is piezoelectric: statement $2$ is correct.
3. Frenkel defect occurs in ionic solids with a large difference in ion sizes (small cation, e.g. $\mathrm{AgCl}$, $\mathrm{ZnS}$); almost equal sizes favour Schottky defect, so statement $3$ is incorrect.
4. In $\mathrm{FeO_{0.98}}$ some $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}$ are missing and the charge is balanced by $\mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$, a non-stoichiometric metal deficiency defect: statement $4$ is correct.
5. The incorrect statement is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Imperfections in Solids · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 45](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q045_a8a94358_29102026.png?v=a8a94358ec6c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 45](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q045_b7d31e2a_29102026.png?v=b7d31e2a850d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Point defects in ionic solids: a Schottky defect lowers the density, a Frenkel defect leaves it unchanged and needs a large size difference between the ions
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 1  
  The density facts of the two defects are printed side by side — Frenkel leaves the density alone, Schottky lowers it — so a student holding the pair the other way round reads this true statement as the incorrect one.
- **Why:** This question requires judging four printed statements about imperfections in solids and returning the incorrect one, demonstrated by the stem's 'Which is the incorrect statement?' against option 3's 'sizes of cation and anions are almost equal'.
- **Note:** The printed answer line reads 'Answer (3 & 4)', so options 3 and 4 are both accepted and neither carries a trap or a filler; only options 1 and 2 had to be accounted for, and both are true statements, which is what the negated task turns on. Option 4's acceptance deserves a reviewer's eye, since FeO0.98 having a metal-deficiency defect is ordinarily taught as correct, but the tag follows the key as printed and the key is not re-judged here. The nearby_fact against look_alike_term test was applied to option 1: 'Schottky' and 'Frenkel' do not resemble each other at all, so the resemblance cannot be the pull — what carries the student is the two defects being printed in the same paragraph with their density effects adjacent.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q46

Biology · zoology · Excretory Products and Their Elimination

A decrease in blood pressure/volume will not cause the release of

- **(1)** Atrial Natriuretic Factor
- (2) Aldosterone
- (3) ADH
- (4) Renin

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. A decrease in blood pressure / volume stimulates the release of renin, aldosterone, and ADH while increase in blood pressure / volume stimulates the release of Atrial Natriuretic Factor (ANF) which cause vasodilation and also inhibits RAAS (Renin Angiotensin Aldosterone System) mechanism that decreases the blood volume/pressure.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A fall in blood pressure/volume makes the JG cells release $\text{renin}$, which activates $\text{angiotensin II}$ and so stimulates $\text{aldosterone}$ release from the adrenal cortex.
2. A fall in blood volume is also a stimulus for $\text{ADH}$ release from the neurohypophysis.
3. $\text{ANF}$ is released by atrial walls only when blood pressure/volume rises (atrial distension), so a decrease does not cause its release; this is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Regulation Of Kidney Function · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

![printed question 46](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q046_38513248_29102026.png?v=38513248a9eb)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 46](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q046_352c4343_29102026.png?v=352c4343d42d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** ANF is the counter-hormone of the kidney's pressure circuit: it is secreted when blood volume rises, while renin, aldosterone and ADH all answer a fall.
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  Read as 'which hormone is released when blood pressure falls', the stem is answered by renin, because the fall in glomerular blood pressure releasing renin is the line that uses the stem's own words.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated fact that ANF answers a rise in blood volume while renin, aldosterone and ADH answer a fall, demonstrated by the stem 'A decrease in blood pressure/volume will not cause the release of' set against four hormones of one circuit. The label is condition_missed because no factual error reaches any distractor; only dropping the printed 'not' does, which is the case the rulebook maps from the old true_statement_trap.
- **Note:** All three distractors are flatly true, so the negation is the whole device. Option 4 is named as the landing option because the stem's words 'decrease in blood pressure' and 'release' are the words of the JG-cell line; options 2 and 3 carry the same route and are recorded as traps rather than fillers for that reason.

## Q47

Biology · both · Biomolecules

Which of the following are not polymeric?

- (1) Proteins
- (2) Polysaccharides
- **(3)** Lipids
- (4) Nucleic acids

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. – Nucleic acids are polymers of nucleotides
2. – Proteins are polymers of amino acids
3. – Polysaccharides are polymers of monosaccharides
4. – Lipids are the esters of fatty acids and alcohol

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Proteins (polypeptides of amino acids), polysaccharides (chains of monosaccharides) and nucleic acids (polynucleotides) are true polymers, whereas lipids are not strictly polymeric (they only appear in the acid-insoluble fraction because they form membrane aggregates), so the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Biomacromolecules, Lipids · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

- **Concept:** Proteins, polysaccharides and nucleic acids are built by joining repeating units and so are polymers, while lipids are small molecules that only travel with the macromolecular fraction.

  Read without the 'not', the stem becomes 'which of these are polymeric', and the option whose own name spells out the joined units answers it.

## Q48

Biology · zoology · Digestion and Absorption

A baby boy aged two years is admitted to play school and passes through a dental check-up. The dentist observed that the boy had twenty teeth. Which teeth were absent?

- (1) Canines
- **(2)** Pre-molars
- (3) Molars
- (4) Incisors

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Total number of teeth in human child $= 20$.
2. Premolars are absent in primary dentition.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A two-year-old with $20$ teeth has the full milk (deciduous) set.
2. Milk dental formula per half jaw: $\frac{I\,2,\ C\,1,\ PM\,0,\ M\,2}{I\,2,\ C\,1,\ PM\,0,\ M\,2}$, giving $5$ teeth per quadrant.
3. $5 \times 4 = 20$ teeth: $8$ incisors, $4$ canines, $0$ premolars, $8$ molars.
4. Premolars are therefore absent, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Human Digestive System: Alimentary Canal and Associated Glands · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

![printed question 48](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q048_f9740934_29102026.png?v=f97409343052)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 48](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q048_0b998e0b_29102026.png?v=0b998e0bd6b4)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** The milk dentition is two incisors, one canine and two molars per half jaw - twenty teeth with no premolars at all - while the permanent set adds two premolars per half jaw to make thirty-two.
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 3  
  The late arrival of the permanent molars is a true fact about the neighbouring tooth class, and carried across to the milk set it names molars as the missing teeth.
- **Why:** This question requires the dental formula applied to the milk set, demonstrated by the stem supplying 'the boy had twenty teeth' and asking which class is absent rather than stating it. The trap is nearby_fact and not look_alike_term: swap 'molars' for an unlike word and the pull still stands, because what carries the student is the true fact about late-erupting molars rather than the shared word inside 'pre-molars'.
- **Note:** This is the only member of the group whose answer needs the stem's own number, which is why its asks differs from the rest.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q49

Biology · zoology · Excretory Products and Their Elimination

Which of the following statements is correct?

- (1) The descending limb of loop of Henle is impermeable to water
- (2) The ascending limb of loop of Henle is permeable to water
- (3) The descending limb of loop of Henle is permeable to electrolytes
- **(4)** The ascending limb of loop of Henle is impermeable to water

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Descending limb of loop of Henle is permeable to water but impermeable to electrolytes while ascending limb is impermeable to water but permeable to electrolytes.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The descending limb of the loop of Henle is permeable to water but almost impermeable to electrolytes, so options $1$ and $3$ are false.
2. The ascending limb of the loop of Henle is impermeable to water but allows transport of electrolytes, so option $2$ is false and option $4$ is correct.

**Subtopic:** Function of the Tubules and their types · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

![printed question 49](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q049_fbed1e4d_29102026.png?v=fbed1e4d057e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 49](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q049_67e026d6_29102026.png?v=67e026d6df37)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** The descending limb of the loop of Henle lets water out and holds electrolytes in; the ascending limb does the reverse, and that is what keeps the medullary gradient standing.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  The filtrate leaving the ascending limb is dilute, and reading that dilution as water moving out states the printed line backwards - the wall is impermeable to water and it is the salt that leaves.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated permeabilities of the loop's two limbs, demonstrated by the four options being those two limbs crossed with water and electrolytes, three of them the printed line with one word flipped. qtype is statements because the stem reads 'Which of the following statements is correct?'.
