## Q114

Biology · zoology · Structural Organisation in Animals

Select the correct route for the passage of sperms in male frogs :

- (1) Testes $\rightarrow$ Vasa efferentia $\rightarrow$ Kidney $\rightarrow$ Seminal Vesicle $\rightarrow$ Urinogenital duct $\rightarrow$ Cloaca
- (2) Testes $\rightarrow$ Vasa efferentia $\rightarrow$ Bidder's canal $\rightarrow$ Ureter $\rightarrow$ Cloaca
- **(3)** Testes $\rightarrow$ Vasa efferentia $\rightarrow$ Kidney $\rightarrow$ Bidder's canal $\rightarrow$ Urinogenital duct $\rightarrow$ Cloaca
- (4) Testes $\rightarrow$ Bidder's canal $\rightarrow$ Kidney $\rightarrow$ Vasa efferentia $\rightarrow$ Urinogenital duct $\rightarrow$ Cloaca

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In male frog the sperms will move from Testes $\rightarrow$ Vasa efferentia $\rightarrow$ Kidney $\rightarrow$ Bidder’s canal $\rightarrow$ Urinogenital duct $\rightarrow$ Cloaca.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The male frog's vasa efferentia leave the testes, enter the kidney and open into Bidder's canal; Bidder's canal joins the urinogenital duct, which opens into the cloaca. This order is $\text{Testes} \rightarrow \text{Vasa efferentia} \rightarrow \text{Kidney} \rightarrow \text{Bidder's canal} \rightarrow \text{Urinogenital duct} \rightarrow \text{Cloaca}$, which is option $3$.

## Q115

Biology · zoology · Chemical Coordination and Integration

A temporary endocrine gland in the human body is

- (1) Corpus cardiacum
- **(2)** Corpus luteum
- (3) Corpus allatum
- (4) Pineal gland

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. MALT is Mucosa Associated Lymphoid Tissue and it constitutes about $50$ percent of the lymphoid tissue in human body.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue (MALT), which lines the respiratory, digestive and urogenital tracts, makes up about $50\%$ of the lymphoid tissue in the human body.
2. Options $1$ ($20\%$), $2$ ($70\%$) and $3$ ($10\%$) do not equal $50\%$, so none of them is correct.
3. Option $4$ is blank in the input, so it is presumably $50\%$. That makes option $4$ the best choice.

## Q127

Biology · botany · Respiration in Plants

Which statement is wrong for Krebs' cycle?

- (1) There is one point in the cycle where $\mathrm{FAD^{+}}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{FADH_2}$
- (2) During conversion of succinyl CoA to succinic acid, a molecule of GTP is synthesised
- **(3)** The cycle starts with condensation of acetyl group (acetyl CoA) with pyruvic acid to yield citric acid
- (4) There are three points in the cycle where $\mathrm{NAD^{+}}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{NADH + H^{+}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Krebs cycle starts with condensation of acetyl CoA (2C) with oxaloacetic acid (4C) to form citric acid (6C).

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Krebs' cycle begins with condensation of the acetyl group of acetyl CoA with oxaloacetic acid (OAA, $4C$) and water to form citric acid ($6C$), not with pyruvic acid, so statement $3$ is wrong.
2. In one turn $\mathrm{NAD^{+}}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{NADH + H^{+}}$ at three points (isocitrate, $\alpha$-ketoglutarate, malate oxidations), so statement $4$ is correct.
3. $\mathrm{FAD}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{FADH_2}$ at one point (succinate to fumarate), so statement $1$ is correct.
4. Succinyl CoA to succinic acid is coupled to substrate-level synthesis of one GTP, so statement $2$ is correct; the wrong statement is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Aerobic Respiration And Tricarboxylic Acid Cycle · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 19)

![printed question 127](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q127_167de414_29102026.png?v=167de41426ed)

![printed solution 127](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q127_c1bd6453_29102026.png?v=c1bd6453abd4)

---

#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** One turn of the citric acid cycle starts by condensing acetyl CoA with oxaloacetic acid and runs three NAD+ reductions, one FAD reduction and one GTP-forming step.
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 2  
  Substrate-level phosphorylation is learnt in glycolysis, where the nucleotide formed is ATP, so a student holding that version reads the printed 'GTP' as the planted error and marks this line as the wrong one.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated facts of the citric acid cycle graded one line at a time, demonstrated by the keyed option replacing oxaloacetic acid with 'pyruvic acid' while the other three are the chapter's own sentences. Both trap tests were applied to option 2: GTP and ATP do resemble each other, but swapping the nucleotide for an unlike token leaves the pull intact, because what carries it is the ATP printed at the neighbouring stage, so nearby_fact.

## Q128

Biology · zoology · Chemical Coordination and Integration

Hypersecretion of Growth Hormone in adults does not cause further increase in height, because

- **(1)** Epiphyseal plates close after adolescence
- (2) Bones loose their sensitivity to Growth Hormone in adults
- (3) Muscle fibres do not grow in size after birth
- (4) Growth Hormone becomes inactive in adults

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Epiphyseal plate is responsible for the growth of bone which close after adolescence so hypersecretion of growth hormone in adults does not cause further increase in height.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Increase in height is due to longitudinal growth of long bones at the epiphyseal (growth) plates.
2. After adolescence the epiphyseal plates ossify and close, so long bones can no longer grow in length; adult GH excess therefore causes acromegaly rather than gigantism, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** The Pituitary Gland · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 19)

- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** Growth hormone can add height only while the epiphyseal cartilage plates are still open; once they ossify after adolescence, excess hormone thickens bone instead of lengthening it.
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  Needing a reason the adult skeleton no longer lengthens, a student who knows the same excess gives gigantism in children and acromegaly in adults converts 'the adult bone behaves differently' into 'the adult bone no longer answers the hormone' -- a mechanism the chapter never gives.

The label is formula_misused rather than nearby_fact because the offered mechanism is not true of any neighbouring structure -- it is a wrong causal relation supplied in place of the printed one.

## Q130

Biology · botany · Organisms and Populations

Asymptote in a logistic growth curve is obtained when

- **(1)** $K = N$
- (2) $K > N$
- (3) $K < N$
- (4) The value of '$r$' approaches zero

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. A population growing in a habitat with limited resources shows logistic growth curve.
2. For logistic growth
3. $\frac{dN}{dt} = rN\left(\frac{K - N}{K}\right)$
4. If $K = N$ then $\frac{K - N}{K} = 0$
5. $\therefore$ the $\frac{dN}{dt} = 0$, the population reaches asymptote.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Logistic (Verhulst-Pearl) growth: $\frac{dN}{dt} = rN\left(\frac{K-N}{K}\right)$.
2. When $N = K$, $\frac{K-N}{K} = 0$, so $\frac{dN}{dt} = 0$ and the curve flattens into its asymptote.
3. $r$ is the intrinsic rate of natural increase, a constant; it does not approach zero. So the asymptote occurs at $K = N$, option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Logistic Growth · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 130](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q130_50b7c291_29102026.png?v=50b7c29115e8)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 130](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q130_1d5335f8_29102026.png?v=1d5335f858b8)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** In logistic growth the curve flattens when the population reaches the carrying capacity, so the braking factor $\frac{K-N}{K}$ falls to zero while $r$ stays what it is.
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  Growth stopping is read as the rate constant having fallen away, so the student sets $r$ to zero in $\frac{dN}{dt}=rN\frac{K-N}{K}$ instead of the bracket that actually closes.
- **Why:** This question requires the logistic equation applied to the plateau of its own curve, demonstrated by the stem asking when the 'Asymptote' is obtained and by option 4 naming the equation's other factor, 'The value of r approaches zero'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q131

Biology · zoology · Neural Control and Coordination

Good vision depends on adequate intake of carotene rich food
Select the best option from the following statements

- **((a))** Vitamin A derivatives are formed from carotene
- **((b))** The photopigments are embedded in the membrane discs of the inner segment
- **((c))** Retinal is a derivative of vitamin A
- **((d))** Retinal is a light absorbing part of all the visual photopigments

- **(1)** (a), (c) & (d)
- (2) (a) & (c)
- (3) (b), (c) & (d)
- (4) (a) & (b)

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Carotene is the source of retinal which is involved in formation of rhodopsin of rod cells.
2. Retinal, a derivative of vitamin A, is the light-absorbing part of all visual photopigments.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Statement $(a)$: carotene (provitamin A) is converted in the body to vitamin A and its derivatives, so $(a)$ is true.
2. Statement $(b)$: the photopigments of rods and cones sit in the membrane discs of the $\text{outer}$ segment, not the inner segment, so $(b)$ is false.
3. Statement $(c)$: retinal is the aldehyde of vitamin A (retinol), so $(c)$ is true.
4. Statement $(d)$: every visual photopigment is opsin $+$ retinal, and retinal is the part that absorbs light, so $(d)$ is true.
5. True set $= \{(a), (c), (d)\}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Sense Organs: The Eye · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 131](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q131_9a6a01da_29102026.png?v=9a6a01da6e2b)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 131](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q131_d81c5dc9_29102026.png?v=d81c5dc90f32)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Carotene yields vitamin A, retinal is its derivative and is the light-absorbing part of every visual photopigment, and those photopigments lie in the membranous discs of the outer segment of rods and cones
- **Trap:** **Wrong site attacked** (`wrong_site_attacked`) — the reagent is right but it attacks the other site, atom or bond than the one the question turns on — option 3  
  A student who holds the carotene-to-retinal chain but remembers only that the photopigment sits in stacked membrane discs puts those discs in the inner segment, and then drops (a) because it looks like a repeat of the stem's own premise.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated line on where the visual photopigments sit, demonstrated by (b) placing them 'in the membrane discs of the inner segment' as the only item whose truth separates the four combinations.

## Q132

Biology · zoology · Evolution

Artificial selection to obtain cows yielding higher milk output represents

- **(1)** Directional as it pushes the mean of the character in one direction
- (2) Disruptive as it splits the population into two one yielding higher output and the other lower output
- (3) Stabilizing followed by disruptive as it stabilizes the population to produce higher yielding cows
- (4) Stabilizing selection as it stabilizes this character in the population

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Artificial selection to obtain cow yielding higher milk output will shift the peak to one direction, hence, will be an example of Directional selection.
2. In stabilizing selection, the organisms with the mean value of the trait are selected.
3. In disruptive selection, both extremes get selected.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Breeding only the cows that give more milk favours one extreme of the trait, so the mean milk yield moves in one direction. This is directional selection, option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Types of Natural Selection · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 132](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q132_3fb4ff54_29102026.png?v=3fb4ff54df49)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 132](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q132_21bc8667_29102026.png?v=21bc86672039)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Selection that favours one extreme drags the population mean towards it, which is directional selection; stabilizing favours the mean and disruptive favours both extremes.
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 4  
  The option pairs a neighbouring selection type with its own true definition, and the word 'stabilizes' matches the breeder's aim of fixing a high-yield stock, so a student who has the three types as names reaches for the one whose wording echoes the goal.
- **Why:** This question requires the three types of selection applied to a case the chapter does not list, demonstrated by each option carrying its own criterion -- the keyed one being 'Directional as it pushes the mean of the character in one direction'.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier recorded statement_options, which maps to qtype statements; the stem is a trailing 'represents' with no statement block and no lettered list, so mcq is recorded.

## Q133

Biology · zoology · Body Fluids and Circulation

Frog's heart when taken out of the body continues to beat for some time
Select the best option from the following statements

- **((a))** Frog is a poikilotherm
- **((b))** Frog does not have any coronary circulation
- **((c))** Heart is "myogenic" in nature
- **((d))** Heart is autoexcitable

- (1) Only (d)
- (2) (a) & (b)
- **(3)** (c) & (d)
- (4) Only (c)

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Frog or the vertebrates have myogenic heart having self contractile system or are autoexcitable; because of this condition, it will keep on working outside the body for some time.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The beat continuing outside the body means the impulse for contraction starts within the heart itself, so the heart is myogenic ($c$) and autoexcitable ($d$); being a poikilotherm ($a$) or lacking coronary circulation ($b$) does not explain it, so the answer is $(c)\ \&\ (d)$, option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Regulation of Cardiac Activity · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 133](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q133_2c174275_29102026.png?v=2c174275b9ec)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 133](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q133_13c2e139_29102026.png?v=13c2e139223c)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** The heart's beat starts in its own nodal muscle — the heart is myogenic and that muscle is autoexcitable — so it keeps beating when cut off from the body's nerves
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 1  
  'Keeps beating by itself' maps straight onto the one word autoexcitable, and a student who reads 'myogenic' as a remark about the heart being made of muscle keeps (d) and drops (c).
- **Why:** This question requires the reason a heart beats with no body attached, demonstrated by the key taking (c) 'Heart is "myogenic" in nature' together with (d) 'Heart is autoexcitable' and leaving out (a) 'Frog is a poikilotherm', which is true of the animal but accounts for no beat.
- **Note:** (a) is true of a frog yet is excluded by the key because it does not explain the beating; the question is tagged as printed. (b)'s claim about coronary circulation is used here only as what a student recalls on the way to option 2, not judged for truth.

## Q134

Biology · botany · Plant Kingdom

Select the mismatch :

- (1) Cycas – Dioecious
- (2) Salvinia – Heterosporous
- (3) Equisetum – Homosporous
- **(4)** Pinus – Dioecious

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Pinus is monoecious plant having both male and female cones on same plant.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\text{Cycas}$: male cones and megasporophylls are borne on different plants, so it is dioecious; option $1$ is a correct match.
2. $\text{Salvinia}$ produces microspores and megaspores, so it is heterosporous; option $2$ is a correct match.
3. $\text{Equisetum}$ produces one kind of spore, so it is homosporous; option $3$ is a correct match.
4. $\text{Pinus}$ bears male and female cones on the same tree, so it is monoecious, not dioecious; option $4$ is the mismatch.

**Subtopic:** Gymnosperms · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

## Q136

Physics · Gravitation

Two astronauts are floating in gravitational free space after having lost contact with their spaceship. The two will

- **(1)** Move towards each other
- (2) Move away from each other
- (3) Will become stationary
- (4) Keep floating at the same distance between them

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Both the astronauts are in the condition of weightness.
2. Gravitational force between them pulls towards each other.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. "Gravitational free space" means no external gravitational field acts, but the two masses still attract each other: $F = \frac{G m_1 m_2}{r^2}$.
2. This attractive force on each astronaut points towards the other, giving accelerations $a_1 = \frac{F}{m_1}$ and $a_2 = \frac{F}{m_2}$ directed towards each other.
3. With no external force the centre of mass stays fixed ($\vec{F}_{ext} = 0$), so starting from relative rest both move towards it, i.e. towards each other, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Universal Law of Gravitation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 136](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q136_11f93383_29102026.png?v=11f933833b35)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 136](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q136_e3521846_29102026.png?v=e3521846578e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Every pair of masses attracts by gravitation, so two bodies alone in space accelerate towards each other; 'free space' removes outside fields, not their pull on one another
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  'Gravitational free space' is read as 'no gravitational force at all', so the pair is treated as force-free and its separation is held fixed; the phrase removes only outside fields, and each astronaut is still a mass pulling on the other
- **Why:** This question requires applying the universal law of gravitation to the two bodies the stem supplies rather than to any outside one, demonstrated by 'Two astronauts are floating in gravitational free space' with no third mass, charge or thrust named.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q137

Physics · Wave Optics

Young’s double slit experiment is first performed in air and then in a medium other than air. It is found that $8^{\text{th}}$ bright fringe in the medium lies where $5^{\text{th}}$ dark fringe lies in air. The refractive index of the medium is nearly

- (1) $1.59$
- (2) $1.69$
- **(3)** $1.78$
- (4) $1.25$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $X_{1} = X_{5\text{th dark}} = (2 \times 5 - 1)\frac{\lambda D}{2d}$
2. $X_{2} = X_{8\text{th bright}} = 8\frac{\lambda D}{\mu d}$
3. $X_{1} = X_{2}$
4. $\frac{9}{2}\frac{\cancel{\lambda D}}{\cancel{d}} = 8\frac{\cancel{\lambda D}}{\mu \cancel{d}}$
5. $\mu = \frac{16}{9} = 1.78$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In a medium of refractive index $\mu$ the wavelength becomes $\lambda' = \frac{\lambda}{\mu}$.
2. Position of the $8^{\text{th}}$ bright fringe in the medium: $y_8 = \frac{8\lambda' D}{d} = \frac{8\lambda D}{\mu d}$.
3. Position of the $5^{\text{th}}$ dark fringe in air: $y_5 = \frac{(2\cdot5-1)\lambda D}{2d} = \frac{4.5\lambda D}{d}$.
4. Equate the positions: $\frac{8}{\mu} = 4.5$.
5. $\mu = \frac{8}{4.5} \approx 1.78$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Fringe Width in YDSE · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 137](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q137_a2b83654_29102026.png?v=a2b83654f145)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 137](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q137_a381dc94_29102026.png?v=a381dc94a759)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In a medium the wavelength becomes $\lambda/\mu$, so every fringe position shrinks by $1/\mu$; bright fringes sit at whole multiples of $\lambda D/d$ and dark fringes at half-integer multiples
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The 5th dark fringe is placed at $5\lambda D/d$, which is the position formula for a bright fringe, so $8/\mu = 5$ and $\mu = 1.6$, and 1.59 is the only printed value within reach of that
- **Why:** This question requires computing the refractive index from the coincidence of two fringe positions, demonstrated by '$8^{\text{th}}$ bright fringe in the medium lies where $5^{\text{th}}$ dark fringe lies in air' with four numerical options.
- **Note:** Only option 3 is an exact value of the coincidence condition, $16/9 = 1.78$; the other three are printed to two decimals around it, so the two trap routes are given with the number they produce (1.6 and 1.67) and the printed option they read onto.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q138

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

In a common emitter transistor amplifier the audio signal voltage across the collector is $3\,\mathrm{V}$. The resistance of collector is $3\,\mathrm{k\Omega}$. If current gain is 100 and the base resistance is $2\,\mathrm{k\Omega}$, the voltage and power gain of the amplifier is

- (1) 15 and 200
- **(2)** 150 and 15000
- (3) 20 and 2000
- (4) 200 and 1000

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Current gain $(\beta) = 100$
2. Voltage gain $(A_V) = \beta\frac{R_c}{R_b}$
3. $= 100\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)$
4. $= 150$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a CE amplifier the voltage gain is $A_V = \beta \frac{R_C}{R_B}$.
2. $A_V = 100 \times \frac{3\,\mathrm{k\Omega}}{2\,\mathrm{k\Omega}} = 150$.
3. The power gain is $A_P = \beta \times A_V$.
4. $A_P = 100 \times 150 = 15000$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Transistor as an Amplifier (Common Emitter Configuration): Voltage, Current and Power Gain · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 138](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q138_8e0e4a01_29102026.png?v=8e0e4a01c90b)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 138](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q138_944bdbd1_29102026.png?v=944bdbd1275c)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In a common-emitter amplifier the voltage gain is the current gain times the collector-to-base resistance ratio, and the power gain is the voltage gain times the current gain
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  The set-up $A_V = \beta R_C/R_B = 100 \times (3\,\mathrm{k\Omega}/2\,\mathrm{k\Omega})$ is right and the resistance ratio is carried as $0.15$ instead of $1.5$, giving 15 — and '15 and 200' is the only option whose first number is that
- **Why:** This question requires computing two gains from the stem's numbers, demonstrated by 'If current gain is 100 and the base resistance is $2\,\mathrm{k\Omega}$, the voltage and power gain of the amplifier is' with each option printing two numbers.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier returned plain_mcq, but the stem asks for two quantities and each option is a pair of numbers accepted or rejected whole, so the printed shape recorded here is two_part.

## Q139

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

> ⚠ The booklet lost this question's number and first line, and no other booklet code of this exam prints it; the stem starts mid-sentence.

An arrangement of three parallel straight wires placed perpendicular to plane of paper carrying same current ‘$I$’ along the same direction is shown in Fig. Magnitude of force per unit length on the middle wire ‘$B$’ is given by

![printed figure: Three parallel wires A, B, C perpendicular to the page carrying current out of the page, with AB = BC = d and a right angle at B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q139_013d9dcb_29102026.svg?v=013d9dcb9834)

*Three parallel wires A, B, C perpendicular to the page carrying current out of the page, with AB = BC = d and a right angle at B*

![drawn: Three parallel wires A, B, C perpendicular to the page carrying current out of the page, with AB = BC = d and a right angle at B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q139_question_ac91c62c_29102026.svg?v=ac91c62c1ca1)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $\frac{2\mu_0 I^2}{\pi d}$
- (2) $\frac{\sqrt{2}\mu_0 I^2}{\pi d}$
- **(3)** $\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{\sqrt{2}\pi d}$
- (4) $\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Force between $BC$ and $AB$ will be same in magnitude.

![printed figure: Forces F on wire B due to wires C and A at distance d, at 90 degrees](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q139_sol_1_b8b77ee6_29102026.svg?v=b8b77ee6b994)

*Forces F on wire B due to wires C and A at distance d, at 90 degrees*

![drawn: Forces F on wire B due to wires C and A at distance d, at 90 degrees](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q139_solution_1_46f09fd3_29102026.svg?v=46f09fd35dd0)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

2. $F_{BC} = F_{BA} = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$
3. $F = \sqrt{2}F_{BC}$
4. $= \sqrt{2}\frac{\mu_0}{2\pi}\frac{I^2}{d}$
5. $F = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{\sqrt{2}\pi d}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure, $A$ is at distance $d$ from $B$ and $C$ is at distance $d$ from $B$, with $\angle ABC = 90^\circ$.
2. Like (parallel) currents attract, so the force on $B$ due to $A$ points towards $A$ and the force due to $C$ points towards $C$.
3. Force per unit length from each wire: $F_{BA} = F_{BC} = \frac{\mu_0 I \cdot I}{2\pi d} = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$.
4. The two forces are perpendicular, so $F = \sqrt{F_{BA}^2 + F_{BC}^2} = \sqrt{2}\,\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$.
5. $\sqrt{2}\,\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d} = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{\sqrt{2}\pi d}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Force Between Two Parallel Current-Carrying Conductors · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 139](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q139_1a1b4213_29102026.png?v=1a1b42136396)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 139](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q139_cd2d31d2_29102026.png?v=cd2d31d202e1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Parallel currents in the same direction attract with force per unit length $\mu_0 I^2/(2\pi d)$; the middle wire feels two such pulls at right angles, so the resultant is $\sqrt{2}$ times one of them
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 4  
  $\mu_0 I^2/(2\pi d)$ is the force per unit length between TWO parallel wires a distance $d$ apart, so a student who recalls that standard result and substitutes stops with the pull from one neighbour and never adds the second
- **Why:** This question requires deriving the resultant of two equal perpendicular forces from the two-wire force law, demonstrated by 'Magnitude of force per unit length on the middle wire B' with the figure placing $A$ and $C$ at distance $d$ on two sides of a right angle at $B$.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied to this step as a drawn spec and confirms what the derivation needs: $A$, $B$, $C$ all carry current out of the page, $AB = BC = d$, and the angle at $B$ is $90^\circ$ (claimed and checked within 1.5 degrees). The geometry is only the input; the work the question asks for is the derivation, which is why asks is calculate and not read_diagram.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q140

Physics · Modern Physics

The de-Broglie wavelength of a neutron in thermal equilibrium with heavy water at a temperature $T$ (Kelvin) and mass $m$, is

- **(1)** $\dfrac{h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$
- (2) $\dfrac{2h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$
- (3) $\dfrac{2h}{\sqrt{mkT}}$
- (4) $\dfrac{h}{\sqrt{mkT}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. de-Broglie wavelength
2. $\lambda = \frac{h}{mv}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A neutron in thermal equilibrium at temperature $T$ has mean kinetic energy $E = \frac{3}{2}kT$.
2. Momentum from kinetic energy: $p = \sqrt{2mE}$.
3. $p = \sqrt{2m \cdot \frac{3}{2}kT} = \sqrt{3mkT}$.
4. $\lambda = \frac{h}{p} = \frac{h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** de Broglie Wavelength of Matter Waves · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 140](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q140_84c76f6d_29102026.png?v=84c76f6d30ba)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 140](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q140_fefdabe3_29102026.png?v=fefdabe3b0f0)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A free particle in thermal equilibrium carries kinetic energy $\tfrac{3}{2}kT$ over its three translational degrees of freedom, so $p = \sqrt{3mkT}$ and $\lambda = h/\sqrt{3mkT}$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  The thermal energy is taken as $\tfrac{1}{2}kT$, the one-degree-of-freedom form of equipartition, so $p = \sqrt{2m\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}kT} = \sqrt{mkT}$ and the printed $h/\sqrt{mkT}$ comes out exactly
- **Why:** This question requires chaining the thermal kinetic energy into the de Broglie relation to get an expression, demonstrated by 'in thermal equilibrium with heavy water at a temperature $T$ (Kelvin) and mass $m$' with four symbolic options differing only in the 3 and a factor 2.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q141

Physics · Modern Physics

> ⚠ The booklet lost this question's number and first line, and no other booklet code of this exam prints it; the stem starts mid-sentence.

> ⚠ The printed solution says "No option is correct": the question has no correct option as printed, and the printed answer comes from a corrected reading.

Radioactive material 'A' has decay constant '$8\lambda$' and material 'B' has decay constant '$\lambda$'. Initially they have same number of nuclei. After what time, the ratio of number of nuclei of material 'B' to that 'A' will be $\frac{1}{e}$?

- **(1)** $\frac{1}{7\lambda}$
- (2) $\frac{1}{8\lambda}$
- (3) $\frac{1}{9\lambda}$
- (4) $\frac{1}{\lambda}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. No option is correct
2. If we take $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = \frac{1}{e}$
3. Then
4. $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = \frac{e^{-8\lambda t}}{e^{-\lambda t}}$
5. $\frac{1}{e} = e^{-7\lambda t}$
6. $-1 = -7\lambda t$
7. $t = \frac{1}{7\lambda}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $N_A = N_0 e^{-8\lambda t}$ and $N_B = N_0 e^{-\lambda t}$, with the same $N_0$ initially.
2. $\frac{N_B}{N_A} = \frac{e^{-\lambda t}}{e^{-8\lambda t}} = e^{7\lambda t}$.
3. For $t > 0$, $e^{7\lambda t} > 1$, so $\frac{N_B}{N_A} = \frac{1}{e}$ would need $t = -\frac{1}{7\lambda}$, which is not a physical time.
4. Taking the intended ratio $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = e^{-7\lambda t} = \frac{1}{e}$ gives $7\lambda t = 1$.
5. $t = \frac{1}{7\lambda}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Law of Radioactive Decay · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 141](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q141_bbbfe117_29102026.png?v=bbbfe1172172)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** When two samples decay with different constants, their number ratio changes as $e^{(\lambda_A-\lambda_B)t}$, so an $e$-fold change in the ratio is set by the DIFFERENCE of the constants, $8\lambda-\lambda = 7\lambda$
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 3  
  Both decay laws are written correctly and then combined by adding the constants, $8\lambda + \lambda = 9\lambda$, instead of subtracting them, so $e^{-9\lambda t} = 1/e$ and $t = 1/(9\lambda)$
- **Why:** This question requires dividing the two decay laws so that the constants subtract, demonstrated by the stem's 'decay constant $8\lambda$' and 'decay constant $\lambda$' against options that are the reciprocals of $7\lambda$, $8\lambda$, $9\lambda$ and $\lambda$.
- **Note:** Tagged as printed. Read strictly, the stem's ratio of $B$ to $A$ is $e^{-\lambda t}/e^{-8\lambda t} = e^{+7\lambda t}$, which grows and never equals $1/e$ for positive $t$; the printed key, $1/(7\lambda)$, is the time at which the $A$-to-$B$ ratio reaches $1/e$. The magnitude is unaffected, so the key is not re-judged here, but the order of the two materials in the stem is worth a reviewer's eye.

## Q142

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

The given electrical network is equivalent to

![printed figure: A NOR gate on inputs A and B, a second NOR gate with both inputs tied together, then a NOT gate giving output Y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q142_2769b0a9_29102026.svg?v=2769b0a9e392)

*A NOR gate on inputs A and B, a second NOR gate with both inputs tied together, then a NOT gate giving output Y*

![drawn: A NOR gate on inputs A and B, a second NOR gate with both inputs tied together, then a NOT gate giving output Y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q142_question_677f55ec_29102026.svg?v=677f55ec8616)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) OR gate
- **(2)** NOR gate
- (3) NOT gate
- (4) AND gate

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $Y = \overline{A + B}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The first gate is a NOR gate: $Y_1 = \overline{A+B}$.
2. The second gate is a NOR gate with both inputs tied together, so it acts as a NOT gate: $Y_2 = \overline{Y_1 + Y_1} = \overline{Y_1} = A+B$.
3. The third gate is a NOT gate: $Y = \overline{Y_2} = \overline{A+B}$.
4. $Y = \overline{A+B}$ is the NOR function, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Logic Gates · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 142](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q142_a07a1f8e_29102026.png?v=a07a1f8ef04a)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 142](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q142_f782e0f4_29102026.png?v=f782e0f4c010)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Figure reading** (`read_diagram`) — read a figure, graph or drawn structure and answer from it
- **Concept:** Inversions cancel in pairs along a chain: a NOR on A and B, then a NOR with its inputs tied (an inverter), then a NOT leaves $Y = (A+B)'$, so the network is a NOR
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  OR is what the network really is at its middle node — the first NOR gives $(A+B)'$ and the second NOR with its inputs tied inverts it back to $A+B$ — so cancelling the first two gates against each other and stopping there gives OR
- **Why:** This question requires propagating the two inputs through the drawn chain of gates, demonstrated by 'The given electrical network is equivalent to' with the figure supplying a NOR on $A$ and $B$, a second NOR with both inputs joined, and a NOT before $Y$.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a drawn spec and reads: OR body with an output bubble on $A$ and $B$, output split to both inputs of a second OR body with an output bubble, then a triangle with an output bubble to $Y$ — that is NOR, NOR-as-NOT, NOT, giving $Y = (A+B)'$, which agrees with the printed key.

## Q143

Physics · Current Electricity

The resistance of a wire is ‘$R$’ ohm. If it is melted and stretched to ‘$n$’ times its original length, its new resistance will be

- (1) $\frac{R}{n}$
- **(2)** $n^{2}R$
- (3) $\frac{R}{n^{2}}$
- (4) $nR$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\frac{R_2}{R_1} = \frac{l_2^2}{l_1^2}$
2. $= \frac{n^2 l_1^2}{l_1^2}$
3. $\frac{R_2}{R_1} = n^2$
4. $R_2 = n^2 R_1$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Melting and stretching keeps the volume constant: $lA = l'A'$.
2. With $l' = nl$, the new area is $A' = \frac{A}{n}$.
3. $R' = \frac{\rho l'}{A'} = \frac{\rho (nl)}{A/n}$.
4. $R' = n^{2}\frac{\rho l}{A} = n^{2}R$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Resistance · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 143](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q143_9ee64098_29102026.png?v=9ee64098dcf6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 143](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q143_6c15fad7_29102026.png?v=6c15fad7766b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** Melting and redrawing the same wire keeps its volume, so stretching to $n$ times the length thins the cross-section to $A/n$ and $R = \rho L/A$ grows by $n^2$
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  $R \propto L$ is applied with the cross-section held fixed, so $n$ times the length gives $nR$; 'melted and stretched' fixes the volume instead, and the area falls to $A/n$ at the same time
- **Why:** This question requires saying by what factor the resistance changes when the length changes, demonstrated by 'melted and stretched to n times its original length, its new resistance will be' with options that are $R$ scaled by powers of $n$.

## Q144

Physics · Waves

Two cars moving in opposite directions approach each other with speed of $22\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and $16.5\,\mathrm{m/s}$ respectively. The driver of the first car blows a horn having a frequency $400\,\mathrm{Hz}$. The frequency heard by the driver of the second car is [velocity of sound $340\,\mathrm{m/s}$]

- (1) $361\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (2) $411\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- **(3)** $448\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (4) $350\,\mathrm{Hz}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $f_A = f\left[\frac{v + v_o}{v - v_s}\right]$
2. $= 400\left[\frac{340 + 16.5}{340 - 22}\right]$
3. $f_A = 448\,\mathrm{Hz}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The source (first car, $v_s = 22\,\mathrm{m/s}$) and the observer (second car, $v_o = 16.5\,\mathrm{m/s}$) move towards each other, so both raise the apparent frequency.
2. $f' = f\,\frac{v + v_o}{v - v_s}$.
3. $f' = 400 \times \frac{340 + 16.5}{340 - 22} = 400 \times \frac{356.5}{318}$.
4. $f' = \frac{142600}{318} \approx 448.4\,\mathrm{Hz} \approx 448\,\mathrm{Hz}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Doppler Effect · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 144](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q144_a064de66_29102026.png?v=a064de66305e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 144](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q144_61197e93_29102026.png?v=61197e93f2c7)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** With both source and listener moving, $f = f_0(v+v_o)/(v-v_s)$ when they approach: the listener's speed adds on top and the source's subtracts below, so the pitch rises
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The two speeds are entered with the receding signs, $400 \times (340-16.5)/(340+22) = 357$ Hz, so the pitch comes out lower than 400 Hz and 361 Hz is the printed value it reads onto
- **Why:** This question requires computing the heard frequency with both motions in the Doppler formula, demonstrated by 'approach each other with speed of $22\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and $16.5\,\mathrm{m/s}$' and the supplied 'velocity of sound $340\,\mathrm{m/s}$'.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier returned multi_part_respectively on the stem's word 'respectively', but that word governs the two cars' speeds, not the answer: one frequency is asked for and each option is a single value, so the printed shape recorded here is mcq. Only the key is an exact value of the formula (448.4 Hz); the wrong options are printed near-misses, so each route names the number it produces and the option that number reads onto.

## Q145

Physics · Electromagnetic Waves

In an electromagnetic wave in free space the root mean square value of the electric field is $E_{\mathrm{rms}} = 6\,\mathrm{V/m}$. The peak value of the magnetic field is

- **(1)** $2.83 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$
- (2) $0.70 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$
- (3) $4.23 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$
- (4) $1.41 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\frac{E_{\mathrm{rms}}}{B_{\mathrm{rms}}} = c$
2. $B_{\mathrm{rms}} = \frac{E_{\mathrm{rms}}}{c}$
3. $= \frac{6}{3\times10^{8}}$
4. $B_{\mathrm{rms}} = 2 \times 10^{-8}$
5. $B_{\mathrm{rms}} = \frac{B_0}{\sqrt{2}}$
6. $B_0 = \sqrt{2}\times B_{\mathrm{rms}}$
7. $= \sqrt{2}\times2\times10^{-8}$
8. $= 2.83 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The peak electric field is $E_0 = \sqrt{2}\,E_{\mathrm{rms}} = \sqrt{2} \times 6\,\mathrm{V/m} \approx 8.485\,\mathrm{V/m}$.
2. In free space the peak fields are related by $B_0 = \frac{E_0}{c}$.
3. $B_0 = \frac{8.485}{3 \times 10^{8}}\,\mathrm{T} \approx 2.83 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Relation Between E, B, and Speed of Light · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 145](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q145_0874aacb_29102026.png?v=0874aacbd7b7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 145](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q145_fd5a3e5c_29102026.png?v=fd5a3e5c2102)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In an electromagnetic wave $E/B = c$ at every instant, and for either field the peak value is $\sqrt{2}$ times the rms value
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  $B_{rms} = E_{rms}/c = 6/(3\times10^8) = 2\times10^{-8}$ T is right, and the $\sqrt{2}$ between rms and peak is then used as a divisor, giving $1.41\times10^{-8}$ T
- **Why:** This question requires computing a peak field from an rms field through $E/B = c$, demonstrated by 'the root mean square value of the electric field is $E_{\mathrm{rms}} = 6\,\mathrm{V/m}$' against 'The peak value of the magnetic field'.

## Q146

Physics · Thermal Properties of Matter

Two rods $A$ and $B$ of different materials are welded together as shown in figure. Their thermal conductivities are $K_1$ and $K_2$. The thermal conductivity of the composite rod will be

![printed figure: Two hatched rods A (K1) and B (K2) side by side between T1 and T2, length d](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q146_5941708d_29102026.svg?v=5941708d7f43)

*Two hatched rods A (K1) and B (K2) side by side between T1 and T2, length d*

![drawn: Two hatched rods A (K1) and B (K2) side by side between T1 and T2, length d](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q146_question_cd2d11f9_29102026.svg?v=cd2d11f97c48)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $\frac{3(K_1+K_2)}{2}$
- (2) $K_1 + K_2$
- (3) $2(K_1 + K_2)$
- **(4)** $\frac{K_1+K_2}{2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Thermal current
2. $H = H_1 + H_2$
3. $= \frac{K_1 A(T_1 - T_2)}{d} + \frac{K_2 A(T_1 - T_2)}{d}$
4. $\frac{K_{EQ} 2A(T_1 - T_2)}{d} = \frac{A(T_1 - T_2)}{d}\left[K_1 + K_2\right]$
5. $K_{EQ} = \left[\frac{K_1 + K_2}{2}\right]$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure, both rods span the same length $d$ between $T_1$ and $T_2$, so they conduct heat in parallel; take each cross-section as $A$ (equal as drawn).
2. Total heat current: $H = H_1 + H_2 = \frac{K_1 A (T_1 - T_2)}{d} + \frac{K_2 A (T_1 - T_2)}{d}$.
3. For the composite rod of area $2A$: $H = \frac{K_{eq} (2A)(T_1 - T_2)}{d}$.
4. Equating: $2A K_{eq} = A(K_1 + K_2) \Rightarrow K_{eq} = \frac{K_1 + K_2}{2}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Connection of Rods · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 146](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q146_5bc04f53_29102026.png?v=5bc04f53a5a9)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 146](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q146_3579f7de_29102026.png?v=3579f7de28fd)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Two rods side by side across the same temperature drop conduct in parallel, so the composite conductivity is the area-weighted mean of the two — $(K_1+K_2)/2$ for equal cross-sections
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  'In parallel they add' is applied to the conductivities themselves, giving $K_1+K_2$; what adds in parallel is the conductance $KA/d$, and the composite carries twice the area, which brings the mean back
- **Why:** This question requires deriving the conductivity of a composite whose two halves conduct in parallel, demonstrated by 'Their thermal conductivities are $K_1$ and $K_2$' with the figure placing both rods along the same length $d$ between $T_1$ and $T_2$.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a drawn spec: two equal hatched blocks of the same length $d$, stacked one above the other with $T_1$ at the left face and $T_2$ at the right, so the combination is parallel. The series reading is not among the printed options — $2K_1K_2/(K_1+K_2)$ appears nowhere — so the figure does not separate the printed options, which is why asks is calculate rather than read_diagram.

## Q147

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

Which one of the following represents forward bias diode?

- (1) ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series on a horizontal wire, left end at -4 V and right end at -3 V; diode arrow points right (anode left)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q147_option_1_5389ad9a_29102026.svg?v=5389ad9a47be) *A diode and a resistor R in series on a horizontal wire, left end at -4 V and right end at -3 V; diode arrow points right (anode left)* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series on a horizontal wire, left end at -4 V and right end at -3 V; diode arrow points right (anode left)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q147_option_1_77d3842f_29102026.svg?v=77d3842f4954)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $-4\,\mathrm{V}$ $R$ $-3\,\mathrm{V}$
- (2) ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series between -2 V on the left and +2 V on the right](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q147_option_2_9a843ca9_29102026.svg?v=9a843ca932ca) *A diode and a resistor R in series between -2 V on the left and +2 V on the right* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series between -2 V on the left and +2 V on the right](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q147_option_2_7571dde9_29102026.svg?v=7571dde9656e)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $-2\,\mathrm{V}$ $R$ $+2\,\mathrm{V}$
- (3) ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series on one wire, 3 V at the left end and 5 V at the right end](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q147_option_3_eabef2ec_29102026.svg?v=eabef2ec768e) *A diode and a resistor R in series on one wire, 3 V at the left end and 5 V at the right end* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series on one wire, 3 V at the left end and 5 V at the right end](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q147_option_3_7c8e8751_29102026.svg?v=7c8e8751c5d9)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $3\,\mathrm{V}$ $R$ $5\,\mathrm{V}$
- **(4)** ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series between 0 V (left) and -2 V (right)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q147_option_4_405c8476_29102026.svg?v=405c8476f68e) *A diode and a resistor R in series between 0 V (left) and -2 V (right)* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series between 0 V (left) and -2 V (right)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q147_option_4_fcd3e598_29102026.svg?v=fcd3e59878c4)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $0\,\mathrm{V}$ $R$ $-2\,\mathrm{V}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In forward bias, $p$-type semiconductor is at higher potential w.r.t. $n$-type semiconductor.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In every option the diode symbol points from left to right, so the anode is on the left terminal ($V_A$) and the cathode is on the right side, through $R$, at $V_K$.
2. A diode is forward biased when $V_A > V_K$, i.e. the p-side is at a higher potential than the n-side.
3. Option 1: $V_A = -4\,\mathrm{V} < V_K = -3\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is reverse biased.
4. Option 2: $V_A = -2\,\mathrm{V} < V_K = +2\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is reverse biased.
5. Option 3: $V_A = 3\,\mathrm{V} < V_K = 5\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is reverse biased.
6. Option 4: $V_A = 0\,\mathrm{V} > V_K = -2\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is forward biased, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** PN Junction Diode Under Forward Bias · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 147](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q147_1f215847_29102026.png?v=1f21584732ae)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 147](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q147_bd7c5722_29102026.png?v=bd7c57226311)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** A diode conducts when its p-side is held at the higher potential; the bias is set by the potential difference across the junction, not by whether either terminal reads positive
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  The four options are small circuits, and only the two terminal potentials and the resistor label survived into this record; the diode symbols, which carry the p-to-n direction the bias depends on, were not supplied to this step, so no wrong option can be shown to be the end of a route rather than an answer
- **Why:** This question requires applying the forward-bias condition to each printed circuit, demonstrated by 'Which one of the following represents forward bias diode?' with every option printing two terminal potentials across a resistor.
- **Note:** What a reviewer holding the printed options should check: the key, option 4, is the only one whose LEFT terminal is at the higher potential ($0\,\mathrm{V}$ against $-2\,\mathrm{V}$), while options 1, 2 and 3 all have the right terminal higher, so the key is consistent with all four diodes being drawn with the p-side at the left. If that holds, the expected mechanism is that 'forward bias' is read as 'a positive terminal is present' rather than as 'the p-side is the higher side', which would land on option 2 ($-2\,\mathrm{V}$ and $+2\,\mathrm{V}$) or option 3 (both positive). I could not confirm it from this record, so it is left as a note rather than a trap tag. This is a reading limit on our side, not damage to the paper.

## Q148

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

A 250-Turn rectangular coil of length $2.1\,\mathrm{cm}$ and width $1.25\,\mathrm{cm}$ carries a current of $85\,\mu\mathrm{A}$ and subjected to a magnetic field of strength $0.85\,\mathrm{T}$. Work done for rotating the coil by $180^\circ$ against the torque is

- (1) $4.55\,\mu\mathrm{J}$
- (2) $2.3\,\mu\mathrm{J}$
- (3) $1.15\,\mu\mathrm{J}$
- **(4)** $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $W = MB\,(\cos\theta_1 - \cos\theta_2)$
2. When it is rotated by angle $180^\circ$ then
3. $W = 2MB$
4. $W = 2\,(NIA)B$
5. $= 2 \times 250 \times 85 \times 10^{-6}[1.25 \times 2.1 \times 10^{-4}] \times 85 \times 10^{-2}$
6. $= 9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Area of the coil: $A = (2.1\times10^{-2})(1.25\times10^{-2}) = 2.625\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$.
2. Magnetic moment: $M = NIA = 250 \times 85\times10^{-6} \times 2.625\times10^{-4} = 5.578\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{A\,m^2}$.
3. Work done in rotating from $\theta = 0^\circ$ to $180^\circ$: $W = MB(\cos 0^\circ - \cos 180^\circ) = 2MB$.
4. $W = 2 \times 5.578\times10^{-6} \times 0.85 = 9.48\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{J} \approx 9.5\,\mu\mathrm{J}$.
5. The closest option is $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Work Done or Potential Energy of a Current-Carrying Loop in a Uniform Magnetic Field · **Difficulty:** hard (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 148](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q148_e40ec564_29102026.png?v=e40ec5648ff7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 148](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q148_0329fe21_29102026.png?v=0329fe21c738)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A current loop in a field has energy $U = -MB\cos\theta$ with $M = NIA$, so turning it through $180^\circ$ costs $2MB$ — the swing from $-MB$ to $+MB$
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  $M = NIA$ and $MB$ are worked out correctly and the answer is taken as $MB$; the $180^\circ$ turn carries the loop from $U = -MB$ to $U = +MB$, so the work is twice that, and the printed ladder has the halved value sitting one rung below the key
- **Why:** This question requires computing the work of a $180^\circ$ turn from the loop's magnetic moment, demonstrated by 'Work done for rotating the coil by $180^\circ$ against the torque is' with the coil's turns, dimensions, current and field all supplied.
- **Note:** Tagged as printed, but the arithmetic is worth a reviewer's eye: with the record's $1.25\,\mathrm{cm}$ width, $W = 2NIAB = 2 \times 250 \times 85\times10^{-6} \times 2.625\times10^{-4} \times 0.85 = 9.48\,\mu\mathrm{J}$, while the printed key is $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$. A width of $1.2\,\mathrm{cm}$ gives exactly $9.10\,\mu\mathrm{J}$ and makes the 4.55 rung exactly $MB$, so the width is the digit to check against the printed paper. The key is not re-judged here: 9.1 is the nearest printed value either way.

## Q149

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

If $\theta_1$ and $\theta_2$ be the apparent angles of dip observed in two vertical planes at right angles to each other, then the true angle of dip $\theta$ is given by

- (1) $\tan^2\theta = \tan^2\theta_1 + \tan^2\theta_2$
- (2) $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 - \cot^2\theta_2$
- (3) $\tan^2\theta = \tan^2\theta_1 - \tan^2\theta_2$
- **(4)** $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Let the first vertical plane make angle $\alpha$ with the magnetic meridian; the second is at $90^\circ - \alpha$ to it.
2. The vertical component $V$ is the same in both planes; the effective horizontal components are $H\cos\alpha$ and $H\sin\alpha$.
3. $\tan\theta_1 = \frac{V}{H\cos\alpha}$, so $\cot\theta_1 = \frac{H\cos\alpha}{V}$.
4. $\tan\theta_2 = \frac{V}{H\sin\alpha}$, so $\cot\theta_2 = \frac{H\sin\alpha}{V}$.
5. $\cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2 = \frac{H^2(\cos^2\alpha + \sin^2\alpha)}{V^2} = \frac{H^2}{V^2}$.
6. True dip satisfies $\cot\theta = \frac{H}{V}$, so $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Earth's Magnetism · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 149](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q149_4276bf47_29102026.png?v=4276bf47c7fe)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 149](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q149_ac0d93bf_29102026.png?v=ac0d93bfaca3)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Apparent dip in a vertical plane at angle $\alpha$ to the meridian satisfies $\cot\theta_1 = \cot\theta\cos\alpha$, so for two planes at right angles the cotangents add in quadrature: $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The shape 'the squares of the two add' is remembered without which function carries it, and it is written with tangents; tan is the reciprocal of cot, so the printed identity is the right relation turned upside down in every term
- **Why:** This question requires producing the relation between the true dip and two apparent dips, demonstrated by 'the apparent angles of dip observed in two vertical planes at right angles to each other' with four candidate identities as the options.

## Q150

Physics · Current Electricity

A potentiometer is an accurate and versatile device to make electrical measurements of E.M.F, because the method involves :

- (1) Potential gradients
- **(2)** A condition of no current flow through the galvanometer
- (3) A combination of cells, galvanometer and resistances
- (4) Cells

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Reading of potentiometer is accurate because during taking reading it does not draw any current from the circuit.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At the balance point the galvanometer shows null deflection, so $I_g = 0$ through the cell being measured.
2. With no current drawn, there is no drop across the cell's internal resistance: $V = E - Ir = E$ since $I = 0$.
3. So $E = k\,l$ is measured exactly, and this accuracy comes from the zero-current condition, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Potentiometer · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 150](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q150_a7bfe8a9_29102026.png?v=a7bfe8a9158c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 150](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q150_dec97884_29102026.png?v=dec978841f9b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** A potentiometer measures emf accurately because at the balance point the cell drives no current, so what is read is the emf and not the terminal voltage reduced by internal resistance
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  A uniform potential gradient along the wire is a true statement about the potentiometer and the sentence taught beside the balance condition, so it is picked as what the method 'involves'; the gradient explains how the instrument works, not why its emf reading is accurate
- **Why:** This question requires the stated reason the method is accurate, demonstrated by the stem's 'because the method involves :' after 'an accurate and versatile device to make electrical measurements of E.M.F'.
- **Note:** Option 1 is true of the instrument on a literal reading of 'involves', and the key rests on which true statement explains the accuracy. Worth a reviewer's eye as a wording question, though the subject itself favours the null condition, which is what separates an emf from a terminal voltage.

## Q151

Physics · Ray Optics

The ratio of resolving powers of an optical microscope for two wavelengths $\lambda_1 = 4000\,\text{\AA}$ and $\lambda_2 = 6000\,\text{\AA}$ is

- (1) $9 : 4$
- **(2)** $3 : 2$
- (3) $16 : 81$
- (4) $8 : 27$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Resolving power $\propto \frac{1}{\lambda}$
2. $\frac{R_1}{R_2} = \frac{\lambda_2}{\lambda_1}$
3. $= \frac{6000\,\text{\AA}}{4000\,\text{\AA}}$
4. $= \frac{3}{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The resolving power of an optical microscope is $RP = \frac{2n\sin\theta}{1.22\,\lambda}$, so $RP \propto \frac{1}{\lambda}$.
2. $\frac{RP_1}{RP_2} = \frac{\lambda_2}{\lambda_1} = \frac{6000\,\text{\AA}}{4000\,\text{\AA}}$.
3. $\frac{6000}{4000} = \frac{3}{2}$, so the ratio is $3 : 2$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Resolving and Magnifying Power of Microscope · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 151](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q151_0a279b92_29102026.png?v=0a279b922bc3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 151](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q151_9144969f_29102026.png?v=9144969fad8a)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** A microscope's resolving power is $2\mu\sin\theta/1.22\lambda$, so it goes as $1/\lambda$ to the first power and the shorter wavelength resolves better in that same proportion
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The inverse dependence is used with the wavelength squared, $(\lambda_2/\lambda_1)^2 = (6/4)^2 = 9:4$, instead of to the first power
- **Why:** This question requires saying by what factor the resolving power differs between two wavelengths, demonstrated by 'The ratio of resolving powers of an optical microscope for two wavelengths $\lambda_1 = 4000\,\text{\AA}$ and $\lambda_2 = 6000\,\text{\AA}$' with ratios as the options.

## Q152

Physics · Ray Optics

A thin prism having refracting angle $10^\circ$ is made of glass of refractive index $1.42$. This prism is combined with another thin prism of glass of refractive index $1.7$. This combination produces dispersion without deviation. The refracting angle of second prism should be

- **(1)** $6^\circ$
- (2) $8^\circ$
- (3) $10^\circ$
- (4) $4^\circ$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $(\mu - 1)A + (\mu' - 1)A' = 0$
2. $|(\mu - 1)A| = |(\mu' - 1)A'|$
3. $(1.42 - 1) \times 10^\circ = (1.7 - 1)A'$
4. $4.2 = 0.7A'$
5. $A' = 6^\circ$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a thin prism the deviation is $\delta = (\mu - 1)A$.
2. Dispersion without deviation needs the two deviations to cancel: $(\mu_1 - 1)A_1 = (\mu_2 - 1)A_2$.
3. Substitute: $(1.42 - 1)(10^\circ) = (1.7 - 1)A_2$, so $4.2^\circ = 0.7\,A_2$.
4. $A_2 = \frac{4.2^\circ}{0.7} = 6^\circ$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Dispersion, Prism · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 152](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q152_b20bbf67_29102026.png?v=b20bbf678011)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 152](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q152_fa6a99e9_29102026.png?v=fa6a99e92623)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Two thin prisms give dispersion without deviation when their deviations cancel, $(\mu_1-1)A_1 = (\mu_2-1)A_2$ — it is $\mu-1$, not $\mu$, that scales the deviation
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  The cancellation is written with the refractive indices themselves, $A_2 = A_1\mu_1/\mu_2 = 10 \times 1.42/1.7 = 8.35^\circ$, which reads onto the printed $8^\circ$; the deviation of a thin prism is $(\mu-1)A$, so the 1 must be subtracted first
- **Why:** This question requires computing the second refracting angle from the cancellation of deviations, demonstrated by 'This combination produces dispersion without deviation. The refracting angle of second prism should be' with both indices and the first angle supplied.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q153

Physics · Capacitance

A capacitor is charged by a battery. The battery is removed and another identical uncharged capacitor is connected in parallel. The total electrostatic energy of resulting system

- **(1)** Decreases by a factor of 2
- (2) Remains the same
- (3) Increases by a factor of 2
- (4) Increases by a factor of 4

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A capacitor C charged by a battery V in one rectangular loop](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q153_sol_1_9fc27ef1_29102026.svg?v=9fc27ef1fb9a)

*A capacitor C charged by a battery V in one rectangular loop*

![drawn: A capacitor C charged by a battery V in one rectangular loop](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q153_solution_1_1716dec8_29102026.svg?v=1716dec8baa6)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. Charge on capacitor $q = CV$
2. when it is connected with another uncharged capacitor.

![printed figure: Two capacitors C in one loop (in parallel), the top one carrying charge q](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q153_sol_2_09458c4c_29102026.svg?v=09458c4c5a8d)

*Two capacitors C in one loop (in parallel), the top one carrying charge q*

![drawn: Two capacitors C in one loop (in parallel), the top one carrying charge q](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q153_solution_2_8c262632_29102026.svg?v=8c262632ef77)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

3. $V_c = \frac{q_1 + q_2}{C_1 + C_2} = \frac{q + 0}{C + C}$
4. $V_c = \frac{V}{2}$
5. Initial energy $U_i = \frac{1}{2}CV^2$
6. Final energy $U_f = \frac{1}{2}C\left(\frac{V}{2}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{2}C\left(\frac{V}{2}\right)^2$
7. $= \frac{CV^2}{4}$
8. Loss of energy $= U_i - U_f$
9. $= \frac{CV^2}{4}$
10. i.e. decreases by a factor (2)

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Initial energy of the charged capacitor: $U_i = \frac{Q^2}{2C}$.
2. With the battery removed, the total charge $Q$ is conserved when the identical uncharged capacitor is connected.
3. Parallel combination gives $C_{eq} = C + C = 2C$.
4. Final energy: $U_f = \frac{Q^2}{2(2C)} = \frac{Q^2}{4C}$.
5. $\frac{U_f}{U_i} = \frac{1}{2}$, so the energy decreases by a factor of $2$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Redistribution of Charges, Energy Stored in a Capacitor · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 153](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q153_43ee37b2_29102026.png?v=43ee37b25c61)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 153](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q153_fb24568a_29102026.png?v=fb24568a7322)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** With the battery removed the charge is fixed, so sharing it with an identical capacitor doubles $C$ at constant $Q$ and $U = Q^2/2C$ falls to half, the rest going into the connecting process
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 3  
  $U = \tfrac{1}{2}CV^2$ is used with $V$ held at the value the battery set, so doubling the capacitance doubles the energy; that is the right answer for a battery still connected, and the stem has already removed it
- **Why:** This question requires saying by what factor the stored energy changes when the charge is shared, demonstrated by 'The battery is removed and another identical uncharged capacitor is connected in parallel' with options that are factors of 2 and 4 up or down.

## Q154

Physics · Kinematics

Preeti reached the metro station and found that the escalator was not working. She walked up the stationary escalator in time $t_1$. On other days, if she remains stationary on the moving escalator, then the escalator takes her up in time $t_2$. The time taken by her to walk up on the moving escalator will be

- (1) $\dfrac{t_1 t_2}{t_2 - t_1}$
- **(2)** $\dfrac{t_1 t_2}{t_2 + t_1}$
- (3) $t_1 - t_2$
- (4) $\dfrac{t_1 + t_2}{2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Velocity of girl w.r.t. elevator $= \frac{d}{t_1} = v_{ge}$
2. Velocity of elevator w.r.t. ground $v_{eG} = \frac{d}{t_2}$ then
3. velocity of girl w.r.t. ground
4. $\vec{v}_{gG} = \vec{v}_{ge} + \vec{v}_{eG}$
5. i.e, $v_{gG} = v_{ge} + v_{eG}$
6. $\frac{d}{t} = \frac{d}{t_1} + \frac{d}{t_2}$
7. $\frac{1}{t} = \frac{1}{t_1} + \frac{1}{t_2}$
8. $t = \frac{t_1 t_2}{(t_1 + t_2)}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Let the escalator length be $L$. Walking on the stationary escalator gives her speed $v_1 = \frac{L}{t_1}$.
2. Standing on the moving escalator gives the escalator speed $v_2 = \frac{L}{t_2}$.
3. Walking on the moving escalator, the speeds add relative to the ground: $v = v_1 + v_2 = \frac{L}{t_1} + \frac{L}{t_2}$.
4. $t = \frac{L}{v} = \frac{L}{\frac{L}{t_1} + \frac{L}{t_2}} = \frac{t_1 t_2}{t_1 + t_2}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Relative Motion in 1D · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 154](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q154_c1ad0ff4_29102026.png?v=c1ad0ff428e6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 154](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q154_21cb1fa8_29102026.png?v=21cb1fa84870)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Speeds add when two agents carry you the same way, so the reciprocals of the times add: $1/t = 1/t_1 + 1/t_2$ and $t = t_1t_2/(t_1+t_2)$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The reciprocals are combined with a minus sign, $1/t = 1/t_1 - 1/t_2$, which gives $t_1t_2/(t_2-t_1)$; that is the form for walking AGAINST the escalator, and here her walking and the escalator both carry her the same way
- **Why:** This question requires producing the combined time from two separately measured times, demonstrated by 'She walked up the stationary escalator in time $t_1$' and 'the escalator takes her up in time $t_2$' with symbolic options.
