## Q155

Physics · Thermodynamics

Thermodynamic processes are indicated in the following diagram.
Match the following

![printed figure: P-V diagram with isotherms 300 K, 500 K, 700 K and processes I-IV from i to f](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q155_9db1ba4f_29102026.svg?v=9db1ba4faaba)

*P-V diagram with isotherms 300 K, 500 K, 700 K and processes I-IV from i to f*

![drawn: P-V diagram with isotherms 300 K, 500 K, 700 K and processes I-IV from i to f](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q155_question_9d427fc1_29102026.svg?v=9d427fc196ff)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

| List-I | | List-II | |
|---|---|---|---|
| P | Process I | a | Adiabatic |
| Q | Process II | b | Isobaric |
| R | Process III | c | Isochoric |
| S | Process IV | d | Isothermal |

- **(1)** P $\rightarrow$ c, Q $\rightarrow$ a, R $\rightarrow$ d, S $\rightarrow$ b
- (2) P $\rightarrow$ c, Q $\rightarrow$ d, R $\rightarrow$ b, S $\rightarrow$ a
- (3) P $\rightarrow$ d, Q $\rightarrow$ b, R $\rightarrow$ a, S $\rightarrow$ c
- (4) P $\rightarrow$ a, Q $\rightarrow$ c, R $\rightarrow$ d, S $\rightarrow$ b

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Process I = Isochoric
2. II = Adiabatic
3. III = Isothermal
4. IV = Isobaric

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Process I is a vertical line on the $P$-$V$ diagram, so $V$ is constant: isochoric, $P \rightarrow c$.
2. Process III runs along the isotherm through $i$, so $T$ is constant: isothermal, $R \rightarrow d$.
3. Process II is steeper than that isotherm ($PV^{\gamma} = \text{const}$ with $\gamma > 1$) and ends on the lower $300\,\text{K}$ isotherm, so it is adiabatic: $Q \rightarrow a$.
4. Process IV is a horizontal line, so $P$ is constant: isobaric, $S \rightarrow b$.
5. $P \rightarrow c,\ Q \rightarrow a,\ R \rightarrow d,\ S \rightarrow b$ is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Thermodynamic Processes, Problems on Different Graphs of P, V, and T · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 155](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q155_64500565_29102026.png?v=64500565a4c2)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 155](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q155_f34d43a4_29102026.png?v=f34d43a4acca)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Match the columns** (`match_columns`) — two labelled lists to pair, the answer is a pairing code
- **Asks:** **Figure reading** (`read_diagram`) — read a figure, graph or drawn structure and answer from it
- **Concept:** On a P-V diagram the shape of the path names the process: horizontal is isobaric, vertical is isochoric, running along an isotherm is isothermal, and a curve steeper than the isotherm is adiabatic
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  The slope rule carried as 'the adiabatic curve is the steeper one' is applied to the steepest line in the figure, which is the vertical path I, so I is called adiabatic and II picks up isochoric; the other two pairs stay right, and only option 4 prints P $\rightarrow$ a
- **Why:** This question requires reading the geometry of each drawn path off the P-V plot and naming the process it is, demonstrated by the stem 'Thermodynamic processes are indicated in the following diagram' with List-I holding only 'Process I' to 'Process IV' and no property printed to reason from.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied to this step as a full drawn spec, so the four paths from i could be read: horizontal to the 700 K curve, vertical down to the 300 K curve, along the 500 K curve, and a steeper curve crossing to 300 K. That reading agrees with the printed key, option 1.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q156

Physics · Laws of Motion

One end of string of length $l$ is connected to a particle of mass ‘$m$’ and the other end is connected to a small peg on a smooth horizontal table. If the particle moves in circle with speed ‘$v$’, the net force on the particle (directed towards center) will be ($T$ represents the tension in the string)

- (1) $T+\frac{mv^{2}}{l}$
- (2) $T-\frac{mv^{2}}{l}$
- (3) Zero
- **(4)** $T$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Centripetal force $\left(\frac{mv^{2}}{l}\right)$ is provided by tension so the net force will be equal to tension i.e., $T$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The table is smooth and horizontal, so the vertical forces cancel: $N = mg$.
2. The only horizontal force on the particle is the tension $T$ along the string, directed towards the peg (centre).
3. So the net force towards the centre is $F_{net} = T$, which supplies the centripetal force $T = \frac{mv^{2}}{l}$; this is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Tension in String in Circular Motion · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 156](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q156_61bc0cc7_29102026.png?v=61bc0cc7e306)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 156](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q156_f44f032e_29102026.png?v=f44f032e2848)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** On a smooth horizontal table the string's tension is the only horizontal force on the particle, so the net force towards the centre IS $T$, and $mv^2/l$ is what that net force equals, not a second force
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  The centripetal requirement $mv^2/l$ is entered as a second force opposing the tension, so the net force is written $T - mv^2/l$; that is the force balance of the rotating frame, where the pseudo-force appears, and the question is asked in the frame where only the string pulls
- **Why:** This question requires applying Newton's second law to a case with a single horizontal force, demonstrated by 'the other end is connected to a small peg on a smooth horizontal table' and 'the net force on the particle (directed towards center)'.
- **Note:** Options 2 and 3 are the same claim in two forms: for the motion described, $T = mv^2/l$, so $T - mv^2/l$ IS zero. Both are therefore the pseudo-force balance, one written as an expression and one evaluated, which is why both carry routes and neither is a filler.

## Q157

Physics · Waves

The two nearest harmonics of a tube closed at one end and open at other end are $220\,\mathrm{Hz}$ and $260\,\mathrm{Hz}$. What is the fundamental frequency of the system?

- **(1)** $20\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (2) $30\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (3) $40\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (4) $10\,\mathrm{Hz}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Two successive frequencies of closed pipe
2. $\frac{nv}{4l} = 220$ ...(i)
3. $\frac{(n + 2)v}{4l} = 260$ ...(ii)
4. Dividing (ii) by (i), we get
5. $\frac{n + 2}{n} = \frac{260}{220} = \frac{13}{11}$
6. $11n + 22 = 13n$
7. $n = 11$
8. So, $11\frac{v}{4l} = 220$
9. $\frac{v}{4l} = 20$
10. So fundamental frequency is $20\,\mathrm{Hz}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A tube closed at one end supports only odd harmonics: $f = (2n-1)f_0$, i.e. $f_0, 3f_0, 5f_0, \ldots$.
2. Two nearest (consecutive) harmonics therefore differ by $2f_0$: $260 - 220 = 2f_0$.
3. $2f_0 = 40\,\mathrm{Hz} \Rightarrow f_0 = 20\,\mathrm{Hz}$.
4. Check: $\frac{220}{20} = 11$ and $\frac{260}{20} = 13$, both odd, so $f_0 = 20\,\mathrm{Hz}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Organ Pipe · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 157](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q157_1a89886e_29102026.png?v=1a89886e5c64)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 157](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q157_cb9e563c_29102026.png?v=cb9e563c2349)

- **Concept:** A pipe closed at one end sounds only odd harmonics, so neighbouring harmonics differ by $2f_0$ and the fundamental is half the spacing
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 3  
  The spacing of neighbouring harmonics, $260 - 220 = 40$ Hz, is taken as the fundamental; that holds for a pipe open at both ends, where every integer multiple sounds, and the stem's closed end leaves only the odd ones, spaced $2f_0$ apart
- **Why:** This question requires getting the fundamental from the spacing of two harmonics of a closed pipe, demonstrated by 'The two nearest harmonics of a tube closed at one end and open at other end are $220\,\mathrm{Hz}$ and $260\,\mathrm{Hz}$'.

## Q158

Physics · Modern Physics

The photoelectric threshold wavelength of silver is $3250 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$. The velocity of the electron ejected from a silver surface by ultraviolet light of wavelength $2536 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$ is (Given $h = 4.14 \times 10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eVs}$ and $c = 3 \times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$)

- **(1)** $\approx 0.6 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$
- (2) $\approx 61 \times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$
- (3) $\approx 0.3 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$
- **(4)** $\approx 6 \times 10^{5}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1, 4) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\lambda_0 = 3250 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$
2. $\lambda = 2536 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$
3. $\phi = \frac{1242\,\mathrm{eV\text{-}nm}}{325\,\mathrm{nm}} = 3.82\,\mathrm{eV}$
4. $h\nu = \frac{1242\,\mathrm{eV\text{-}nm}}{253.6\,\mathrm{nm}} = 4.89\,\mathrm{eV}$
5. $\mathrm{KE}_{\mathrm{max}} = (4.89 - 3.82)\,\mathrm{eV} = 1.077\,\mathrm{eV}$
6. $\frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = 1.077 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}$
7. $v = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 1.077 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}}{9.1 \times 10^{-31}}}$
8. $v = 0.6 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{m/s}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $hc = 4.14 \times 10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \times 3 \times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} = 1.242 \times 10^{-6}\,\mathrm{eV\,m}$.
2. Work function: $\phi_0 = \frac{hc}{\lambda_0} = \frac{1.242 \times 10^{-6}}{3250 \times 10^{-10}} \approx 3.822\,\mathrm{eV}$.
3. Photon energy: $E = \frac{hc}{\lambda} = \frac{1.242 \times 10^{-6}}{2536 \times 10^{-10}} \approx 4.898\,\mathrm{eV}$.
4. $K_{max} = E - \phi_0 = 4.898 - 3.822 \approx 1.076\,\mathrm{eV}$.
5. $K_{max} = 1.076 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J} \approx 1.722 \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J}$.
6. $v = \sqrt{\frac{2K_{max}}{m_e}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 1.722 \times 10^{-19}}{9.1 \times 10^{-31}}} = \sqrt{3.78 \times 10^{11}} \approx 6.15 \times 10^{5}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$.
7. $6.15 \times 10^{5}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \approx 0.6 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, which is option $1$ (and equally option $4$, $6 \times 10^{5}$).

**Subtopic:** Photoelectric Effect · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 158](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q158_d1387a8d_29102026.png?v=d1387a8dae52)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 158](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q158_ee31c5bc_29102026.png?v=ee31c5bc65a6)

<!--ecat-->

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Einstein's equation in eV: the photon energy $hc/\lambda$ less the threshold energy $hc/\lambda_0$ is the electron's kinetic energy, and the speed follows from $\tfrac{1}{2}mv^2$ after converting eV to joules
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  The energies come out right — 4.90 eV of photon against 3.82 eV of threshold, leaving 1.08 eV — and a factor of 100 is then lost in the eV-to-joule conversion, which the square root turns into one decade in the speed: the printed 61 $\times 10^3$ carries the key's own digits, 6.1, one decade down
- **Why:** This question requires computing the ejected electron's speed from the photon and threshold energies, demonstrated by 'The photoelectric threshold wavelength of silver is $3250\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$' against light of '$2536\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$' with the constants supplied.
- **Note:** Options 1 and 4 are the same number written two ways ($0.6\times10^6$ and $6\times10^5$ m/s), which is why the answer line reads 'Answer (1 & 4)* Both answers are correct' and accepted_answers holds both. Neither carries a tag, so only option 3 appears under other_options. The duplication is in the printed paper and its own key, not in our record. The pattern classifier returned statement_options; the printed options are single numerical values, so the shape recorded here is mcq. My own working gives $6.15\times10^5$ m/s, which agrees with both accepted options.

## Q159

Physics · Electromagnetic Induction

Figure shows a circuit contains three identical resistors with resistance $R = 9.0\,\Omega$ each, two identical inductors with inductance $L = 2.0\,\mathrm{mH}$ each, and an ideal battery with emf $\varepsilon = 18\,\mathrm{V}$. The current '$i$' through the battery just after the switch closed is

![printed figure: Battery with switch feeding L-R branch, R branch, and R in series with parallel L and C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q159_fd678334_29102026.svg?v=fd678334411d)

*Battery with switch feeding L-R branch, R branch, and R in series with parallel L and C*

![drawn: Battery with switch feeding L-R branch, R branch, and R in series with parallel L and C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q159_question_492e7b7c_29102026.svg?v=492e7b7c82f1)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $0.2\,\mathrm{A}$
- **(2)** $2\,\mathrm{A}$
- (3) $0$ ampere
- (4) $2\,\mathrm{mA}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Battery ε across three parallel branches: L1 in series with R1, R2 alone, and R3 in series with L2 that has C in parallel](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q159_sol_1_130bc40e_29102026.svg?v=130bc40eb11e)

*Battery ε across three parallel branches: L1 in series with R1, R2 alone, and R3 in series with L2 that has C in parallel*

![drawn: Battery ε across three parallel branches: L1 in series with R1, R2 alone, and R3 in series with L2 that has C in parallel](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q159_solution_1_aaeba7c0_29102026.svg?v=aaeba7c0fd73)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. At $t = 0$, no current flows through $R_1$ and $R_3$

![printed figure: Battery of emf ε in a single loop with resistor R2, current i clockwise](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q159_sol_2_6870cc22_29102026.svg?v=6870cc22ef67)

*Battery of emf ε in a single loop with resistor R2, current i clockwise*

![drawn: Battery of emf ε in a single loop with resistor R2, current i clockwise](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q159_solution_2_85cbbaf8_29102026.svg?v=85cbbaf88fd7)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

2. $i = \frac{\varepsilon}{R_2}$
3. $= \frac{18}{9}$
4. $= 2\,\mathrm{A}$
5. Note : Not correctly framed but the best option out of given is (2).

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Just after the switch closes, an inductor opposes any sudden change in current, so each inductor behaves as an open circuit at $t=0^+$.
2. Branch with $L$ in series with $R$: the open inductor blocks it, so $i_1 = 0$.
3. Branch with $R$ alone: $i_2 = \frac{\varepsilon}{R} = \frac{18\,\mathrm{V}}{9.0\,\Omega} = 2\,\mathrm{A}$.
4. Branch with $R$ in series with $L \parallel C$: taking the stem's components only (no capacitor), the open inductor blocks it, so $i_3 = 0$.
5. $i = i_1 + i_2 + i_3 = 0 + 2 + 0 = 2\,\mathrm{A}$, which is option $2$.
6. Caveat: if the drawn capacitor $C$ is uncharged it acts as a short at $t=0^+$, giving $i_3 = \frac{18}{9} = 2\,\mathrm{A}$ and $i = 4\,\mathrm{A}$, which matches no option.

**Subtopic:** LR Circuits · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 159](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q159_799f160a_29102026.png?v=799f160a00de)

- **Asks:** **Figure reading** (`read_diagram`) — read a figure, graph or drawn structure and answer from it
- **Concept:** An inductor keeps its current at zero through the instant a switch closes, so just after closing only the branch with no inductor in it carries current and the battery supplies $\varepsilon/R$
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 3  
  Zero is the true current at that instant — through each inductor branch, which is what 'the current cannot change suddenly through an inductor' delivers — but the stem asks for the current through the BATTERY, and the battery also feeds the branch that holds only a resistor
- **Why:** This question requires reading which branch of the drawn network can carry current at the instant of closing, demonstrated by 'The current $i$ through the battery just after the switch closed' with the figure placing an inductor in series in some branches and a resistor alone in another.
- **Note:** The figure supplied to this step carries a capacitor $C$ in parallel with the second inductor (netlist: $C$ and $L2$ both between nodes N and G), which the stem does not mention — it lists 'three identical resistors... two identical inductors... and an ideal battery'. With an uncharged capacitor that third branch conducts at $t = 0^+$, which would make the battery current $2 + 2 = 4$ A, and 4 A is not among the options; the printed key, 2 A, is what follows when only the resistor-alone branch conducts. The answer line itself is printed 'Answer (2*)' with an asterisk. Tagged as printed; a reviewer should check the capacitor against the printed figure and whether the official solution treated it as non-conducting.

## Q160

Physics · Thermal Properties of Matter

A spherical black body with a radius of $12\,\mathrm{cm}$ radiates $450\,\mathrm{watt}$ power at $500\,\mathrm{K}$. If the radius were halved and the temperature doubled, the power radiated in watt would be

- (1) $450$
- (2) $1000$
- **(3)** $1800$
- (4) $225$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Rate of power loss $r \propto R^{2}T^{4}$
2. $\frac{r_1}{r_2} = \frac{R_1^{2}T_1^{4}}{R_2^{2}T_2^{4}}$
3. $= 4 \times \frac{1}{16}$
4. $\frac{450}{r_2} = \frac{1}{4}$
5. $r_2 = 1800\,\mathrm{watt}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. By the Stefan-Boltzmann law for a black sphere, $P = \sigma (4\pi R^2) T^4$, so $P \propto R^2 T^4$.
2. $\frac{P'}{P} = \left(\frac{R'}{R}\right)^2 \left(\frac{T'}{T}\right)^4 = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 (2)^4$.
3. $\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 (2)^4 = \frac{1}{4} \times 16 = 4$.
4. $P' = 4 \times 450\,\mathrm{W} = 1800\,\mathrm{W}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Radiation Laws · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 160](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q160_f5481404_29102026.png?v=f54814040e3d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 160](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q160_3006baec_29102026.png?v=3006baec0dd2)

---

<!--ecat-->

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A black body radiates $P = \sigma A T^4$, so the power follows the square of the radius and the FOURTH power of the temperature: halving $r$ divides by 4 and doubling $T$ multiplies by 16
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  With the temperature carried as a square rather than a fourth power, the area's factor of $1/4$ and the temperature's factor of 4 cancel exactly, so the power is reported unchanged — and 450 W is the number already printed in the stem, so 'unchanged' needs no arithmetic at all
- **Why:** This question requires computing the new radiated power from the two changes, demonstrated by 'If the radius were halved and the temperature doubled, the power radiated in watt would be' with powers in watts as the options.

## Q161

Physics · Rotational Motion

A rope is wound around a hollow cylinder of mass $3\,\mathrm{kg}$ and radius $40\,\mathrm{cm}$. What is the angular acceleration of the cylinder if the rope is pulled with a force of $30\,\mathrm{N}$?

- (1) $0.25\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}$
- **(2)** $25\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}$
- (3) $5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- (4) $25\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Rope wound on a hollow cylinder of radius 40 cm pulled down with F = 30 N](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q161_sol_1_3e9d0f7a_29102026.svg?v=3e9d0f7ae283)

*Rope wound on a hollow cylinder of radius 40 cm pulled down with F = 30 N*

![drawn: Rope wound on a hollow cylinder of radius 40 cm pulled down with F = 30 N](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q161_solution_1_ccd4963f_29102026.svg?v=ccd4963f8c1a)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. $\tau = I\,\alpha$
2. $F \times R = MR^{2}\alpha$
3. $30 \times 0.4 = 3 \times (0.4)^{2}\,\alpha$
4. $12 = 3 \times 0.16\,\alpha$
5. $400 = 16\,\alpha$
6. $\alpha = 25\,\mathrm{rad/s^{2}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Torque about the axis from the rope: $\tau = F R = 30 \times 0.40 = 12\,\mathrm{N\,m}$.
2. Moment of inertia of a thin hollow cylinder about its axis: $I = M R^2 = 3 \times (0.40)^2 = 0.48\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$.
3. $\alpha = \frac{\tau}{I} = \frac{12}{0.48} = 25\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Torque of a Force About the Axis of Rotation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 161](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q161_8fd65fa9_29102026.png?v=8fd65fa9d063)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 161](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q161_f679478c_29102026.png?v=f679478c21a0)

- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  The radius is left in centimetres: $\alpha = F/mR = 30/(3\times40) = 0.25$, which is exactly the printed value, where $40\,\mathrm{cm} = 0.4\,\mathrm{m}$ gives $30/(3\times0.4) = 25$
- **Why:** This question requires computing an angular acceleration from a torque and a moment of inertia, demonstrated by 'What is the angular acceleration of the cylinder if the rope is pulled with a force of $30\,\mathrm{N}$?' with the mass and the radius in centimetres supplied.

## Q162

Physics · Rotational Motion

Two discs of same moment of inertia rotating about their regular axis passing through centre and perpendicular to the plane of disc with angular velocities $\omega_1$ and $\omega_2$. They are brought into contact face to face coinciding the axis of rotation. The expression for loss of energy during this process is

- **(1)** $\frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
- (2) $I(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
- (3) $\frac{I}{8}(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
- (4) $\frac{1}{2}I(\omega_1 + \omega_2)^2$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\Delta \mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}\frac{I_1 I_2}{I_1 + I_2}(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
2. $= \frac{1}{2}\frac{I^2}{(2I)}(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
3. $= \frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. No external torque acts, so angular momentum is conserved: $I\omega_1 + I\omega_2 = 2I\omega$.
2. $\omega = \frac{\omega_1 + \omega_2}{2}$.
3. Initial kinetic energy: $K_i = \frac{1}{2}I\omega_1^2 + \frac{1}{2}I\omega_2^2$.
4. Final kinetic energy: $K_f = \frac{1}{2}(2I)\left(\frac{\omega_1+\omega_2}{2}\right)^2 = \frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1+\omega_2)^2$.
5. $\Delta K = K_i - K_f = \frac{1}{4}I\left[2\omega_1^2 + 2\omega_2^2 - (\omega_1+\omega_2)^2\right]$.
6. $\Delta K = \frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1^2 - 2\omega_1\omega_2 + \omega_2^2) = \frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Conservation of Angular Momentum, Kinetic Energy of a Rigid Body Rotating About a Given Axis · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 162](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q162_132c1f07_29102026.png?v=132c1f07d35d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 162](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q162_28f55b42_29102026.png?v=28f55b428662)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Angular momentum is conserved when the discs couple, so $\omega_f = (\omega_1+\omega_2)/2$ and the energy lost is $\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{I_1I_2}{I_1+I_2}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2 = \tfrac{1}{4}I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  The loss is written as $\tfrac{1}{2}I_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$ with $I_{\mathrm{eff}}$ taken as the SUM of the two moments, $2I$, instead of the harmonic combination $I_1I_2/(I_1+I_2) = I/2$, which gives $I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$ — four times the key
- **Why:** This question requires deriving the energy lost when two rotating discs couple, demonstrated by 'They are brought into contact face to face coinciding the axis of rotation. The expression for loss of energy during this process is'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q163

Physics · Thermodynamics

A gas mixture consists of 2 moles of $\mathrm{O_2}$ and 4 moles of $\mathrm{Ar}$ at temperature $T$. Neglecting all vibrational modes, the total internal energy of the system is

- (1) $15\,RT$
- (2) $9\,RT$
- **(3)** $11\,RT$
- (4) $4\,RT$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $U = n_1\frac{f_1}{2}RT + n_2\frac{f_2}{2}RT$
2. $= 2\times\frac{5}{2}RT + 4\times\frac{3}{2}RT$
3. $= 5\,RT + 6\,RT$
4. $U = 11\,RT$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Neglecting vibration, $\mathrm{O_2}$ (diatomic) has $f = 5$ degrees of freedom and $\mathrm{Ar}$ (monatomic) has $f = 3$.
2. Internal energy per mole is $U_m = \frac{f}{2}RT$.
3. $U_{\mathrm{O_2}} = 2 \times \frac{5}{2}RT = 5RT$.
4. $U_{\mathrm{Ar}} = 4 \times \frac{3}{2}RT = 6RT$.
5. $U = 5RT + 6RT = 11RT$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Specific Heat Capacity of Monoatomic, Diatomic, and Polyatomic Gases · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 163](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q163_9984f194_29102026.png?v=9984f194ebbc)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 163](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q163_07aa0780_29102026.png?v=07aa0780784e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Internal energy is $\tfrac{f}{2}nRT$ per gas, with $f = 3$ for a monatomic gas like argon and $f = 5$ for a diatomic gas like oxygen once vibrations are neglected
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The coefficient $\tfrac52 R$ is used for all six moles, $6\times\tfrac52 RT = 15RT$; argon is monatomic and carries $\tfrac32 R$, so only the two moles of oxygen take $\tfrac52 R$
- **Why:** This question requires summing the internal energies of two gases with different degrees of freedom, demonstrated by 'consists of 2 moles of $\mathrm{O_2}$ and 4 moles of $\mathrm{Ar}$' with 'Neglecting all vibrational modes'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q164

Physics · Mechanical Properties of Solids

The bulk modulus of a spherical object is ‘$B$’. If it is subjected to uniform pressure ‘$p$’, the fractional decrease in radius is

- (1) $\frac{B}{3p}$
- (2) $\frac{3p}{B}$
- **(3)** $\frac{p}{3B}$
- (4) $\frac{p}{B}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $B = \frac{p}{\left(\frac{\Delta V}{V}\right)}$
2. $\frac{\Delta V}{V} = \frac{p}{B}$
3. $3\frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{B}$
4. $\frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{3B}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Bulk modulus definition: $B = \frac{p}{\Delta V / V}$, so $\frac{\Delta V}{V} = \frac{p}{B}$.
2. For a sphere $V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3$, so $\frac{\Delta V}{V} = 3\frac{\Delta r}{r}$.
3. $3\frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{B} \Rightarrow \frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{3B}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Relation Between Density, Pressure and Bulk Modulus · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 164](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q164_deea86ee_29102026.png?v=deea86eef6a0)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 164](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q164_5ee732e1_29102026.png?v=5ee732e105e2)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Bulk modulus gives the volume strain, $\Delta V/V = p/B$, and for a sphere $V \propto r^3$, so the radius strain is a third of it: $\Delta r/r = p/3B$
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 4  
  $p/B$ is the true fractional decrease in VOLUME, straight out of the definition of the bulk modulus, and the stem asks for the fractional decrease in radius, which is a third of it
- **Why:** This question requires turning a volume strain into a radius strain, demonstrated by 'The bulk modulus of a spherical object is B' against 'the fractional decrease in radius is'.

## Q165

Physics · Rotational Motion

Which of the following statements are correct?

- **((a))** Centre of mass of a body always coincides with the centre of gravity of the body.
- **((b))** Centre of mass of a body is the point at which the total gravitational torque on the body is zero
- **((c))** A couple on a body produce both translational and rotational motion in a body.
- **((d))** Mechanical advantage greater than one means that small effort can be used to lift a large load.

- (1) (a) and (b)
- (2) (b) and (c)
- (3) (c) and (d)
- **(4)** (b) and (d)

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Centre of mass may or may not coincide with centre of gravity.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. (a): $\vec{r}_{CG} = \vec{r}_{CM}$ only when $\vec{g}$ is uniform over the body, so 'always coincides' is false.
2. (b): In uniform $\vec{g}$, $\vec{\tau}_{CM} = \sum_i \vec{r}_i' \times m_i\vec{g} = \left(\sum_i m_i\vec{r}_i'\right) \times \vec{g} = 0$ since $\sum_i m_i\vec{r}_i' = 0$ about the CM, so (b) is taken as true.
3. (c): A couple is two equal and opposite forces, so $\vec{F}_{net} = 0$ and there is no translational acceleration; it produces rotation only, so (c) is false.
4. (d): $MA = \frac{\text{Load}}{\text{Effort}} > 1 \Rightarrow \text{Effort} < \text{Load}$, so (d) is true.
5. True statements are (b) and (d), which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Equilibrium of a Rigid Body · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q165_b7e5ef9a_29102026.png?v=b7e5ef9a43fa)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q165_75ea3575_29102026.png?v=75ea3575de14)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** The centre of gravity coincides with the centre of mass only while $g$ is the same across the whole body, and a couple has zero net force so it turns a body without moving it along
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 1  
  The textbook sentence that the centre of gravity coincides with the centre of mass carries the condition "in a uniform gravitational field", and statement (a) prints it as "always coincides"; a student holding the sentence without its condition accepts (a) and pairs it with the true (b), which is option 1.
- **Why:** This question requires each lettered claim to be checked against the line the textbook states about it, demonstrated by statement (a)'s "always coincides with the centre of gravity" differing from the printed sentence only in dropping its uniform-field condition, with no quantity produced and no new case supplied.
- **Note:** Statement (b) attributes to the centre of mass the property the textbook states of the centre of gravity, "the point at which the total gravitational torque on the body is zero". The key accepts (b) and rejects (a), and (b) reads as true only under the same uniform-field assumption whose absence makes (a) false. Tagged as printed; recorded here because a reviewer may want to look at the wording of (b).
<!--/ecat-->

## Q166

Physics · Gravitation

The acceleration due to gravity at a height $1\,\mathrm{km}$ above the earth is the same as at a depth $d$ below the surface of earth. Then

- (1) $d = 1\,\mathrm{km}$
- (2) $d = \frac{3}{2}\,\mathrm{km}$
- **(3)** $d = 2\,\mathrm{km}$
- (4) $d = \frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{km}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Above earth surface
2. $g' = g\left(1 - \frac{2h}{R_e}\right)$
3. $\Delta g' = g\,\frac{2h}{R_e} \quad \ldots(1)$
4. Below earth surface
5. $g' = g\left(1 - \frac{d}{R_e}\right)$
6. $\Delta g = g\,\frac{d}{R_e} \quad \ldots(2)$
7. From (1) & (2)
8. $d = 2h$
9. $d = 2 \times 1\,\mathrm{km}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At height $h \ll R$: $g_h = g\left(1 - \frac{2h}{R}\right)$.
2. At depth $d$: $g_d = g\left(1 - \frac{d}{R}\right)$.
3. Setting $g_h = g_d$: $\frac{2h}{R} = \frac{d}{R} \Rightarrow d = 2h$.
4. $d = 2 \times 1\,\mathrm{km} = 2\,\mathrm{km}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Variation in g Due to Height and Rotation, Variation in g Due to Depth Inside the Earth · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 166](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q166_9cff7526_29102026.png?v=9cff75260848)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 166](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q166_245f2663_29102026.png?v=245f26630d09)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Above the surface $g$ falls as $1-2h/R$ and below it as $1-d/R$, so the same $g$ is reached at twice the depth: $d = 2h$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  One law is used for both directions — $1-h/R$ above and $1-d/R$ below — so the two distances come out equal and $d = 1$ km; above the surface the fall is twice as fast, $1-2h/R$
- **Why:** This question requires equating the two expressions for $g$ and solving for the depth, demonstrated by 'The acceleration due to gravity at a height $1\,\mathrm{km}$ above the earth is the same as at a depth $d$ below the surface of earth'.

## Q167

Physics · Laws of Motion

Two blocks $A$ and $B$ of masses $3m$ and $m$ respectively are connected by a massless and inextensible string. The whole system is suspended by a massless spring as shown in figure. The magnitudes of acceleration of $A$ and $B$ immediately after the string is cut, are respectively

![printed figure: Block A (3m) hung from a ceiling by a spring, block B (m) hung from A by a string](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q167_d110879f_29102026.svg?v=d110879fa85b)

*Block A (3m) hung from a ceiling by a spring, block B (m) hung from A by a string*

![drawn: Block A (3m) hung from a ceiling by a spring, block B (m) hung from A by a string](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q167_question_682afe7b_29102026.svg?v=682afe7b8987)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- **(1)** $\frac{g}{3}, g$
- (2) $g, g$
- (3) $\frac{g}{3}, \frac{g}{3}$
- (4) $g, \frac{g}{3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Free-body diagram of block 3m: kx upward, T and 3mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q167_sol_1_bc244d8b_29102026.svg?v=bc244d8b2134)

*Free-body diagram of block 3m: kx upward, T and 3mg downward*

![drawn: Free-body diagram of block 3m: kx upward, T and 3mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q167_solution_1_ceb414dd_29102026.svg?v=ceb414dddd50)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. Before the string is cut
2. $kx = T + 3mg$ ...(1)
3. $T = mg$ ...(2)

![printed figure: Free-body diagram of block m: tension T upward, weight mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q167_sol_2_da60d500_29102026.svg?v=da60d500b964)

*Free-body diagram of block m: tension T upward, weight mg downward*

![drawn: Free-body diagram of block m: tension T upward, weight mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q167_solution_2_953b932a_29102026.svg?v=953b932a2f63)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

4. $\Rightarrow kx = 4mg$
5. After the string is cut, $T = 0$

![printed figure: Free-body diagrams of block 3m (kx up, 3mg down) and block m (mg down, a = g) after the string is cut](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q167_sol_3_7cf4329a_29102026.svg?v=7cf4329a7ac4)

*Free-body diagrams of block 3m (kx up, 3mg down) and block m (mg down, a = g) after the string is cut*

![drawn: Free-body diagrams of block 3m (kx up, 3mg down) and block m (mg down, a = g) after the string is cut](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q167_solution_3_d5f7ed81_29102026.svg?v=d5f7ed8115ca)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

6. $a = \frac{kx - 3mg}{3m}$
7. $a = \frac{4mg - 3mg}{3m}$
8. $a = \frac{g}{3} \uparrow$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Before the cut the spring holds both blocks in equilibrium, so the spring force is $F_s = (3m + m)g = 4mg$.
2. A spring cannot change its extension instantly, so just after the string is cut $F_s = 4mg$ still acts on $A$.
3. Net force on $A$: $F_{net} = 4mg - 3mg = mg$ upward.
4. $a_A = \frac{mg}{3m} = \frac{g}{3}$.
5. $B$ is acted on only by gravity once the string is cut, so $a_B = g$.
6. The magnitudes are $\frac{g}{3}, g$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Block and Spring Problem · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q167_77f8c94f_29102026.png?v=77f8c94f32f0)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q167_b0f6854f_29102026.png?v=b0f6854fdc24)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A spring's force cannot change in the instant a string is cut, so the spring still pulls up with $4mg$: block A has a net $mg$ upward and accelerates at $g/3$, while block B, now held by nothing, falls at $g$
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  Cutting the string is read as releasing both blocks, so both fall at $g$; the stem has A 'suspended by a massless spring', and the spring is still stretched to carry $4mg$ at that instant because its length has not yet changed
- **Why:** This question requires both accelerations at the instant of cutting, demonstrated by 'The magnitudes of acceleration of A and B immediately after the string is cut, are respectively' with each option printing a pair.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a drawn spec and settles the arrangement the answer depends on: a hatched ceiling, a spring down to the block labelled A with 3m, then a straight line down to the smaller block labelled B with m. The spec's print match is lower here (73 per cent of its lines on the printed ones), but every label needed is present.

## Q168

Physics · Modern Physics

The ratio of wavelengths of the last line of Balmer series and the last line of Lyman series is

- (1) $1$
- **(2)** $4$
- (3) $0.5$
- (4) $2$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. For last Balmer series
2. $\frac{1}{\lambda_b} = R\left[\frac{1}{2^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right]$
3. $\lambda_b = \frac{4}{R}$
4. For last Lyman series
5. $\frac{1}{\lambda_l} = R\left[\frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right]$
6. $\lambda_l = \frac{1}{R}$
7. $\frac{\lambda_b}{\lambda_l} = \frac{\frac{4}{R}}{\frac{1}{R}}$
8. $\frac{\lambda_b}{\lambda_l} = 4$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The last line of a series is the series limit, a transition from $n_i = \infty$ to the series' final level $n_f$.
2. Balmer series limit ($n_f = 2$): $\frac{1}{\lambda_B} = R\left(\frac{1}{2^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right) = \frac{R}{4}$, so $\lambda_B = \frac{4}{R}$.
3. Lyman series limit ($n_f = 1$): $\frac{1}{\lambda_L} = R\left(\frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right) = R$, so $\lambda_L = \frac{1}{R}$.
4. $\frac{\lambda_B}{\lambda_L} = \frac{4/R}{1/R} = 4$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Spectral Lines of Hydrogen Atom · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q168_74fdc266_29102026.png?v=74fdc266f476)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q168_3b654449_29102026.png?v=3b6544499e98)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** The last line of a series is its limit, $n \to \infty$, so $1/\lambda = R/n^2$ and the limiting wavelength goes as the SQUARE of the lower level: $\lambda_{\mathrm{Balmer}}/\lambda_{\mathrm{Lyman}} = 2^2/1^2 = 4$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  The lower levels 2 and 1 are carried into the ratio to the first power, giving 2, because the limiting wavelength is taken as proportional to $n$ rather than to $n^2$
- **Why:** This question requires the factor between two series limits, demonstrated by 'The ratio of wavelengths of the last line of Balmer series and the last line of Lyman series' with bare numbers as the options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q169

Physics · Ray Optics

A beam of light from a source $L$ is incident normally on a plane mirror fixed at a certain distance $x$ from the source. The beam is reflected back as a spot on a scale placed just above the source $L$. When the mirror is rotated through a small angle $\theta$, the spot of the light is found to move through a distance $y$ on the scale. The angle $\theta$ is given by

- (1) $\frac{y}{x}$
- (2) $\frac{x}{2y}$
- (3) $\frac{x}{y}$
- **(4)** $\frac{y}{2x}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. When mirror is rotated by $\theta$ angle reflected ray will be rotated by $2\theta$.

![printed figure: Reflected beam turned through 2θ over distance x moves the spot by y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q169_sol_1_d38b6de6_29102026.svg?v=d38b6de62e45)

*Reflected beam turned through 2θ over distance x moves the spot by y*

![drawn: Reflected beam turned through 2θ over distance x moves the spot by y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q169_solution_1_c3164dbd_29102026.svg?v=c3164dbdb6fd)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

2. $\frac{y}{x} = 2\theta$
3. $\theta = \frac{y}{2x}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Rotating a plane mirror through $\theta$ rotates the reflected ray through $2\theta$.
2. The reflected ray travels distance $x$ back to the scale, so $\tan 2\theta = \frac{y}{x}$.
3. For a small angle, $\tan 2\theta \approx 2\theta$, so $2\theta = \frac{y}{x}$.
4. $\theta = \frac{y}{2x}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Reflection at a Smooth Surface · **Difficulty:** hard (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q169_97078350_29102026.png?v=97078350e10d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q169_d45c8824_29102026.png?v=d45c8824508a)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Turning a mirror by $\theta$ turns the reflected ray by $2\theta$, so the spot at distance $x$ moves $y = 2\theta x$ and $\theta = y/2x$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The spot's shift is set against the mirror's own rotation, $y = \theta x$, giving $\theta = y/x$; the reflected ray turns through twice the mirror's angle, so the arc is $2\theta x$
- **Why:** This question requires the mirror's rotation from the spot's displacement, demonstrated by 'When the mirror is rotated through a small angle $\theta$, the spot of the light is found to move through a distance $y$ on the scale'.

## Q170

Physics · Oscillations

A spring of force constant $k$ is cut into lengths of ratio $1 : 2 : 3$. They are connected in series and the new force constant is $k'$. Then they are connected in parallel and force constant is $k''$. Then $k' : k''$ is

- (1) $1 : 9$
- **(2)** $1 : 11$
- (3) $1 : 14$
- (4) $1 : 6$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Spring constant $\propto \frac{1}{\text{length}}$
2. $k \propto \frac{1}{l}$
3. i.e, $k_{1} = 6k$
4. $k_{2} = 3k$
5. $k_{3} = 2k$
6. In series
7. $\frac{1}{k'} = \frac{1}{6k} + \frac{1}{3k} + \frac{1}{2k}$
8. $\frac{1}{k'} = \frac{6}{6k}$
9. $k' = k$
10. $k'' = 6k + 3k + 2k$
11. $k'' = 11k$
12. $\frac{k'}{k''} = \frac{1}{11}$ i.e $k' : k'' = 1 : 11$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a spring, $k \propto \frac{1}{l}$, so a piece of length $l$ cut from a spring of length $L$ has constant $k\frac{L}{l}$.
2. The pieces have lengths $\frac{L}{6}, \frac{2L}{6}, \frac{3L}{6}$, so their constants are $k_1 = 6k$, $k_2 = 3k$, $k_3 = 2k$.
3. Series: $\frac{1}{k'} = \frac{1}{6k} + \frac{1}{3k} + \frac{1}{2k} = \frac{1+2+3}{6k} = \frac{1}{k}$, so $k' = k$.
4. Parallel: $k'' = 6k + 3k + 2k = 11k$.
5. $k' : k'' = k : 11k = 1 : 11$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Combination of Springs · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q170_1da50792_29102026.png?v=1da5079225ee)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q170_a7da7bec_29102026.png?v=a7da7bec72e9)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A shorter piece is stiffer, $k \propto 1/L$, so cutting in $1:2:3$ gives $6k$, $3k$ and $2k$: in series they rebuild the original spring, $k' = k$, and in parallel they add to $k'' = 11k$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  The three pieces put back in series are the original spring, so $k' = k$ — a sound shortcut — and the parallel sum is then taken with the stiffnesses read straight off the length ratio as $k$, $2k$ and $3k$, giving $6k$ and the printed $1:6$
- **Why:** This question requires computing both the series and the parallel constants of the cut pieces, demonstrated by 'cut into lengths of ratio $1:2:3$' with 'Then $k' : k''$ is'.
- **Note:** The ratio is unusually robust: taking the stiffnesses as proportional to length rather than inversely, but applying both combination rules consistently, still yields $1:11$, since the quotient of the harmonic and arithmetic combinations is unchanged. The trap therefore needs the series shortcut to be used alongside the inverted proportionality, which is how the route is written.

## Q171

Physics · Electrostatics

The diagrams below show regions of equipotentials. A positive charge is moved from $A$ to $B$ in each diagram.

![printed figure: Four equipotential patterns (a)-(d), 10 V to 40 V, with points A and B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q171_be2db089_29102026.svg?v=be2db0893f7e)

*Four equipotential patterns (a)-(d), 10 V to 40 V, with points A and B*

![drawn: Four equipotential patterns (a)-(d), 10 V to 40 V, with points A and B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q171_question_663db00f_29102026.svg?v=663db00f314f)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- **(1)** In all the four cases the work done is the same.
- (2) Minimum work is required to move $q$ in figure (a).
- (3) Maximum work is required to move $q$ in figure (b).
- (4) Maximum work is required to move $q$ in figure (c).

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Work done $w = q\Delta V$
2. $\Delta V$ is same in all the cases so work is done will be same in all the cases.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure, in each of (a), (b), (c) and (d) point $A$ is on the $10\,\text{V}$ equipotential and point $B$ is on the $40\,\text{V}$ equipotential.
2. Work done by the external agent in moving charge $q$ is $W = q(V_B - V_A)$, which depends only on the end-point potentials, not on the path or the spacing/shape of the equipotentials.
3. $W = q(40 - 10) = 30q$ in every figure, so the work done is the same in all four cases, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Equipotential Surfaces · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q171_7bc96bed_29102026.png?v=7bc96bedde8e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q171_ec8c8b0f_29102026.png?v=ec8c8b0fe06d)

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Figure reading** (`read_diagram`) — read a figure, graph or drawn structure and answer from it
- **Concept:** Work in an electrostatic field is $q$ times the potential difference between the endpoints, so with A on the 10 V surface and B on the 40 V surface in every diagram, the work is the same in all four whatever the spacing or shape of the surfaces
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  Work is taken as force times distance, so the diagram with the longest A-to-B gap wins; in (c) the surfaces are unevenly spaced and A and B are the furthest apart of the four, so (c) reads as the costliest
- **Why:** This question requires reading which equipotential each endpoint lies on, demonstrated by 'The diagrams below show regions of equipotentials. A positive charge is moved from A to B in each diagram' with options comparing the work across the four figures.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a drawn spec and all four panels read the same way: the surfaces are labelled 10, 20, 30 and 40 V, with A on the 10 V surface and B on the 40 V surface in each of (a), (b), (c) and (d), so every journey is across 30 V. The A-to-B separations do differ on the page - 76, 78, 97 and 83 units for (a) to (d) - which is what the three wrong options trade on. The pattern classifier returned statement_options; the stem prints no question sentence at all and each option is a full claim to be judged, so the shape recorded here is statements.

## Q172

Physics · Electrostatics

Suppose the charge of a proton and an electron differ slightly. One of them is $-e$, the other is $(e + \Delta e)$. If the net of electrostatic force and gravitational force between two hydrogen atoms placed at a distance $d$ (much greater than atomic size) apart is zero, then $\Delta e$ is of the order of [Given mass of hydrogen $m_h = 1.67 \times 10^{-27}\,\mathrm{kg}$]

- (1) $10^{-23}\,\mathrm{C}$
- **(2)** $10^{-37}\,\mathrm{C}$
- (3) $10^{-47}\,\mathrm{C}$
- (4) $10^{-20}\,\mathrm{C}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $F_e = F_g$
2. $\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\frac{\Delta e^2}{d^2} = \frac{Gm^2}{d^2}$
3. $9 \times 10^{9} (\Delta e^2) = 6.67 \times 10^{-11} \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27} \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27}$
4. $\Delta e^2 = \frac{6.67 \times 1.67 \times 1.67}{9} \times 10^{-74}$
5. $\Delta e \approx 10^{-37}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Each hydrogen atom has a proton $(e + \Delta e)$ and an electron $(-e)$, so its net charge is $\Delta e$.
2. The two atoms repel electrostatically with $F_e = \frac{k(\Delta e)^2}{d^2}$ and attract gravitationally with $F_g = \frac{G m_h^2}{d^2}$.
3. Net force zero: $\frac{k(\Delta e)^2}{d^2} = \frac{G m_h^2}{d^2}$, so $\Delta e = m_h\sqrt{\frac{G}{k}}$.
4. $\frac{G}{k} = \frac{6.67 \times 10^{-11}}{9 \times 10^{9}} \approx 7.41 \times 10^{-21}$, so $\sqrt{\frac{G}{k}} \approx 8.61 \times 10^{-11}$.
5. $\Delta e = 1.67 \times 10^{-27} \times 8.61 \times 10^{-11} \approx 1.44 \times 10^{-37}\,\mathrm{C}$, which is of the order $10^{-37}\,\mathrm{C}$, option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Coulomb's Law · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q172_74c452b6_29102026.png?v=74c452b671c4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q172_707f71de_29102026.png?v=707f71dea9a2)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Balancing the two inverse-square forces between the atoms cancels $d^2$ and leaves $\Delta e = m_h\sqrt{G\cdot4\pi\varepsilon_0} = m_h\sqrt{G/k}$, which is about $10^{-37}$ C
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The gravitational side is written with one mass instead of the product of the two, so $\Delta e = \sqrt{Gm_h/k} = 3.5\times10^{-24}$, which reads onto the printed $10^{-23}$
- **Why:** This question requires computing the charge imbalance that makes the two forces cancel, demonstrated by 'If the net of electrostatic force and gravitational force between two hydrogen atoms placed at a distance $d$... is zero, then $\Delta e$ is of the order of'.

## Q173

Physics · Units and Measurements

A physical quantity of the dimensions of length that can be formed out of $c$, $G$ and $\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}$ is [$c$ is velocity of light, $G$ is universal constant of gravitation and $e$ is charge]

- (1) $c^2\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- (2) $\frac{1}{c^2}\left[\frac{e^2}{G4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- (3) $\frac{1}{c}G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}$
- **(4)** $\frac{1}{c^2}\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Let $\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}} = A = \mathrm{ML^{3}T^{-2}}$
2. $l = C^{x}G^{y}(A)^{z}$
3. $L = [\mathrm{LT^{-1}}]^{x}\,[\mathrm{M^{-1}L^{3}T^{-2}}]^{y}\,[\mathrm{ML^{3}T^{-2}}]^{z}$
4. $-y + z = 0 \Rightarrow y = z$ ...(i)
5. $x + 3y + 3z = 1$ ...(ii)
6. $-x - 4z = 0$ ...(iii)
7. From (i), (ii) & (iii)
8. $z = y = \frac{1}{2},\ x = -2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From Coulomb's law, $\left[\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right] = [F r^2] = M L T^{-2} \cdot L^2 = M L^3 T^{-2}$.
2. From Newton's law of gravitation, $[G] = \left[\frac{F r^2}{m^2}\right] = M^{-1} L^3 T^{-2}$.
3. $\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right] = M^{-1} L^3 T^{-2} \cdot M L^3 T^{-2} = L^6 T^{-4}$.
4. $\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}} = L^3 T^{-2}$.
5. $[c^2] = L^2 T^{-2}$, so $\frac{1}{c^2}\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}} = \frac{L^3 T^{-2}}{L^2 T^{-2}} = L$.
6. Check option 2: $\left[\frac{e^2}{G\,4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}} = (M^2)^{\frac{1}{2}} = M$, and $\frac{M}{L^2 T^{-2}} = M L^{-2} T^2$, not length; options 1 and 3 give $L^5 T^{-4}$ and $L^5 T^{-3}$. So the answer is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Deducing Relation Among the Physical Quantities Using Dimensional Equation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q173_e79cba06_29102026.png?v=e79cba06aa79)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q173_c8f8d793_29102026.png?v=c8f8d7937f3f)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** $G\cdot e^2/4\pi\varepsilon_0$ has dimensions $\mathrm{m^6s^{-4}}$, so its square root is $\mathrm{m^3s^{-2}}$ and dividing by $c^2$ leaves a length
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The bracket is the right combination and is mass-free, so a student who checks only that the kilogram of $G$ cancels is left to place $c^2$, and puts it in the numerator; the root of the bracket is $\mathrm{m^3s^{-2}}$, which must be DIVIDED by $c^2$ to come down to metres
- **Why:** This question requires combining three given constants into something with the dimensions of length, demonstrated by 'A physical quantity of the dimensions of length that can be formed out of $c$, $G$ and $e^2/4\pi\varepsilon_0$'.
- **Note:** All four options are built from the same three ingredients, so the question is settled only by a full dimensional check; the three wrong ones are the three distinct misplacements - $c^2$ in the numerator, $G$ in the denominator, and the square root omitted - which is why each carries a route and none is a filler.

## Q174

Physics · Wave Optics

Two Polaroids $P_1$ and $P_2$ are placed with their axis perpendicular to each other. Unpolarised light $I_0$ is incident on $P_1$. A third polaroid $P_3$ is kept in between $P_1$ and $P_2$ such that its axis makes an angle $45^\circ$ with that of $P_1$. The intensity of transmitted light through $P_2$ is

- (1) $\frac{I_0}{4}$
- **(2)** $\frac{I_0}{8}$
- (3) $\frac{I_0}{16}$
- (4) $\frac{I_0}{2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Polaroids P1, P3, P2 on a light beam with their axes meeting P1's axis at 45° and 90°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q174_sol_1_cd1d207c_29102026.svg?v=cd1d207ceddd)

*Polaroids P1, P3, P2 on a light beam with their axes meeting P1's axis at 45° and 90°*

![drawn: Polaroids P1, P3, P2 on a light beam with their axes meeting P1's axis at 45° and 90°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q174_solution_1_1f21b618_29102026.svg?v=1f21b6188c05)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. $I_2 = \frac{I_0}{2}\cos^2 45^\circ$
2. $= \frac{I_0}{2} \times \frac{1}{2}$
3. $= \frac{I_0}{4}$
4. $I_3 = \frac{I_0}{4}\cos^2 45^\circ$
5. $I_3 = \frac{I_0}{8}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Unpolarised light through $P_1$: $I_1 = \frac{I_0}{2}$.
2. Through $P_3$ at $45^\circ$ to $P_1$ (Malus's law): $I_2 = I_1 \cos^2 45^\circ = \frac{I_0}{2} \times \frac{1}{2} = \frac{I_0}{4}$.
3. Angle between $P_3$ and $P_2$ is $90^\circ - 45^\circ = 45^\circ$.
4. Through $P_2$: $I_3 = I_2 \cos^2 45^\circ = \frac{I_0}{4} \times \frac{1}{2} = \frac{I_0}{8}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Polaroids · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q174_0d49b5e7_29102026.png?v=0d49b5e704d1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q174_1d1a6631_29102026.png?v=1d1a66319da8)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** An unpolarised beam loses half its intensity at the first polaroid, and every later polaroid multiplies the intensity by $\cos^2$ of the angle between its axis and the axis before it
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  The chain is stopped one sheet short: the factor $\tfrac{1}{2}$ at $P_1$ and one $\cos^2 45^\circ$ at $P_3$ give $I_0/4$, which is the intensity leaving $P_3$ and not the intensity leaving $P_2$
- **Why:** This question requires Malus's law chained across three sheets, demonstrated by the stem fixing $P_3$ "such that its axis makes an angle $45^\circ$ with that of $P_1$" while $P_2$ stays perpendicular to $P_1$.

## Q175

Physics · Oscillations

A particle executes linear simple harmonic motion with an amplitude of $3\,\mathrm{cm}$. When the particle is at $2\,\mathrm{cm}$ from the mean position, the magnitude of its velocity is equal to that of its acceleration. Then its time period in seconds is

- (1) $\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2\pi}$
- **(2)** $\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{5}}$
- (3) $\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}$
- (4) $\frac{\sqrt{5}}{\pi}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $v = \omega\sqrt{A^2 - x^2}$
2. $a = x\omega^2$
3. $v = a$
4. $\omega\sqrt{A^2 - x^2} = x\omega^2$
5. $\sqrt{(3)^2 - (2)^2} = 2\left(\frac{2\pi}{T}\right)$
6. $\sqrt{5} = \frac{4\pi}{T}$
7. $T = \frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{5}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In SHM the speed at displacement $x$ is $v = \omega\sqrt{A^2 - x^2}$.
2. With $A = 3$ and $x = 2$: $v = \omega\sqrt{9 - 4} = \sqrt{5}\,\omega$.
3. The acceleration magnitude is $a = \omega^2 x = 2\omega^2$.
4. Setting $|v| = |a|$: $\sqrt{5}\,\omega = 2\omega^2 \Rightarrow \omega = \frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}$.
5. $T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{5}/2} = \frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{5}}\,\mathrm{s}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Calculate Time Period of SHM · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q175_2790e351_29102026.png?v=2790e35111f4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q175_1f341526_29102026.png?v=1f341526557f)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In SHM the speed is $\omega\sqrt{A^2-x^2}$ and the acceleration magnitude is $\omega^2 x$, and the period follows from $T=2\pi/\omega$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The condition $|v|=|a|$ is solved correctly for $\omega=\sqrt5/2$ and then the link between $\omega$ and the period is written upside down, so $\omega$ lands on top of $\pi$: $\sqrt5/2\pi$ instead of $4\pi/\sqrt5$
- **Why:** This question requires equating the SHM speed and acceleration magnitudes at a stated displacement and converting $\omega$ to a period, demonstrated by the stem's "the magnitude of its velocity is equal to that of its acceleration. Then its time period in seconds is".
- **Note:** None of the three distractors is the exact output of one named slip in the physics: options 1 and 4 are exactly $\omega/\pi$ and $2\omega/\pi$, i.e. the $\omega$-to-$T$ relation written upside down, which is why the trap is placed on that family; option 3 keeps the right shape with a radicand the stem's numbers cannot produce.
