## Q176

Physics · Mechanical Properties of Fluids

A U tube with both ends open to the atmosphere, is partially filled with water. Oil, which is immiscible with water, is poured into one side until it stands at a distance of $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ above the water level on the other side. Meanwhile the water rises by $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ from its original level (see diagram). The density of the oil is

![printed figure: U-tube open at both ends: oil column AB in the left limb, water risen from D to E in the right limb](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_agent/q176_90fe9a5b_29102026.svg?v=90fe9a5b9a79)

*U-tube open at both ends: oil column AB in the left limb, water risen from D to E in the right limb*

![drawn: U-tube open at both ends: oil column AB in the left limb, water risen from D to E in the right limb](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/figures_drawn/q176_question_2b47f80c_29102026.svg?v=2b47f80c7b58)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $425\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$
- (2) $800\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$
- **(3)** $928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$
- (4) $650\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $h_{\mathrm{oil}}\,\rho_{\mathrm{oil}}\,g = h_{\mathrm{water}}\,\rho_{\mathrm{water}}\,g$
2. $140 \times \rho_{\mathrm{oil}} = 130 \times \rho_{\mathrm{water}}$
3. $\rho_{\mathrm{oil}} = \frac{13}{14} \times 1000\,\mathrm{kg/m^{3}}$
4. $\rho_{\mathrm{oil}} = 928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Water drops $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ on the oil side and rises $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ on the other, so the water column above level $BC$ is $h_w = 65 + 65 = 130\,\mathrm{mm}$.
2. The oil top stands $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ above the final water level, so the oil column is $h_o = 130 + 10 = 140\,\mathrm{mm}$.
3. Pressures at $B$ and $C$ (same level, same liquid below) are equal: $P_a + \rho_o g h_o = P_a + \rho_w g h_w$.
4. $\rho_o = \rho_w \frac{h_w}{h_o} = 1000 \times \frac{130}{140} \approx 928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Hydrostatics · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 176](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q176_9a997928_29102026.png?v=9a99792857b1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 176](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q176_d623c617_29102026.png?v=d623c61790ab)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In a U-tube the pressures at a common level must match, so the oil column balances the water column measured from the oil-water interface
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 2  
  $800\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ is the stock density of oil carried from floating-and-buoyancy problems, and it is offered here as if the question asked what oil weighs rather than what this balance gives
- **Why:** This question requires a hydrostatic balance between the oil and water columns read off the figure, demonstrated by the stem's "it stands at a distance of $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ above the water level on the other side" together with "the water rises by $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ from its original level".
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a checked drawn spec carrying the labels 10 mm, 65 mm twice, initial and final water levels, so the geometry was readable. Worth recording that the obvious geometric slip — taking the oil column as $65+10=75\,\mathrm{mm}$ against $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ of water, i.e. $867\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ — is not among the options, so the only pull on a printed option is the remembered stock value.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q177

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

Consider a drop of rain water having mass $1\,\mathrm{g}$ falling from a height of $1\,\mathrm{km}$. It hits the ground with a speed of $50\,\mathrm{m/s}$. Take $g$ constant with a value $10\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$. The work done by the (i) gravitational force and the (ii) resistive force of air is

- (1) (i) $1.25\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $-8.25\,\mathrm{J}$
- (2) (i) $100\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $8.75\,\mathrm{J}$
- **(3)** (i) $10\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $-8.75\,\mathrm{J}$
- (4) (i) $-10\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $-8.25\,\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $w_g + w_a = K_f - K_i$
2. $mgh + w_a = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} - 0$
3. $10^{-3} \times 10 \times 10^{3} + w_a = \frac{1}{2} \times 10^{-3} \times (50)^{2}$
4. $w_a = -8.75\,\mathrm{J}$ i.e. work done due to air resistance and work done due to gravity $= 10\,\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Work done by gravity: $W_g = mgh = (10^{-3}\,\mathrm{kg})(10\,\mathrm{m/s^2})(10^{3}\,\mathrm{m}) = 10\,\mathrm{J}$.
2. Final kinetic energy (starting from rest): $K_f = \frac{1}{2}mv^2 = \frac{1}{2}(10^{-3})(50)^2 = 1.25\,\mathrm{J}$.
3. Work-energy theorem: $W_g + W_r = K_f - 0$.
4. $W_r = 1.25\,\mathrm{J} - 10\,\mathrm{J} = -8.75\,\mathrm{J}$, so (i) $10\,\mathrm{J}$ and (ii) $-8.75\,\mathrm{J}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Work–Energy Theorem, Work Done by Gravity or Spring Force · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q177_ee80175f_29102026.png?v=ee80175f64fa)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q177_5fef09ab_29102026.png?v=5fef09ab91fc)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Work done by gravity on a falling body is $+mgh$, and the work-energy theorem makes the air's work the difference between the kinetic energy gained and that gravity supplied
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  The magnitude $mgh=0.001\times10\times1000=10\,\mathrm{J}$ is right and the sign is flipped, because "work and gravity" is remembered with a minus sign from lifting problems
- **Why:** This question requires both the gravitational work and the air's work from the work-energy theorem, demonstrated by the stem asking for "the work done by the (i) gravitational force and the (ii) resistive force of air" and by every option printing two signed entries.
- **Note:** E1 recorded plain_mcq, but each option prints two entries, (i) and (ii), that are accepted or rejected together, so the printed shape is two_part and that is what is tagged.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q178

Physics · Kinematics

The $x$ and $y$ coordinates of the particle at any time are $x = 5t - 2t^2$ and $y = 10t$ respectively, where $x$ and $y$ are in meters and $t$ in seconds. The acceleration of the particle at $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$ is

- (1) $5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- **(2)** $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- (3) $-8\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- (4) $0$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $x = 5t - 2t^{2} \qquad y = 10t$
2. $\frac{dx}{dt} = 5 - 4t \qquad \frac{dy}{dt} = 10$
3. $v_{x} = 5 - 4t \qquad v_{y} = 10$
4. $\frac{dv}{dt}x = -4 \qquad \frac{dv}{dt}y = 10$
5. $a_{x} = -4 \qquad a_{y} = 0$
6. Acceleration of particle at $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$ is $= -4\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $v_x = \frac{dx}{dt} = 5 - 4t$.
2. $a_x = \frac{dv_x}{dt} = -4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$.
3. $v_y = \frac{dy}{dt} = 10\,\mathrm{m/s}$.
4. $a_y = \frac{dv_y}{dt} = 0$.
5. $\vec{a} = a_x\hat{i} + a_y\hat{j} = -4\hat{i}\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, constant, so at $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$ it is $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Instantaneous Acceleration · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 178](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q178_f69cf8ef_29102026.png?v=f69cf8efde3d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 178](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q178_a51f5c56_29102026.png?v=a51f5c5692c8)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Acceleration is the second time derivative of position, so a position quadratic in $t$ has a constant acceleration that does not depend on the instant named
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  The $t$-carrying term of the velocity, $-4t$, is used as the acceleration and $t=2$ is substituted into it, giving $-8\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$ instead of the constant $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- **Why:** This question requires differentiating the coordinates twice, demonstrated by "$x = 5t - 2t^2$ and $y = 10t$" with the acceleration asked for at a named instant that the second derivative does not contain.
- **Note:** E1 recorded multi_part_respectively, but "respectively" in the stem labels the two coordinate expressions, not the answer: the options are single values, so the printed shape is a plain mcq. The key prints the signed $x$-component, $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$; tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q179

Physics · Electromagnetic Induction

A long solenoid of diameter $0.1\,\mathrm{m}$ has $2 \times 10^{4}$ turns per meter. At the centre of the solenoid, a coil of $100$ turns and radius $0.01\,\mathrm{m}$ is placed with its axis coinciding with the solenoid axis. The current in the solenoid reduces at a constant rate to $0\,\mathrm{A}$ from $4\,\mathrm{A}$ in $0.05\,\mathrm{s}$. If the resistance of the coil is $10\pi^{2}\,\Omega$, the total charge flowing through the coil during this time is

- (1) $16\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- **(2)** $32\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- (3) $16\pi\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- (4) $32\pi\,\mu\mathrm{C}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\varepsilon = -N\frac{d\phi}{dt}$
2. $\left|\frac{\varepsilon}{R}\right| = \frac{N}{R}\frac{d\phi}{dt}$
3. $dq = \frac{N}{R}d\phi$
4. $\Delta Q = \frac{N(\Delta\phi)}{R}$
5. $\Delta Q = \frac{\Delta\phi_{\mathrm{total}}}{R}$
6. $= \frac{(NBA)}{R}$
7. $= \frac{\mu_{0} n i \pi r^{2}}{R}$
8. Putting values
9. $= \frac{4\pi \times 10^{-7} \times 100 \times 4 \times \pi \times (0.01)^{2}}{10\pi^{2}}$
10. $\Delta Q = 32\,\mu\mathrm{C}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Inside the long solenoid the field is $B = \mu_0 n I$, uniform over the small coil.
2. The coil area is $A = \pi r^2 = \pi (0.01)^2 = \pi \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$.
3. Total flux linkage change: $\Delta(N\Phi) = N \mu_0 n \Delta I A = 100 \times 4\pi \times 10^{-7} \times 2 \times 10^{4} \times 4 \times \pi \times 10^{-4} = 3.2\pi^2 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{Wb}$.
4. Charge flowed: $Q = \frac{\Delta(N\Phi)}{R} = \frac{3.2\pi^2 \times 10^{-4}}{10\pi^2} = 3.2 \times 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{C}$.
5. $Q = 32\,\mu\mathrm{C}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Faraday's Law of Electromagnetic Induction, Mutual Induction · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 179](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q179_2592a731_29102026.png?v=2592a7311034)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 179](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q179_521729b9_29102026.png?v=521729b9a0e4)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The charge that flows through a closed coil is $q = N\,\Delta\Phi/R$, which depends on the total flux change and not on how long the change takes
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 4  
  The flux carries $\pi$ twice — once from $\mu_0 = 4\pi\times10^{-7}$ and once from $A=\pi r^2$ — and only one of them is cancelled against the $10\pi^2\,\Omega$ resistance, leaving a stray $\pi$ and $32\pi\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- **Why:** This question requires the charge from $q=N\Delta\Phi/R$ with the solenoid field supplying the flux, demonstrated by the stem giving "the resistance of the coil is $10\pi^{2}\,\Omega$" and asking for "the total charge flowing through the coil during this time".
- **Note:** The solenoid diameter $0.1\,\mathrm{m}$ and the interval $0.05\,\mathrm{s}$ are both unused in the answer; the coil's own radius sets the area and the time cancels out of $q$.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q180

Physics · Thermodynamics

A Carnot engine having an efficiency of $\frac{1}{10}$ as heat engine, is used as a refrigerator. If the work done on the system is $10\,\mathrm{J}$, the amount of energy absorbed from the reservoir at lower temperature is

- **(1)** $90\,\mathrm{J}$
- (2) $99\,\mathrm{J}$
- (3) $100\,\mathrm{J}$
- (4) $1\,\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\beta = \frac{1-\eta}{\eta}$
2. $= \frac{1-\frac{1}{10}}{\frac{1}{10}} = \frac{\frac{9}{10}}{\frac{1}{10}}$
3. $\beta = 9$
4. $\beta = \frac{Q_2}{W}$
5. $Q_2 = 9 \times 10 = 90\,\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Carnot efficiency: $\eta = 1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1} = \frac{1}{10}$, so $\frac{T_2}{T_1} = \frac{9}{10}$.
2. Coefficient of performance of the same cycle run as a refrigerator: $\beta = \frac{T_2}{T_1 - T_2} = \frac{1-\eta}{\eta}$.
3. $\beta = \frac{9/10}{1/10} = 9$.
4. Heat absorbed from the cold reservoir: $Q_2 = \beta W = 9 \times 10\,\mathrm{J} = 90\,\mathrm{J}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Refrigerator and Heat Pump, Carnot Engine · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 180](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/crops/q180_bbbc4dc5_29102026.png?v=bbbc4dc565b2)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 180](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_B/solutions/q180_4b9ff973_29102026.png?v=4b9ff973d9eb)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A Carnot engine run backwards as a refrigerator: the efficiency fixes the hot-reservoir heat from the work, $Q_1 = W/\eta$, and the cold reservoir gives up what is left once the work is taken out, $Q_2 = Q_1 - W$
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 3  
  $Q_1 = W/\eta = 10/(1/10) = 100\,\mathrm{J}$ is the one number the two given figures yield in a single step, so a student who gets there reports it as the energy absorbed; $100\,\mathrm{J}$ is true of this machine but it is the heat at the HOT reservoir, not the lower-temperature one the stem names
- **Why:** This question requires computing a number from the stem's own figures, demonstrated by "an efficiency of $\frac{1}{10}$" and "the work done on the system is $10\,\mathrm{J}$" having to be combined before "the amount of energy absorbed from the reservoir at lower temperature" can be named; no quantity is asked to be compared against another, so this is `calculate` and not `relation`.
<!--/ecat-->
